Ford simplifies using your EV as a home battery with the $30K Fathom
2026-10-27

Ford simplifies using your EV as a home battery with the $30K Fathom

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
This morning, Ford revealed that buyers of the forthcoming Fathom—the company’s sub-$30,000 electric pickup truck due next year—will get more than just a truck. Until now, vehicle-to-home technology—like the setup Ford developed for the now-discontinued F-150 Lightning—takes DC power from the electric vehicle and runs it through an inverter built into the wallbox to turn it into AC the house can use. But with the Fathom, that inverter is built into the truck from the start. So every Fathom will be able to act as a backup battery for your home. It’s typical: You wait a decade for a cheap electric truck, and then two come along almost at once. And they’re two radically different products from two radically different automakers that I think will reveal a few preferences from the EV-buying public. One is the Blank Slate, from a brand new startup offering a bare-bones two-door that capitalizes on features the Internet says it wants, like no onboard modem, no touchscreens, and no infotainment system. The other is the result of one of Detroit’s big three taking a long, hard look at Chinese EVs and asking itself, “How do we make a car that cheap?” Although the Ford Fathom will cost $5,000 more than a Slate, that money does buy an extra pair of doors, modern conveniences like an infotainment system, CarPlay, electric windows, and a vast dealership network to service the car. That may be helpful given how many recalls Ford issues each year. The new take on bidirectional EV charging came about when Ford acquired Auto Motive Power three years ago. The result is the Fathom’s charging module, one of five powerful computers that each handle a different aspect of the Fathom’s functions. (Known as a software-defined vehicle, this handful of domain controllers replace the dozens and dozens of discrete ECUs you’d find in an older design.) Now, the Fathom won’t need a wallbox with an in-built inverter, like the 80 A unit needed for the Lightning. But you will still need the Ford Home Power Station, which Ford says should cost about half as much as the $1,310 device needed for the older, bigger truck. The management software knows to charge your car when electricity is cheap and uses it to power the house when rates are expensive. To use the Fathom as an emergency battery during outages, customers will also need Ford’s newer Home Backup Panel. The automaker says this should cost 60 percent less than the first-generation panel, which cost around $5,000. That means adding V2H to your house still isn’t free, but it should be a lot cheaper than before. It’s also the first sub-$30k bidirectional EV with AC power. Ford is also working with a number of utilities across the US and Canada to hash out the details needed to begin vehicle-to-grid pilot programs with the Fathom. And it has made sure the system meets existing standards, including the UL 1741 standard for chargers, the IEEE 1547 standard for connecting vehicles to the grid, and SAE J3068, a communications standard.

DeepMind unveils Genie 4 world model promising more realistic physics and humans
2026-10-27

DeepMind unveils Genie 4 world model promising more realistic physics and humans

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Last year, Google DeepMind sent gaming stocks tumbling with the reveal of its Genie 3 world model. Since then, world models have become a major focus of AI labs around the world, but Google isn’t resting on its laurels. Researchers have been hard at work on an updated model that improves on Genie 3 in almost every way. And if you pay Google enough money, you might actually get to use Genie 4 before too long. It’s easy to look at the output of Genie 4 and see a video game, which is why Genie 3 caused so much consternation in the gaming industry. DeepMind doesn’t think about it that way, though. According to Google, world models are a key element in the march toward artificial general intelligence (AGI). A world model creates a virtual world, but it doesn’t require pre-rendering environments or programming interactive elements. You simply tell the model what you want, and it generates the experience frame by frame in real time as you explore it. DeepMind says this technology could create infinite training environments for physical robots or unlock new avenues of rapid prototyping for creative fields. Of course, that requires the model to accurately simulate what you want. Genie 4 has several improvements that make its simulations more useful, and that’s thanks in part to Google’s updated Gemini models. When you prompt a world into existence in Genie 4, it leverages Gemini 3.8 Flash as a guiding agent to break down goals and evaluate each output frame. This helps keep the simulation on track, letting you move through varying environments without losing the original context. However, DeepMind notes that Genie 4 worlds still only remain consistent for a couple of minutes before they forget how things started. Like Genie 3, the new version supports changing the parameters midstream. You can change the weather, add elements, or move the camera, and Gemini will help steer the simulation with each update. This is similar to the way reasoning models generate additional internal prompts to zero in on the right output tokens. The physics in AI-generated video can sometimes look a bit unnatural, but DeepMind says Genie 4 has reached a new level of realism. Genie 4 can apparently do a better job with elements like aerodynamics and collisions, which makes the simulations more useful and visually appealing. People appearing in these simulations should also look less uncanny, with more natural movement and responses to the changing environment. Genie 4 is also the first DeepMind world model with native audio. Videos will have ambient background sounds, and you can specify audio elements in your prompt. The biggest leap, if it works, is the addition of human speech. DeepMind promises that Genie 4 will be able to create interactive dialog that adapts as you change the simulation. Realistic speech in standard AI video was a major shift in realism, and now a similar revolution may be coming to Google’s world models. DeepMind is careful to point out that world models are still an active area of research—Genie 3 never expanded beyond the very limited Project Genie web experience. However, the team has been working with a small group of trusted testers to build toward a wider release for Genie 4. Access will start expanding soon, but there’s no specific timeline yet. It won’t be cheap, though. Google plans to roll out Genie 4 for developers via the Gemini API and AI Studio, but pricing isn’t yet available. Consumers will be able to get access, but it will roll out in phases with Google’s most expensive AI Ultra plans, which start at $100 per month. Google may expand access to AI Pro and AI Plus plans eventually. This will still be handled through Project Genie, which will limit how much you can do with Genie. There’s a waitlist for users who are interested in getting early access.

From cheap tool to premium subscription: When a beloved app's price skyrockets
2026-10-27

From cheap tool to premium subscription: When a beloved app's price skyrockets

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
How much are you willing to pay to keep using your favorite app? Maybe you’ve used it for years. Perhaps it performs a function so essential and specific that you couldn’t imagine what you’d do without it. But when the developer announces an extraordinary price hike, you’re forced to consider how much you really value that app. Earlier this year, Evernote, a note-taking app, changed its subscription tiers, forcing some users to go from paying $130 per year to $250. The highest tier now costs more than 3.3 times as much as the most advanced tier did in 2023 and 10 times more than what Evernote charged for annual subscriptions from its 2008 launch until 2016. That’s just one example of many longstanding consumer pieces of software that have seen dramatic price hikes in recent years. A lifetime pass to Plex, for example, was $75 in 2012. Today, it’s $250. Individual plans for Quicken, Robokiller, and Inkdrop have each doubled in price. It’s easy to assume that the developers are miscalculating the value of their app or the loyalty of their users. But an unconventionally honest assessment regarding price hikes from Evernote owner Bending Spoons illustrates how owners of legacy software can apply pressure to loyal customers to extract additional revenue. When Bending Spoons bought Evernote in 2023, the app had a sizable customer base. In a June SEC filing, Bending Spoons explained that it was attracted to Evernote’s 200 million accounts and high user engagement. “We… valued its robust subscriber retention, driven by a rich feature set and a highly engaged and tenured customer base,” the filing says. “Many customers had thousands of notes stored in Evernote and had developed personalized workflows on and around the product. At the time of acquisition, the average customer tenure was 7.2 years.” The price hikes weren’t evidence of Bending Spoon undervaluing Evernote’s users but rather of the company seeing an opportunity to turn that loyalty into more revenue. According to Bending Spoons, “year-over-year growth in new user registrations had been negative for several years” at -23 percent in 2021, -23 percent in 2022, and -20 percent in 2023. After the 2023 acquisition, “new user registrations increased by 29 percent in 2024 and 20 percent in 2025,” the filing says. This suggests that slower-than-desired growth, which is often associated with streaming price hikes, wasn’t to blame for Evernote’s higher prices. Although price hikes risk eroding customer loyalty, Bending Spoons investigated how much change in pricing and subscription tiers Evernote users would tolerate. From 2023 through 2025, we leveraged our proprietary technologies to launch more than 200 experiments across a range of areas, including evaluating the impact of new features on retention, testing alternative price points, and iterating on the user experience at onboarding. Shortly after the acquisition, Evernote prices increased (from $8 per month to $15/month, or from $70/year to $130/year). The new prices aimed to better align “with how different customer segments… engage with” Evernote, and, in combination with product improvements, led to “average revenue per monthly active user being 2.5 times as high in 2025 as in 2022,” Bending Spoons said. Bending Spoons also made some previously paywalled Evernote features available to non-paying users and added usage limits to certain features. After this change, “subscription conversions triggered by reaching a usage limit increased in 2024 to approximately twice their level in 2022,” and “in 2025, such usage limits accounted for 51 percent of all conversions,” according to Bending Spoons. Additionally, Evernote released over 150 product updates in 2025, the filing says. Evernote v11, a huge update, came out in 2026. While issuing layoffs in July 2023, Bending Spoons said Evernote had been “unprofitable for years.” By November 2024, the app had reportedly reached record levels of profitability. This year’s Evernote price bump followed years of experimenting with pricing structures and adding new features. The price hikes were not the result of Evernote undervaluing longtime customers. Instead, Evernote’s owner saw that loyal customer base as an opportunity to generate even more revenue through carefully calibrated changes to pricing, feature access, and capabilities. Comments from Inkdrop’s developer also help explain steep price hikes imposed on longtime app users. In 2024, the Markdown note-taking app’s price doubled from $5/month to $10/month or $50/year to $100/year. At the time, Inkdrop’s creator, Takuya Matsuyama, wrote a blog post explaining the app’s first price change in seven years: Maintaining a smaller scale allows me to keep focusing on what really matters. I’d prefer to have a few thousand customers who really like my app rather than >10k broader customers. Instead of spending my energy and money to pursue more and more users, I’d like to make the existing happy customers even happier by focusing on a specific niche, keeping things simple and clean. While Evernote sought to combat negative new registrations after its acquisition, Inkdrop’s developer was less concerned about growing its customer base and more interested in extracting additional revenue from current users. Both apps have customer bases filled with users who have subscribed for over five years, which makes finding new customers challenging. Mature apps with longtime subscribers, initially drawn in through low prices and reliable features, face a predicament. Those loyal users are good for business, but they’re often content with software that functions as it always has—and don’t expect to pay more for it. But stagnant growth doesn’t mix well with inflation or stockholder expectations. It can also leave companies without enough revenue to develop new features and keep software up to date. Sometimes, the prices apps launch with aren’t sustainable in the long term, as Plex has said about its lifetime pass, which has seen its price increase by 525 percent over two price hikes since March 2024. We’ve seen a similar pattern with streaming apps. New features can help soften the blow of massive price hikes. But it’s still hard for old customers to accept prices that double, or go even higher, especially if they were happy with the app as it was. Developers have options for covering rising costs and funding new features beyond price hikes. For example, they could reward longtime customers with grandfathered pricing, exclusive features, or other perks to help offset higher costs. And when price hikes are unavoidable, giving customers advance warning can go a long way toward showing loyal users they’re valued. Another option is to offer more subscription tiers in general. Developers often cite new features to justify higher subscription prices, but not all users want those features. A wider range of subscription options, including a cheaper, AI-free tier, could let developers expand an app’s features and audience without penalizing users who are happy with its original functionality. Alternatively, developers could offer advanced features to power users as individual or bundled add-ons. Sometimes, various internal and external factors make higher prices inevitable. When that happens, users would be wise to reexamine whether that beloved app is still the best option after all these years.

Apple iPhone Duo review: Two screens, two experiences, two thousand dollars
2026-10-27

Apple iPhone Duo review: Two screens, two experiences, two thousand dollars

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
I understand the appeal of foldable phones—they let manufacturers keep expanding screen sizes while still producing devices that can reasonably fit in a pocket. In practice, though, every foldable Android phone I’ve seen in public has had a ridiculously visible seam between the two halves of the screen, often with what looks like delamination from the underlying surface, giving the display an ugly, bubbly appearance. Back when Android phones began shipping with bigger and bigger screens and the iPhone was stuck at a miserly four inches, I was jealous. But I’ve never looked at anyone using a chunky, fragile-looking foldable phone in public and pined for Apple’s take on the idea. Nevertheless, that take has arrived, in the form of the iPhone Duo. This $1,999-and-up foldable is the biggest change to the iPhone in years, with both hardware and software updates that dramatically change how you interact with the device. Like most Apple products, it looks and feels solid, and Apple has managed to engineer its way around at least some of the problems that have bedeviled other foldables (in particular, it has wrangled the between-screen crease so close to invisibility that I rarely thought about it while using the phone unfolded). But like most first-generation Apple products, it has some odd rough edges and costs more than most people will want to spend. Some aspects of its design feel like steps backward from the standard iPhone design, as exemplified by the current iPhone 17 and iPhone 18 Pro. The result is something that is technically impressive but not always enjoyable to use. As a little iPad, it’s surprisingly good. As an iPhone, though, I struggle to think of a single way I prefer it to a regular old iPhone. The iPhone Duo is the first iPhone I’ve used in a long time that comes with a learning curve. It’s the first time since 2018’s iPhone X that a hardware update has fundamentally changed how you interact with the software. For the first day or two, I found this pretty off-putting. I kept losing track of the iPad-style button-mounted Touch ID sensor and flipping the phone around with the camera upside down. When fully opened, the phone acts like an iPad: It’s orientation-agnostic, and it will work in portrait or landscape, regardless of which end is up. But in its folded one-handed mode, the Duo still has a right-side-up and an upside-down just like any iPhone, and it took a couple of days of retraining my muscle memory before I was consistently pulling it out of my pocket and looking at it the right way without thinking about it. When folded up, the iPhone Duo has a more compact footprint than Apple’s other phones and a screen that’s both shorter and wider. The titanium frame looks and feels a lot like last year’s iPhone Air, which plenty of people (us included) pegged as a half-step toward a foldable design. The outer screen looks and feels like a typical glassy iPhone screen, and the “back” of the phone where the camera is (the only quadrant of the phone that isn’t a screen) uses familiar-looking and -feeling Ceramic Shield glass. Despite having a familiar-looking camera cutout and Dynamic Island on the front screen, the Duo totally lacks a Face ID scanner, which is a pretty big behavioral change following years of face-scanning phones. The Touch ID button has been migrated to the power button, iPad-style, which is in the upper-right corner of the phone when it’s folded (when unfolded, the positioning depends on which direction is “up” because the phone flips its screen orientation no matter which way you’re holding it). In general, the Duo’s button positioning is strange. The power button’s position is… fine. It’s the only placement that makes Touch ID authentication even remotely possible with one hand, even though I felt like I was about to drop the phone every time I tried. But the volume buttons are on the top of the phone in this mode. The left button is volume down and the right button is volume up in this orientation, like on a landscape iPad, even though the volume indicator itself is a tiny bar that lives in the Dynamic Island and travels up and down vertically. The Duo’s other physical button is the Camera Control button. I would vastly prefer it be a customizable Action Button, which I use constantly on my iPhone 17 Pro. For a phone with this many acres of edge, it feels like the Action Button should have room to exist somewhere, but it doesn’t. That shorter, wider screen leads to the biggest and most noticeable of the iPhone Duo’s software design changes, at least in folded-phone mode: Your dock moves to the right edge of the screen, and that’s where most app controls move, too. Controls for apps that normally appear on the top or the bottom of the screen are now generally relocated to the right side of the screen, which is still something I’m still getting used to, even after nearly a week of daily use. These side-mounted controls feel like they’re swimming upstream against almost two decades of smartphone app design—and they don’t even really solve the “lack of vertical space” problem because as soon as the iOS keyboard pops up, the viewable area still shrinks to just a few lines of text. This right-hand navigation area also feels weirdly underutilized, given the percentage of the front screen it takes up. Your dock is there, but Apple still limits it to four icons, leaving a ton of unused space above and below it. The nav area feels too wide, with too much negative space. In the end, I only really liked the Duo in folded-down phone mode when it was sitting on a desk, couch, or table next to my laptop, serving as a separate compact notification screen—in other words, when I was not actively holding or touching it. And this isn’t something no other phone can do; it’s just a regular phone thing that the Duo doesn’t make worse. While folded, the Duo is fundamentally designed for right-handed use. Controls throughout iOS and Duo-optimized apps have been scooted to the screen’s right edge to make up for the loss of vertical screen area. The screen is wide enough that any attempt by lefties to reach across with their thumbs to tab that part of the screen will be difficult to impossible, and I say this as someone with fairly large hands. The Touch ID sensor—integrated, iPad-style, into the phone’s long, flat power button—is also mounted on the right side. Even if iOS did flip around the other way when you hold the Duo with the rounded edge on the left (and it doesn’t, to be clear), the camera would then be on the bottom of the phone, blocked by your fingers. But even as a right-hander, I don’t find the Duo to be especially comfortable to use one-handed. Sure, I can reach everything, but the phone is so heavy and thick that the standard “rest the phone on my pinkie and use it with my thumb” move is fatiguing. A left-handed friend who held and tested the Duo found it extremely uncomfortable, particularly the stretch you need to do to hit any app icon in the side-mounted dock. As frustrating as the Duo is when it’s folded, I genuinely enjoy it when it’s unfolded. Think of an iPad Nano, and you’ll be pretty close to what the experience is. To answer the biggest question first: Yes, you can see and feel the seam between the screens if you’re looking for it. But most of the time, if you’re just holding it and watching video or reading a webpage and using a thumb to scroll in the corner of the screen, you just don’t notice it. Apple has gotten very close to making it invisible. Frankly, I’m much more bothered by the under-screen camera, which is visible pretty much constantly. The seam is visible from some angles if you go looking for them. The under-screen camera pinhole is only invisible from some angles if you go looking for it. It’s out of the way enough not to be experience-ruining, but I did find it distracting, and I don’t know that I find it to be a net improvement over the actual-pinhole-with-Dynamic-Island solution that Apple has used for the last few iPhone generations. When unfolded, the Duo’s screen expands from a squat 5.4 inches to a more comfortable 7.6 inches, which isn’t so far off from the new iPad mini’s 8.5-inch screen. The dock is still limited to four app icons, like a regular iPhone, but it moves back to a familiar place at the bottom of the screen, and apps have more room to breathe. In landscape, apps usually retain the same side-mounted controls you see on the phone’s front screen, but when you pivot to portrait mode, they revert to more familiar positioning at the top and bottom of the screen—portrait mode looks pretty much like an iPad, while landscape mode looks more like a pair of iPhone home screens next to each other (more on that in a minute). The inner screen has a matte nano-texture finish, which shares a name with the technology used in some Macs and iPads. But Apple says that both the materials used and the actual nano-texture pattern are both different, changes that were necessitated by the fact that this is a foldable phone and not an inert glass slab. A slightly raised rim around the edge of the screen keeps it from resting directly on a table or desk and leaves room for tiny bits of dust or crud to get trapped without grinding into the screen. If the inner screen does get damaged, this cover layer can apparently be removed and replaced without too much trouble. I find the screen mostly pleasant to look at and interact with, and I really appreciate the lack of glare. My only gripes are that it picks up smudges that are slightly harder to wipe off than they are on regular glass and that contrast (particularly for dark/black elements) is visibly worse than on the front glassy screen. The inner screen does use OLED display panels, the same as the front screen, but this specific benefit of OLED is missing from the inner screen. While an unfolded Duo is decidedly iPad-like, it’s still running iOS, not iPadOS, and Apple has gone back to the drawing board for yet another take on split-screen iDevice multitasking. What the company landed on is most similar to the iPad’s old Split View interface, but with some limitations and benefits that help it feel somewhat more at home on the Duo. To enter split-screen multitasking, open an app and swipe up from the bottom of the screen, then drag the window to the areas that appear on the left and right of the screen. Once you’ve anchored an app in this way, the unused half of the screen simply becomes a standard iPhone home screen, with all your icons, widgets, and the side-mounted dock (which can appear on the left side of the screen when the phone is unfolded). Launch another app from that home screen, and it will take up the other half of the screen. Both apps can be used and dismissed independently, as though you were using two separate iPhones next to each other; you can even launch the same app twice if you really want to. The center of the screen, where the fold is, is mostly treated as a dead zone by default, but drag it one way or the other to resize one of the apps to full screen, and tap the divider to lock two apps together so they always appear and are dismissed in tandem. If you close the phone’s screen, what you were doing on the right half of the inner screen is preserved on the outer screen, giving the transition a seamless feel. The hardware’s design makes sense when you look at multitasking mode. Half of an unfolded iPhone Duo is exactly the same as using the iPhone Duo in its folded-down phone mode. The side-nav is pinned to the left side of the screen instead of the right side, but it otherwise feels very similar to having a pair of iPhones you can use in tandem (I would say “two iPhones for the price of one,” but that doesn’t work when the base Duo model is even more expensive than buying two regular iPhones). Third-party apps need to be updated in one way or another to support the Duo, with baseline support granted at least partly for “free” to apps that target the Xcode 27.1 SDK. Apps that haven’t been updated look mostly OK on the outer screen, since its strip of usable space is roughly the same width as a regular iPhone. Apps that haven’t been updated look like iPhone apps running on an iPad when the screen is unfolded, though: little phone-sized windows with black borders all around, with an option to expand them so they take up the entire screen. I’ve been surprised how many apps have added Duo support in the few days since the device came out, given that Apple didn’t even make these tools available to developers until just a few weeks ago. But early adopters will run into tons of apps that don’t know what to make of the Duo’s larger inner screen, and I suspect it will be a while before you can assume that any given app will work properly on it. The Duo can be a phone. The Duo can be a tablet. But it also supports several liminal states that exist somewhere in between the two (nomenclature mine): Not all apps that support the Duo do different things in every one of its modes, but apps can treat them all as their own unique views if they want to. (I asked a developer from the New York Times Cooking team at an Apple-hosted developer showcase how many distinct views they needed to support to cover every possible position that an app can appear in on the Duo. “Seven. It’s seven,” he replied without hesitation.) But we’ve already seen a few examples of developers doing things that wouldn’t be at home on a regular iPhone or an iPad. The Headroom app uses a remote desktop connection to connect to a remote Mac and simulate a tiny MacBook; the Delta emulator app has been updated with a Nintendo DS mode; and the Tiny Computers app will change it into arguably copyright-safe facsimiles of distinctive classic computers. I can’t vouch for the quality of any of these apps, and Headroom and Tiny Computers in particular have a distinct air of “quickly vibe-coded to catch the wave of early adopter interest” to them. But the ideas are interesting, and they wouldn’t work as well on different hardware. Along with the review unit, Apple included a $79 iPhone Duo Case and a $129 iPhone Duo Folio with Kickstand. The case provides a basic level of protection without really changing anything about how the Duo works (though the fact that it sticks to the phone using adhesive raises concerns about its longevity). The Folio is more interesting. It attaches with magnets, and it has a retractable strip between its two halves that stretches to cover the hinge when the phone is folded and retreats into the case when it’s opened. The kickstand that props the phone up in tablet mode is genuinely useful; in phone mode, it solves one problem while creating another. Here’s the problem it solves: By default, if you’re using the Duo in tablet mode and you fold it closed, the phone transitions your current app to the outer screen rather than putting the phone to sleep. Flipping the Folio cover open and closed does put the phone to sleep and wake it up. It makes the question of “is my phone awake or isn’t it” easier to answer. The problem it creates is that this cover is opaque, and when you fold it around to the back of the phone, it covers both the camera and the flashlight. These are two things I regularly need fast access to when I pull my phone out of my pocket, and the Folio case asks you to leave the cover flopping around, remove the case temporarily, or snap the phone open into tablet mode instead of using it in phone mode. The chip and the camera in the iPhone Duo are way less interesting than the brand-new hardware and software changes, so apologies if this section feels like an afterthought. But it kind of is. The Apple A20 Pro is Apple’s first 2 nm phone chip, and its second 2 nm chip overall after the M6 that debuted in the new Mac mini. It still uses two large CPU cores and four efficiency cores, the same as the last few generations of A-series chips, but it’s the first phone chip to include “super” cores instead of performance cores. Unlike the M6, which ships all three kinds of cores, the A20 Pro just uses super and efficiency cores. The A20 Pro does ship with 7 GPU cores, up from the 5-6 cores in the A19 Pro, plus Apple’s upgraded 32-core Neural Engine (Apple usually refers to this as a “dual Neural Engine” with 16 cores each). The A20 Pro still includes 12GB of RAM, the same as the A19 Pro (both upgraded from the 8GB of RAM in the A18 Pro—either chip would be a solid upgrade option for a future MacBook Neo). In the benchmarks I ran, Apple’s performance promises for the A20 Pro end up in the ballpark of the figures Apple has published: around 20 to 30 percent better single- and multi-core CPU performance compared to A19 Pro, and between 40 and 50 percent faster GPU performance (the 73 percent performance improvement for the 3DMark Wild Life Extreme test is an outlier). If you own any iPhone made within the last three or four years, the chip isn’t the reason to buy the phone, but it’s a respectable upgrade. The Duo’s camera system is fine. Like the iPhone Air, this hardware is priced well into iPhone Pro territory while lacking the camera system that makes the Pro my default iPhone choice. The Duo uses the same basic camera configuration as the basic iPhone. It has one 48 MP main sensor and one 48 MP ultrawide sensor, both of which enable a 2x optical zoom feature by cropping a 12 MP patch out of the middle. (Apple’s tech specs page also claims that the ultrawide lens can zoom out by 2x, giving it a “4x optical-quality zoom range,” which feels hand-wavy even if you accept “optical-quality zoom” as an accurate description of what Apple’s cameras are doing). What keeps me away from the standard iPhone these days is that I find the ultrawide lens to be by far the least useful one Apple ships and the telephoto lens to be the most useful. The Duo’s camera asks you to make the same kind of downgrade from a Pro iPhone, though it at least improves on the one-camera setups Apple ships in the iPhone 17e and iPhone Air. Once you get past the lack of a telephoto lens, the Duo’s camera system is perfectly competent. Apple’s year-to-year camera quality improvements generally happen around the margins—a little less noise here, a little better low-light performance there—and that’s what I observed while comparing it to my iPhone 17 Pro. To my eye, the differences between the 17 Pro and Duo in good lighting were minimal, but the Duo did capture marginally better detail in low light (see the cat pictures). Of course, when you try comparing the Duo’s digitally zoomed 4x and 8x photos to the 17 Pro’s telephoto lens, there’s zero contest. The front cameras, like Face ID, are another casualty of the Duo’s design changes. There are technically two front-facing cameras: a typical-ish pinhole cutout on the front of the outer screen and the one hidden underneath the inner screen (as we discussed before, “hidden” is generous; the camera is almost always visible underneath whatever you’re looking at). The outer camera is lower-resolution than most other iPhones (12 MP, down from 18 MP), but it’s in the ballpark when you’re on a FaceTime call or taking a selfie. The under-screen inner camera is noticeably worse, capturing noisier video and photos that look excessively digitally sharpened to try to cover for its reduced quality and lack of detail. Apple doesn’t list a resolution for the inner camera, but its 2432 x 1824 resolution works out to around 4.4 megapixels, just one-third the resolution of the outer camera. That the inner camera is so much worse than the outer camera is made even more obvious by the fact that the inner screen is twice as large; when you fire up a FaceTime call, you’re looking at a lower-resolution image on a larger screen. This isn’t a dealbreaker; the bi-directional FaceTime call that shows the person on the other end of the line on both the outer and inner screens remains genuinely cool, even with the potato-esque quality of the inner camera. But it’s yet another place where the core/traditional iPhone experience is degraded to make room for the Duo’s hero features. In some ways, foldable phones remind me of the PC industry’s push toward convertible tablet PCs in the early 2010s. In the wake of Apple’s original iPad, Microsoft and its partners clearly thought that they had to figure out tablets or get left behind, but even the designs with staying power (mainly Microsoft’s Surface and Lenovo’s Yoga) aren’t as good at being laptops as a purpose-built laptop or as good at being tablets as a purpose-built tablet. For me, the iPhone Duo straddles a similar line between the iPhone and the iPad. As a regular one-handed iPhone, it feels deeply compromised—too thick and heavy to comfortably hold in one hand for long, no Face ID, no action button, and power/volume/camera button positioning that feels like it was dictated by “where the buttons can physically fit on the phone” rather than ergonomics. It costs nearly twice as much as the iPhone Pro but has an inferior camera system. I do really like the Duo as a Kindle-sized iPad. But if that’s what I want, I can buy something like it for less money without saddling myself with a regular phone I don’t particularly like using. I see the promise of the Duo. It feels well-built—some users have had problems with screen breakage, but it’s not something we ran into. Its hardware and software are cleverly designed. It mostly solves the visible-seam problem that most Android foldables come with. An updated version of the Duo with Face ID support would be just about the best possible version of this form factor, given the limits of battery technology and physics. But for me personally—and for many happy iPhone users—it requires too many compromises to enable features I don’t want badly enough.
Ars Technica - All content
Some iPhone Duo screens are already failing, Apple says only a "small number" are affected
2026-10-27

Some iPhone Duo screens are already failing, Apple says only a "small number" are affected

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
The iPhone Duo launched last week, offering anyone willing to spend $2,000 a chance to experience Apple’s first foldable phone. For some early buyers, the morning after isn’t going great. Despite the company’s big talk about its “precision-engineered” hinge, some devices appear to have a hardware flaw that destroys the device’s impressive internal display within hours (or minutes) of use. The Internet is currently overflowing with images of busted iPhone Duos, all sporting almost identical damage. The OLED is marred by several bright lines originating just below the midpoint of the display. These bands of damaged pixels extend all the way to the bottom and right edges. Some units also show lines of dead pixels following the crease from the top to the area where the bright lines begin. Anyone who has seen a damaged OLED panel will be familiar with this type of defect. When the layers of the display are damaged, the pixels no longer receive the correct voltage to illuminate them. Instead, they get the full driving voltage and are permanently on at maximum brightness. Some pixels can get no voltage, leaving them totally black. The only fix is to replace the OLED, but swapping the iPhone Duo’s screen reportedly costs half as much as the device itself. It sounds like most affected devices are failing within about a day of use. But a few unlucky buyers note they only opened and closed the phone a few times, and in some cases, they hadn’t even finished the initial setup process. Some customers have reported success taking their defective phones to Apple stores for replacement, but spares may be in short supply right now. In the absence of any official explanation, iFixit may be the next best thing. The site has already torn an iPhone Duo apart and taken a close look at the affected area. After years of seeing other foldables fail, there are a few suspects to check. Since foldables need to pass data and power across the hinge, there’s always a cable tucked in there that could press on the screen. The iPhone Duo has a wide cable with “accordion-like pleats,” according to iFixit, but this doesn’t quite match the accounts of the damage. People with broken $2,000 iPhones say there’s unusual physical resistance and sometimes even a clicking sound as the device closes. After that, the lines appear. Using CT scans of the phone, iFixit has shown that the point of failure starts below where the ribbon cable passes through the hinge. The width of the supporting hinge mechanism changes in that spot, suggesting a mechanical misalignment. The animation above shows iFixit’s best guess at what’s happening. The support bar in that narrowed section of the hinge may be getting stuck, preventing it from dropping far enough to accommodate the waterdrop bend in the OLED panel when the device closes. This creates stress on the display that ultimately damages the matrix. YouTuber CuriousHagi also zeroed in on that region, identifying a place where the thinner link may be snapping during operation. He speculates the component is physically sticking to the support plate. If this fiddly little hinge piece is the culprit, there’s no way to prevent the OLED on an affected device from failing. It could happen after five minutes or five days, but continuous pressure on an OLED panel will eventually lead to permanent damage. Apple has issued a brief statement on the Duo issues, but it doesn’t offer much in the way of detail. The response to iPhone Duo from early users has been incredibly positive, and we are seeing exciting things from developers who are rapidly updating their apps to take advantage of its unique form factor. We are aware of an issue impacting the inner display on a small number of iPhone Duo units. If users experience issues with their iPhone Duo display, they should contact AppleCare for a replacement. Apple’s Alex Kirschner has also reportedly said that the issue appears on affected units very quickly, so buyers won’t have to wonder if their expensive foldables are ticking time bombs. The company’s statement stops short of promising a new device, but that’s probably what will happen after contacting support, provided there are units. Apple will want to get damaged Duos in the hands of engineers who can determine the root cause of the failure. The scale is impossible to know—Apple says only a “small number” of units are breaking in this way. Even with all these reports, most iPhone Duos are probably not going to self-destruct. Apple sells a lot of devices, so even a low failure rate could see a flood of reports on social media. Adding moving parts to a phone will lead to at least some increase in hardware issues, and Apple certainly wouldn’t be the first device maker to have problems with its first foldable launch. In 2019, Samsung released the original Galaxy Fold with a much more primitive hinge design. Even before launch, reviewers began seeing OLED damage after a few days of use. The issue in that case was a design with openings that allowed particulates to infiltrate the hinge, where they damaged the screen from underneath. Samsung delayed the release while it re-engineered the hinge. Samsung’s foldables have not been bulletproof by any means, but there have not been widespread reports of defects immediately after unboxing. Other makers of foldable phones move so few units that occasional defects may not make waves online. Apple is just lucky that way. Update 10/27 at 10 a.m. ET with Apple statement.
Ars Technica - All content
Sculpture re-creates odd optical effects of famed Lycurgus Cup
2026-10-27

Sculpture re-creates odd optical effects of famed Lycurgus Cup

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Dating back to around 400 CE and made of dichroic glass, the Lycurgus Cup is notable for exhibiting different colors depending on the light. (It gets its name from the scene depicted on its surface, of King Lycurgus of Thrace.) Light it from the front, and the cup looks green; light it from behind, and the color changes to a deep red. University of Sheffield scientists have re-created the original recipe to produce a contemporary glass sculpture with the same optical effect. The cup is considered one of the seven glass wonders of the world, one of just 40 glass cage cups that survived. Exactly why the unusual color effects of the Lycurgus Cup occurred was a mystery until 2007, when a team of scientists discovered the color shifts were due to the presence of nanoparticles in the glass—the result of Roman artisans adding finely ground gold and silver into the mix. Those nanoparticles change how the electrons in the cup vibrate in response to light so that the color one sees shifts with the observer’s vantage point. As we reported in 2019, it’s a similar phenomenon to the one responsible for the shifting colors of 19th-century daguerreotypes due to the scattering properties of nanoparticles. The Romans added salt when they melted the silicon (sand) to make the glass, and those salts contained gold atoms. In the melting process, those atoms aggregate and produce nanoparticles, which in turn produce the colors. It’s how any stained glass is made. It’s also similar to how the precise lattice-like structure of photonic crystals produces iridescent colors in nature, like the wings of butterflies, or opals. There is a key difference, however: The nanoparticles are embedded into the glass of the cup, whereas with a daguerreotype, they are sitting on top of a substrate, adding some reflection that does not occur with an artifact like the Lycurgus Cup. So the scattering of the particles has a strong dependence on the substrate. Also, if one coats the surface of a daguerreotype with a layer of gold, the colors shift more to the red side of the spectrum. That’s in keeping with a 19th-century trick called “gilding,” in which the photographer would apply a solution of gold salt to the image to achieve warmer tones and sharper contrast. In 2020, Dutch researchers reproduced the green/red dichroic effect of the Lycurgus Cup in a 3D-printable nanocomposite material by embedding small amounts of silver and gold nanoparticles of the proper size and shapes. But nobody had yet devised a consistent, replicable recipe. University of Sheffield scientists Lisa Hollands and Caroline Jackson—the latter an expert on Roman glass—worked together to come up with one, experimenting with different furnace types, materials, oxygen levels, and at one point including sugar in the mix. “I knew that making a glass of this type was likely to be a complex task, as it is not just a case of adding nanoparticles to glass,” said Hollands. “I’ve studied and worked with glass for over 25 years, so I used the knowledge and experience I’ve gained, coupled with a modern scientific understanding of what I was trying to achieve—although if the Romans could have seen me experimenting, they’d probably laugh and ask: ‘Why does she not know how to make this?’” The experimentation paid off when Hollands found a mix that produced glass with the desired optical effects, appearing green in reflected light, red when backlit. Her initial glass piece was exhibited at LightFest, which is part of Molecular Photonic Breadboards, a UK project geared toward designing new materials that harness the power of light for sustainable technologies. Project leaders next decided to commission UK glass artist Elliott Walker—the S2 winner of Netflix’s Blown Away competition—to create a contemporary sculpture to pay homage to the original Lycurgus Cup. “It is a very rare thing to have the opportunity to work with glass developed from ancient mixtures and recipes, and it is always a very exciting one,” said Walker. “You can really get an idea of how the glass makers of the past would have adapted to this sort of glass; it’s like shaking their hands. I wanted to keep the sculpture quite classical in form and to carry through the original story of Lycurgus shown on the cup, but we needed a blown element within this to really show the dichroic glass. The piece is a blend of the Lycurgus story and the Farnese Atlas sculpture, with the globe replaced by a cup.” The public will get its chance to see Walker’s sculpture in person when it is unveiled at the Millennium Gallery next month during the University of Sheffield’s Festival of the Mind. After that, it will have a permanent home on display at the university’s Turner Museum of Glass.
Fortune | FORTUNE
About 70% of tax scholars would vote against California’s billionaire tax, a new survey finds
2026-10-27

About 70% of tax scholars would vote against California’s billionaire tax, a new survey finds

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
News of billionaires fleeing California over the proposed billionaire tax have dominated the headlines: the one-time tax of 5% on the wealth of California’s (perhaps dwindling) 250 billionaires. But what about those who chose to become academics, and as a result, may not have achieved billionaire status? It turns out most academics who study taxes for a living would vote against California’s proposed billionaire tax, a new survey finds. Jeff Hoopes, a professor at the University of North Carolina and research director of the UNC Tax Center, surveyed members of the National Tax Association, a professional group of mostly academic economists, accountants, and lawyers. Of the 105 respondents, 73 (69.5%) said they would vote against Proposition 40, and 32 (30.5%) said they would vote for it. Accountants were the most opposed, with 17 of 20 (85%) against the tax. Among economists, 49 of 69 (71%) opposed the tax. Meanwhile, among lawyers, nine of 15 (60%) favored the measure. Law professors also wrote much of the new measure: Three of its principal drafters, Brian Galle, David Gamage, and Darien Shanske, are tax-law professors. Overall, that comes out to seven out of 10 tax scholars who would think otherwise before voting on Prop 40—but it’s not because they oppose the idea behind it. “The 70% of folks who would not vote for Prop 40 seems to have two different camps,” Hoopes told Fortune. “Those who would favor wealth taxes in general, but just thought this particular version was bad, and those who don’t think a wealth tax is a good idea at all.” Some respondents explained their votes in written comments, and for those in Hoopes’s first camp, respondents said they would back a federal wealth tax but doubted a single state could enforce one when billionaires and their assets can move. Others questioned whether a “one-time” tax would stay that way, or said valuing private companies would bring costly disputes. Supporters in the sample cited the efficiency of a surprise tax on wealth already built up, the case for reducing concentrated wealth, and the revenue it would raise. For Hoopes, the main concern is people moving from the state: “I think there is a real chance that we see some billionaires change where they claim residency based on this tax.” In Norway, he said, the very richest moved in response to its wealth tax: “It would not take very many of the very richest ones moving to make a large dent in the expected tax revenue.” He also cited Elon Musk, who moved from California to Texas and referenced that taxes were part of the reason. “It’s not crazy to think this might push other like-minded billionaires over the edge,” Hoopes said, noting that California already has the highest top income tax rate in the country. “In a state that is near the very top in both spending and taxes per capita, it’s clear California has a spending problem,” he continued. “If I had a friend who made $600,000 a year but had $700,000 in expenses, I would not tell him to get some side-hustle to bring in extra money. I would say figure out how to live on the extraordinary income he already has.” The tax has the potential to raise as much revenue as a 5% income tax on all Californian taxpayers, and even raise about $100 billion to backfill federal Medicaid cuts. But some papers throw water on those estimates: A Hoover Institution working paper estimates the state could lose more in future income tax revenue than the wealth tax brings in. The drafters’ estimate supports the $100 billion figure. Hoopes said he ran the survey after six Nobel Prize–winning economists, including Paul Krugman and Joseph Stiglitz, endorsed the measure in September. “I was baffled, because even the left-leaning economists I know were against it,” he said. “It turns out the economists I know personally are much more like the 105 people who responded to my survey than the six people who won a Nobel Prize.” The survey has limits. Hoopes used a 2024 membership directory, and the NTA did not sponsor or approve the poll. Hoopes sits on the advisory council of the right-leaning Tax Foundation. Voters, meanwhile, are more evenly split. A September Public Policy Institute of California poll found 52% of likely voters back Prop. 40, but majorities also backed Props. 41 and 42, two measures funded largely by Google cofounder Sergey Brin, that would effectively cancel the tax. Fortune | FORTUNE
This Harvard professor spent 8 years traveling the world researching the secret history of capitalism and how ‘marginal’ and ‘weak’ it used to be
2026-01-18

This Harvard professor spent 8 years traveling the world researching the secret history of capitalism and how ‘marginal’ and ‘weak’ it used to be

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-01-18

Visit Original Website
Sven Beckert isn’t here to judge capitalism, even though he just wrote a provocative, ambitious, 1,300-page book on its history. As he writes several times in the book (which this editor has read), he’s not even sure what it is. He’s trying to understand it. The Harvard professor, who Zoomed in to talk to Fortune from his home office in Cambridge, Mass., explained that his new book, Capitalism: A Global History, is the product of an eight-year odyssey to seek understanding of the way we actually live out our economic lives. “Often, when I teach the history of capitalism here at Harvard, many of my students think that capitalism is kind of the state of nature.” But that’s just not the case when you look at the historical record, he added. When pressed to explain what his book accomplishes, he said it’s two-pronged: to offer a more global perspective on the history of capitalism and to “denaturalize” the history of whatever capitalism is. Capitalism is neither eternal nor natural, in Beckert’s telling; itʼs a human invention that spread and evolved over centuries through deliberate choices, sometimes extraordinary violence, and incredible institutional innovation. Capitalism rose from the margins of medieval trade to dominate modern life, and so it could also, someday, fade or transform again. These may be considered bold claims by some who take capitalism’s primacy as inevitable, but the book has been generally well reviewed. Although some critics have taken shots at it for being a bit of a “doorstop,” like Beckert’s previous tome, the Pulitzer finalist, award-winning Empire of Cotton, the ambition and narrative boldness of this global history have been generally praised. In the Boston Globe, Hamilton Cain wrote that Beckert weaves a sprawling tapestry that “unfurls with the suspense and intricacy of a detective novel,” with capitalism’s ascendance reading a bit like a crime story, even a whodunit. Adrian Wooldridge of Bloomberg Opinion criticized it for nearly the identical thing, saying that the book “ignores [the] secret sauce” of innovation, focusing more on its exploitative history than on how it created new value. Beckert insisted that his book is all about the question of how, not why. He attempted to chart “how we get from a world in which this logic exists, but it’s marginal, to a world in the year 2025, where [it] almost structures all of our economic life—and almost all of our lives.” Beckert told Fortune that his research showed traces of capitalist logic can be seen in the historical record as far back as 1,000 years ago, but for hundreds of years it “remained marginal to economic life.” In his book, he talks many times of “islands” and “nodes,” as capitalists were at first outsiders, regarded as almost freakish by people who lived their life without the constant accumulation of more money to invest. Then, when the tide started to turn and capitalism became ascendant, in the last 500 years, the islands and nodes shifted to the holdouts from the capitalist way of life, like the farmers of the 20th century. “The first thing I always say is that capitalism is not the same as the existence of markets,” Beckert said. He pointed out that “economic life” existed for all of human history, but not the relentless accumulation and reinvestment of capital, which was practiced by a few merchant communities on the outskirts of society. Capitalism “has existed in many different parts of the world,” Beckert insisted, “but it was also rather thinly spread and rather weak…This kind of capitalist logic that is so crucial to economic life today, that is something relatively novel.” Beckert points out in his book that until the fall of the USSR in the late 1980s, something like 30% of the world was living in a noncapitalist system, and much of the rural West was able to avoid the capitalist way of life by, for example, growing their own food as subsistence farmers. (Tom Lee of Fundstrat recently compared the emergence of artificial intelligence to flash-frozen food in the 1920s, which changed the composition of the economy from 40% subsistence farmers to just 2%.) The professor highlighted ancient mercantile communities of capitalists in unexpected places such as the Port of Aden, in Yemen, or Cambay, in modern Gujarat, India. Goods were traded across oceans from Aden as early as 1150, he finds, while Song-dynasty China invented paper money centuries before Europe, and textiles thrived in ancient Indian hubs ages before the Industrial Revolution. But these capitalists were encircled for hundreds of years by a sea of subsistence farmers and tributary empires that operated on completely different principles. “The extremely global nature of early merchant communities, that was something that I, broadly speaking, I did have a sense of,” Beckert said, but hadn’t put together in quite this way before. “They were always connected to a state,” Beckert said of the early merchants, but they were also freewheeling and kind of separate from that state,” he said, that is, until things drastically changed in the 19th century. Beckert highlights the disturbing case of Hermann Röchling, the German steel industrialist who saw such capitalist opportunities that he closely allied himself with successive German governments and found himself “on trial for war crimes, not just in one war, but in two wars, World War I and World War II, which must be a world historical record.” To illustrate just how unnatural the capitalist logic was once considered, Beckert’s book highlights the odd case of Robert Keayne, a Puritan merchant in 1639 Boston. Keayne was dragged before a court and church elders nearly 400 years ago, but not for stealing. Instead it was for the “very evil” practice of selling goods for a profit that exceeded community standards. He was nearly excommunicated for behaving in a way that is now considered the basic function of business. It would take centuries, Beckert argues, for the “covetous” accumulation of wealth to be rebranded as a public good, or in other words, for capitalism to be regarded as normal. For his part, Beckert insisted that he’s not trying to judge capitalism, even if some harsh judgments emerge throughout his book. “The ability to write this book is, of course, itself an outcome of the capitalist revolution,” he told Fortune, arguing that at this juncture in the 21st century, the logic of capitalism has brought enormous productivity gains, unprecedented economic growth and made many people much wealthier. He wouldn’t be able to fly to Barbados or Cambodia for research without it, he acknowledged. And Beckert did travel far and wide over eight years to dig up what he regards as a kind of secret history of the origins of capitalism. From the dusty outskirts of Phnom Penh to the archives of the Godrej company in India to the relics of the sugar plantations of Barbados (where he spent 10 days), Beckert surprised himself again and again by what he found. When the American colonies emerge on the scene, for example, more than a third of the way into his narrative, Beckert points out that West Indies were much more central to global capitalism than the 13 territories that would become the United States. The city of Boston, he notes, might have perished if it didn’t figure out how to become a service hub for Barbados, whose sugar plantations generated huge revenues. “That’s also kind of an amazing story,” Beckert agreed. “For us, Barbados is a foreign country and it’s far away, but for them [Bostonians in the early 1700s], it was also part of the British empire. And then it was…kind of a suburb to Boston—or vice versa. They were tightly integrated with one another.” Beckert said he was surprised in what his research turned up of the darkness of capitalism’s past. “I did know a lot about the history of slavery, but being on this island and then reading the historical records and reading the kind of horrific accounts of what happened on this island in the 1600s, that was also not really surprising, but it was really quite shocking, the degree of violence that I found within this history.” Beckert told Fortune that he believes the world is currently in a “moment of transition,” similar to the shift that occurred in the 1970s when the Keynesian order gave way to neoliberalism. One of the largest surprises of the book is at its very conclusion, when he suggested that someday, capitalism could end. The fact that “it rests on the ever expanding accumulation of capital” implies that somehow, someway, the capital will run out. “In the distant future,” he writes, historians will look back at our times and “find it difficult to understand our ways of thinking, our ways of being.” Ultimately, Beckert said he views his book not as a moral tale with capitalism as the villain, but as an investigation into human agency. By revealing that capitalism was once fragile, marginal, and weak—and that it required centuries of specific political choices, violence, and institutional building to become dominant—he hopes to show that the future remains unwritten. “This is not like a machine that kind of unfolds on its own,” Beckert said. “This is a human-created order.” As the global economy faces new shocks in the mid-2020s, Beckert’s history offers a reminder: The economy is not a force of nature. It was made by people, and it can be remade by them. “The future is open,” he told Fortune. “People build a world that’s different from the world that they were born into [and] sometimes the least powerful have made a huge difference in the trajectory of the development of capitalism. So, I think that is important to see at the contemporary moment.” Nick Lichtenberg is business editor and was formerly Fortune's executive editor of global news. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Fortune | FORTUNE
The AI job apocalypse is ‘unhelpful marketing, bad economics and worse history,’ a16z says
2026-05-07

The AI job apocalypse is ‘unhelpful marketing, bad economics and worse history,’ a16z says

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-05-07

Visit Original Website
The doomsday forecasts have been building for years: AI will hollow out the white-collar workforce, destroy entry-level jobs, and create a permanent underclass of technologically displaced workers. Now, one of Silicon Valley’s most influential firms has published a detailed rebuttal saying, basically, don’t believe the hype. In a new essay published Tuesday, Andreessen Horowitz General Partner David George declared that the vision of an “AI job apocalypse” is a “complete fantasy”—”unhelpful marketing, bad economics and worse history,” rooted in what the firm calls a logical error that economists have been debunking for more than a century. The piece represents the most expansive version yet of a case the firm’s co-founders have been making publicly for months. Ben Horowitz made a version of the argument on the Invest Like the Best podcast earlier this year, pointing out that AI technologies have been advancing since at least 2012—when ImageNet changed computer vision—and the catastrophic job destruction hasn’t arrived. The intellectual foundation of the a16z essay is a well-worn economic concept: the “lump-of-labor fallacy,” which holds that an economy only has a fixed amount of work to be done, and that anything—a machine, an AI model, even an immigrant—that does more of it necessarily leaves humans with less. “The AI Alarmist, ‘Permanent Underclass’ panic isn’t a convincing story,” George wrote. “It isn’t even a new story. It’s the “lump-of-labor” fallacy, with updated branding.” The problem, he argued, is that human wants and needs are not fixed. As one technology lowers the cost of some activity, people don’t simply stop wanting things—they find new things to want, creating new categories of work. The obvious example is the great economist John Maynard Keynes, who famously predicted nearly a century ago that automation would produce a 15-hour work week. But people didn’t sit back and enjoy the surplus; they found new and different things to do. George marshaled a sequence of historical examples to make the point. Farm mechanization eliminated roughly a third of U.S. employment in the early 20th century—and yet those workers flowed into factories, offices, hospitals, and eventually the software industry, while farm output nearly tripled. Electrification didn’t destroy manufacturing jobs; it reorganized factories around new workflows, and labor productivity growth doubled for decades after its widespread adoption. And the spreadsheet—often cited as a job-killer for bookkeepers—actually led to an explosion in the number of financial analysts. “We lost ~1M bookkeepers and gained ~1.5M financial analysts,” he wrote. At nearly the same time, across the country in New York, [hotlink]Apollo Global Management[/hotlink] Chief Economist Torsten Slok continued his arguments in a similar vein, working to popularize the “Jevons Paradox” about how declining technology costs lead to a surge in demand and job creation. The release of Microsoft Excel is a perfect example, he wrote on May 7. “The bottom line is that rather than reducing the need for accountants, Excel dramatically lowered the cost of financial analysis, reporting and record-keeping, making these services accessible to a far broader range of businesses and use cases,” Slok wrote. George also cited the Jevons Paradox: when the cost of a powerful input falls, the economy does not politely stand still. It does more. “When fossil fuels first made energy cheap and plentiful, we did more than put whalers and woodchoppers out of business; we invented plastics!” Another Jevons citation came this week from Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei, who mentioned it during his firm’s announcement of supposedly labor-destroying tools to be deployed on Wall Street. Crucially, a16z doesn’t just argue from history and theory—it argues from the present. Citing a battery of recent academic research, the firm concludes that “the weight of the data does not support the doomer claim.” The one notable exception: Stanford researchers found that early-career workers aged 22–25 in the most AI-exposed occupations experienced a 16% relative decline in employment since ChatGPT’s release in late 2022. Even here, a16z argues the picture is more complex: “Before anyone concludes that “AI is killing entry-level jobs,” however, it bears mentioning that these researchers also variously found an increase in entry-level roles where AI is augmentative (and an increase where AI has no impact at all).” The a16z case is powerful—and it has serious, named critics who disagree with almost every premise. Take economist Anton Korinek. If the quest for artificial general intelligence succeeds, he argues, “we are not looking at another Industrial Revolution” that ultimately rewards workers, he told The New York Times in February; rather, “labor itself becomes optional for the economy.” The Carnegie Endowment for International Peace published a detailed taxonomy of the debate in April, categorizing participants into three camps: the “alarmed,” the “patient,” and the “excited.” A16z squarely occupies the excited camp, with co-founder Marc Andreessen identified as one of the most excitable, but the Carnegie analysis shows why the debate is harder to resolve than either side admits. The alarmed and excited aren’t simply arguing about the same facts—they are making different predictions about the speed of AI progress, the ability of firms to adopt it, and whether new jobs will emerge fast enough to absorb displaced workers. What separates this moment from prior technological transitions, critics argue, is velocity. The alarmed, as Carnegie documents, believe that scaling laws, massive investment, and the potential for AI-accelerated AI research will produce capability jumps unlike anything history offers a template for. OpenAI’s GDPVal benchmark—which tests AI systems on complex workforce tasks that take humans an average of seven hours to complete—found that the newest AI models beat human workers in a subset of 220 tasks, with expert judges preferring AI responses 83% of the time. The “patient” camp—represented by Princeton computer scientists Arvind Narayanan and Sayash Kapoor, Nobel laureate Daron Acemoglu, and cognitive scientist Gary Marcus—argue that capabilities gaps, hallucination problems, and the sheer organizational difficulty of integrating AI into enterprises will slow adoption to a pace measured in decades, not years. Scale AI’s Remote Labor Index, which tests models on the kind of multi-day, complex tasks a human freelancer might take on, found that the best AI systems could complete just 2.5% of tasks at a level matching the human gold standard as of March 2026, a percentage that crept up marginally within a few months. The economist David Autor, one of the most careful students of technological displacement, occupies a conditional optimist position that is more nuanced than either camp: “AI, if used well, can assist with restoring the middle-skill, middle-class heart of the US labor market”—but he is explicit that “this is not a forecast but an argument about what is possible.” The a16z argument is, of course, self-interested. Andreessen Horowitz has invested billions across the AI stack, from foundation-model companies to AI-native startups seeking to disrupt legacy industries. A cultural and political environment in which AI is widely perceived as a job-killer creates pressure for regulation, slows enterprise adoption, and clouds the consumer sentiment on which its portfolio companies depend. That conflict doesn’t make this argument wrong, though. The historical record and the cited academic papers are all real. And as Carnegie notes, even the economist survey data shows that the majority of academics expect AI to bring only modest deviations from historical economic trends, even as they acknowledge the possibility of severe disruption under faster-than-expected capability scenarios. What a16z is less forthcoming about is the asymmetry of harm if it’s wrong. If the optimists are right, the labor market reorganizes itself over time and workers find new roles, as they always have. If the alarmists are right and policy has been shaped by bullish venture-capital certainty, millions of displaced workers will face a safety net and a retraining infrastructure that was never built to absorb them. Paradoxically, the Yale Budget Lab recently noted that the very productivity gains that Wall Street appears to be pricing in would result in many millions of displaced workers, both solving the $39 trillion national debt crisis and further exacerbating it simultaneously. A Quinnipiac survey released in March found that 70% of Americans now believe AI will lead to fewer job opportunities for humans, up from 56% the year before. Whether that fear reflects bad economics and worse history—or a genuine intuition about something different this time—is the question that no historical analogy can fully answer. a16z did not respond to a request for comment.
