BofA throws cold water on AI apocalypse panic: 60% of today’s jobs didn’t exist in 1940
2026-05-04

BofA throws cold water on AI apocalypse panic: 60% of today’s jobs didn’t exist in 1940

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-05-04

Visit Original Website
The doomsday crowd may want to check its history books. As fears of an AI-driven jobs apocalypse intensify across boardrooms, union halls, and college campuses, Bank of America’s global research team is urging a reality check. In a report published April 28, BofA economists argue that the “Armageddon narrative” around artificial intelligence “sits uneasily with both economic theory and the evidence so far”—and they’ve got 85 years of labor market data to back them up. The bank’s central argument is simple: 60% of the jobs that exist in the United States today didn’t exist in 1940. Data scientists, social media managers, and cloud developers “barely existed 20 years ago but are now mainstream jobs.” Agriculture, which employed roughly 40% of Americans in the early 1900s, now accounts for just 1% of U.S. employment. In each case of transformation—the Industrial Revolution, electrification, computerization—the economy didn’t just survive the disruption. It invented its way out of it. “Adaptability is the new job security,” the report concludes. The bank isn’t sugarcoating AI’s reach. Globally, roughly 840 million jobs, about one in four, are exposed to generative AI, with high-income economies facing the steepest exposure at 33% of all jobs. Younger workers, women, and the highly educated face the greatest disruption risk, largely because they’re concentrated in the white-collar, language-intensive, and administrative roles that AI can most readily assist or automate. But BofA drew a sharp distinction between exposure and elimination. According to International Labor Organization data cited in the report, 13% of global jobs sit in the “augmentation” category—meaning AI will enhance, not replace, those workers—versus just 2.3% with genuine automation potential. “GenAI will primarily augment rather than replace workers,” the bank writes, with professional and financial services standing to benefit most and repetitive roles in customer service, information/communications technology, and administration facing the highest substitution risk. BofA leans heavily on a favorite economist’s parable: the ATM. When automated teller machines proliferated in the 1970s and ’80s, conventional wisdom held that bank tellers were finished. Instead, lower operating costs allowed banks to open more branches, and tellers were redeployed into sales and customer service. The result was increased total teller employment. Similarly, word processors didn’t eliminate clerical workers; they shifted them toward coordination and communication roles. Excel didn’t gut accounting departments; it expanded them. E-commerce didn’t kill retail employment either; the U.S. still has roughly 15 million to 16 million retail workers today, about the same as in the 1990s. But the ATM parable cuts both ways. Economist and essayist David Oks argued in an influential, widely read Substack post that most of this ATM story is just half the tale. Since the early 2000s, when you could upload checks onto your iPhone and Venmo your friends for meals, “bank teller employment has fallen off a cliff.” “It is paradigm replacement, not task automation, that actually displaces workers,” Oks wrote. The worry, then, is not that AI will slot into existing workflows and do them a bit faster. It’s that agentic AI—systems that can autonomously execute multistep tasks, rewrite codebases, orchestrate entire workflows—may not automate the job. It may make the job irrelevant. BofA acknowledged the risk, flagging agentic AI as a “more structurally disruptive force” that shifts AI from a task-level assistant to, in the bank’s own framing, “AI as worker itself.” Then there’s the Jevons paradox. Writing in the 1860s, economist William Stanley Jevons observed that making steam engines more fuel-efficient didn’t reduce coal consumption—it caused coal consumption to explode, because cheaper energy unlocked entirely new industrial demand. [hotlink]Apollo Global Management[/hotlink] chief economist Torsten Slok has been increasingly applying the same logic to AI, dubbing it the “Jevons employment effect.” As AI makes professional work cheaper, the total market for that work tends to expand rather than contract, potentially growing headcount in fields from law to accounting to consulting. The open question for AI is whether cheaper legal memos and financial models will unlock new, previously unmet demand or whether most of that was already being served with AI simply doing the same work with fewer people. Oks’s iPhone counterpoint applies here too: Jevons worked for the ATM but hasn’t worked for the bank teller. BofA’s research team isn’t alone in reaching for history as a rebuttal to panic. Fundstrat’s Tom Lee has made a similar argument using flash-frozen food. In the 1920s, Clarence Birdseye’s invention of commercial flash freezing reduced farm labor from 30%–40% of the U.S. workforce to just 2%–5%, yet the transition ultimately created enough new jobs that the economy came out ahead. Bank of America CEO Brian Moynihan has been making a version of the same case in his own public appearances. Speaking on LinkedIn’s This Is Working podcast in February, he noted that in 1969, economists predicted computers would eliminate all management roles. BofA today employs roughly 20,000 managers. AI discoveries are augmentations of human capabilities, Moynihan said. “It applies to our auditors, our lawyers, our investment bankers.” If BofA is relatively sanguine about aggregate job creation, other economists have been notably less relaxed about distribution. Early data from the Dallas Fed shows that AI-exposed industries are seeing wages rise for experienced workers even as entry-level hiring slumps, suggesting AI is simultaneously augmenting senior staff and squeezing out younger workers at the bottom of the ladder. Nobel laureate economist Daron Acemoglu’s work offers the starkest warning: Unless AI generates new labor-intensive tasks at scale, its productivity gains will naturally flow to capital owners rather than workers, widening the gap between those who own the machines and those who once operated them. That concern is compounded by a structural gap in the safety net. As Fortune reported in March, nearly 75% of workers displaced by AI won’t collect unemployment benefits, leaving transition support dangerously thin. BofA said AI will increase pressure on governments to provide wage insurance, enhanced unemployment benefits, reskilling incentives, and tax reform to ensure the gains from AI don’t concentrate in too few hands. “Policymakers will need to design transition frameworks that cushion short-term disruption, while preventing labor-market scarring, especially for mid-career workers with skills at risk of obsolescence,” it added. That prescription is gaining unlikely allies in Silicon Valley. When venture capitalist Vinod Khosla sat down with Fortune editor-in-chief Alyson Shontell in March, he floated eliminating federal income taxes entirely for the roughly 100 million Americans earning less than $100,000, paid for by equalizing the tax rate on capital gains with ordinary income. His math: 40% of all capital gains taxes are paid by people earning more than $10 million a year, making the revenue neutral without raising the overall burden. A month later, OpenAI arrived at the same destination by a different route. In a 13-page policy paper titled Industrial Policy for the Intelligence Age, Sam Altman’s company called for shifting the tax base away from payroll and labor income—the very revenue streams AI threatens to hollow out—and toward corporate income and capital gains, including what many have termed a “robot tax” on automated labor. OpenAI warned that as AI automates more work, the wage and payroll tax revenue that funds Social Security, Medicaid, SNAP, and housing assistance could collapse, making capital-based taxation not just equitable, but fiscally necessary. Both Khosla and OpenAI agree that the American tax code was designed for an economy where human labor generated most of the value. That economy is rapidly vanishing. BofA flagged one development that could scramble the historical analogy: the rise of what it calls the OPC, or “one-person company.” Enabled by agentic AI systems that can autonomously manage workflows, schedule tasks, run scripts, and coordinate operations, 2026 may mark the year a single entrepreneur can perform functions that once required entire teams. For now, Anthropic’s own research, published last month, showed the gap between what AI can do and what it’s actually doing remains wide but closing fast, particularly in management, legal, and financial roles. A Fortune 500 consultancy recently estimated 93% of jobs face some level of disruption, with a $4.5 trillion price tag attached. Whether the jobs come back, and for whom, is ultimately a question BofA conceded it cannot fully answer. History says the jobs will return. The question is whether they’ll arrive fast enough to matter, and whether agentic AI turns out to be an ATM or an iPhone. The humanist at the top of real estate’s automation machine: ‘I happen to despise the term headcount in the world of AI’
2026-10-27

The humanist at the top of real estate’s automation machine: ‘I happen to despise the term headcount in the world of AI’

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Neil Murray does not talk like an executive preparing to automate a workforce. As the head of Real Estate Management Services at commercial property giant JLL, Murray oversees an organization of more than 88,000 people responsible for keeping the nearly 6 billion square feet of buildings in his purview functioning daily. Yet when conversation turns to artificial intelligence, he recoils from corporate jargon. “I happen to despise the term ‘headcount’ in the world of AI,” Murray told Fortune. “I think it sort of dehumanizes what we’re talking about.” While Murray dismisses the idea that AI will shrink the workforce, JLL’s own internal staffing tells a more measured story. Asked whether headcount in Murray’s 88,000-person division has actually grown over the past three years, the company declined to provide figures ahead of earnings, describing headcount as merely “stable” and projecting it to hold flat or grow only modestly in 2027. Instead, the company offered a candid redefinition of corporate growth: “Stability does not equate to stagnation,” a JLL spokesperson told Fortune. “In an environment where AI is genuinely improving individual productivity, holding headcount flat while increasing output is actually a meaningful result.” Instead, Murray offers an article of faith increasingly rare among corporate leadership: Automation will not shrink his division. “This is my profound belief,” he said. “I say that as a leader and as a parent—a profound belief that we will create better jobs, more impactful jobs, more human-centric jobs—and automate some of the jobs that people didn’t enjoy doing anyway. The notion that AI will somehow reduce the number of people we have serving this industry is just not right.” JLL does not break out stand-alone headcount for Murray’s division. Murray offers an earnest, deeply appealing defense of physical work. But a few pages into JLL’s own research, provided exclusively to Fortune ahead of release, the arithmetic pushes back a bit. The tension sits squarely inside JLL’s 2026 Global State of Facilities Management Report, a survey of 1,321 organizations across 15 countries. While Murray called technology an unalloyed amplifier of human talent, the organizations surveyed are planning an explicit shift away from payroll. Eighty-nine percent of survey respondents agreed that facility management budgets will shift from labor toward technology and automation. More critically, 87% expect outsourcing contracts to abandon labor-based models in favor of outcome-based pricing by 2030. That subtle contractual tweak rewires the financial incentives of commercial property. Under traditional facilities contracts, providers bill clients for hours and bodies on-site; more staff translates directly into more revenue. Under outcome-based agreements, clients pay fixed fees for performance metrics—unbroken uptime, completed work orders, or reduced energy consumption. In that structure, every hour of human labor ceases to be a revenue stream and becomes a direct cost against the provider’s profit margin. Murray dismissed fears of job replacement by leaning on the limits of current robotics. “Humanoid robots doing engineering work are a long way off,” he said, adding that it was his own opinion, to be sure. He is almost certainly right that an android will not diagnose a complex commercial boiler system anytime soon. But corporate real estate does not need humanoid robots to reduce staffing levels. JLL’s report notes that client adoption of robotics is poised to nearly double, jumping from 37% today to 71% by 2030. The company concedes that this surge will come from “purpose-built technologies, not humanoids”—specifically autonomous scrubbers, robotic lawn care, and remote security systems. These machines do not displace licensed HVAC engineers; they displace cleaners, security personnel, and access-control staff. Murray’s confidence rests heavily on demographic scarcity. In key markets, he pointed out, JLL cannot find enough bodies to fill open roles, with 68% of organizations reporting that skilled-trades shortages disrupted operations over the past year. Across the broader economy, employers require an estimated 1.7 million skilled-trades workers annually through 2035, even as nearly a quarter are 55 or older. “The trades have probably been out of vogue for a generation,” Murray said. “Ironically, with AI, people are seeing the value of those skilled trades now … I do see a golden age for trades.” That is still some way off. Research from the Ad Council and Ford Motor Co. found that only 9% of high school students are actively considering entering a trade. Murray genuinely wants to believe that technology will elevate the physical workforce rather than discard it. His defense is heartfelt, rooted in community, and delivered with obvious warmth. “I certainly hope,” he added, “those comments don’t age badly.” Nick Lichtenberg is business editor and was formerly Fortune's executive editor of global news. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. China’s AI startups can match the U.S.’s models. They can’t yet match the U.S.’s money
2026-10-07

China’s AI startups can match the U.S.’s models. They can’t yet match the U.S.’s money

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Alvin Yap is the chief executive officer for Asia at EquitiesFirst By many measures, China is fast catching up with the U.S. in AI development. Moonshot’s Kimi K3—the world’s largest open-weight model—has approached the performance of America’s frontier systems. Analysts estimate the best Chinese models are now just four months behind the most sophisticated releases from OpenAI and Anthropic, compared to seven months at the start of the year. Chinese models have gone from 1.2% of token traffic (a common way to calculate AI usage) in 2024 to more than half of the total by the summer of 2026. But the biggest threat to that next wave is not technology. It’s money. Between 2023 and 2026, venture funding into U.S. AI companies topped $380 billion; China’s start-ups received barely a tenth of that figure, according to Boston Consulting Group. In the past, Chinese entrepreneurs looked to state guidance funds and venture capital backing, but policy-driven funds are known to prioritize later-stage startups while early-stage venture capital is only just recovering from a three-year fundraising drought. There are three reasons why broader funding channels should be a priority for entrepreneurs in the AI economy. First, inflation is taking hold inside the AI economy. CXMT, for example, has been raising memory prices for months and held firm even when Huawei, one of its largest customers, demanded relief. The war for AI talent is just as fierce: postings for AI-related roles surged roughly twelvefold year-on-year in early 2026. Algorithm engineers specializing in large language models command some of the highest pay packages of any technical role in China. Founders must also outcompete deep-pocketed former employers and U.S. rivals. More than half of studies presented at the world’s top AI conference had lead authors based in China. China’s AI talent is known to be in demand worldwide. Second, external funding is scarce. Venture investment in China totaled just $20 billion in the first quarter of 2026, against $267 billion in the U.S. China has raised impressive sums this year—assets under management for newly registered VC funds hit 154 billion yuan ($22.8 billion) in the first five monthes, already exceeding last year’s total—but that is still far below what US venture capital regularly deploys. Meanwhile, China’s state banks, although directed to prioritize technology lending, are absorbing rising non-performing loans elsewhere on their books, which could lead to weaker overall credit supply. Third, profitability will take a while to achieve. Chinese enterprise software firms primarily sell into the domestic market, which limits their revenue base. U.S. rivals have a head start: a global customer base, stronger brand recognition, and R&D budgets deep enough to fund everything from enterprise-grade cybersecurity to polished customer experience design. To be sure, the next generation of AI ventures may not need vast amounts of capital to build applications on top of existing models or fill the gaps in the tech value chain.  Enterprise customers can also provide essential development funding. Entrepreneurs must nevertheless cope without the kind of financial support that is available for the AI sector in the U.S. Closing the performance gap increasingly depends on bulking up in-house computing capacity—yet China’s AI infrastructure spending remains a fraction of what is being spent in the U.S. This is where access to Hong Kong’s finance industry will play a growing role. Its capital markets remain one of the few channels still capable of moving global capital toward Chinese enterprise at scale. More than 430 applicants are in the IPO pipeline in the second half of 2026. The IPO pipeline, however, tells its own story. Many Chinese technology startups are choosing to list earlier than the previous generation of companies did because they lack an alternative. Compare that with the U.S., where the likes of OpenAI and Anthropic have grown to an enormous scale by raising private capital. OpenAI closed a round of more than $100 billion earlier this year, while Anthropic raised $65 billion in May. China’s leading model developers, Zhipu AI and MiniMax, beat OpenAI and Anthropic to the public markets—but their Hong Kong IPOs in January raised just $558 million and $620 million respectively, despite heavy over-subscription. Private credit is another route. Asia-Pacific private credit assets are projected to grow from $59 billion in 2024 to $92 billion by 2027, with China accounting for a fifth of the region’s activity. However, these loan providers tend to prioritize bigger or established companies. It is already clear that the AI sector, unlike the software sector, will not be dominated by U.S. firms alone. China’s startups have learned the lessons of the previous decade, commanding global respect and winning a growing customer base. The country’s open-weight strategy has given its leading AI companies a cost advantage – even Silicon Valley leaders acknowledge it. For China’s latest generation of AI entrepreneurs, venture capital and bank loans—the two main sources of start-up funding – may not be enough in the coming years. Keeping pace in the AI race will require using every asset and every channel available. It may mean a public listing earlier than they would have preferred, exploring the growing private credit ecosystem, revenue sharing with customers or leveraging the equity they hold as collateral. The entrepreneurs involved in the next wave of China’s AI development will need to be creative with funding to maintain their competitive edge at a time when overseas rivals are spending 10 times as much. How a combination of interests led this leader to a cybersecurity career
2026-10-27

How a combination of interests led this leader to a cybersecurity career

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
TCS’ Virginia Lee explores her career in cybersecurity, the many ways you can find your calling and the changing landscape of cybersecurity. Virginia Lee, the head of cybersecurity for Ireland at Tata Consultancy Services (TCS) told SiliconRepublic.com that she accidentally found her way into a career in the cybersecurity sector. She said: “When I joined TCS as an automation engineer, I had no grand plan to build a career in cyber. I got the opportunity to work on a security-related product and quickly realised it combined many things I enjoyed: technology, problem-solving, continuous learning and working with diverse teams.” In time, the work became more interesting, she gained more exposure and the role often brought unique challenges. “Before long, I moved into dedicated cybersecurity roles,” she said. “Looking back, it is one of the happiest career accidents I’ve had. One thing I’ve learned since then is that not everyone follows a perfectly planned path into cybersecurity. “Many of the best professionals I know arrived through unexpected routes and that keeps the industry interesting.” Cybersecurity has moved from a specialist IT function to a board-level priority. When I started, conversations focused on technical controls and compliance. Today, cyber risk is recognised as a business risk that can impact operations, reputation, customer trust and overall business resilience. In the past, cybersecurity was seen as an add-on with professionals reluctant to embrace new digital initiatives. Today, our job is to help the business evolve quickly and safely. When security and governance is built into cloud and AI projects from day one, organisations innovate with confidence rather than constantly stopping to test vulnerabilities later. The threat landscape has also become far more sophisticated over the course of my career. Organisations are managing increasingly complex environments including cloud adoption, digital transformation programmes and now the rapid emergence of AI. At the same time, the role of cybersecurity professionals has evolved. Technical expertise remains essential, but teams at TCS and across the industry need people who can communicate risk clearly, influence decision-making and work across different business units. Demand outpaces supply in specialist areas like threat hunting and incident management. However, I think the conversation has moved beyond simply talking about a skills shortage generally. Many organisations need experienced professionals with broad skillsets, while also needing clearer pathways for early-career talent. Misconceptions about the work create barriers too. Cybersecurity is still not fully understood by many students and career changers. People often assume it is a highly technical profession where every role requires deep coding knowledge. In reality, cybersecurity offers opportunities across risk management, governance, engineering, architecture, threat intelligence, digital forensics and many other disciplines. We need to challenge traditional hiring norms. Curiosity and willingness to learn determine success in cybersecurity far more than an individual’s academic background. We should not assume talent only comes from computer science degrees. Some of the best cybersecurity professionals I work with at TCS started in linguistics, business operations, or non-technical roles. They succeed because they bring strong problem-solving abilities and a genuine interest in how technology and risk intersect. Cybersecurity encompasses a wide variety of roles and our hiring needs to reflect that. To fix the talent bottleneck, the industry needs accessible entry points, expanded graduate schemes, apprenticeships and structured retraining programmes to support career changers. Retention isn’t an issue for us currently, but this is a competitive industry so retention requires constant attention. Cybersecurity is a fast-moving profession and security professionals want clear career pathways, continuous learning, and exposure to new technologies, industries, and experiences. Keeping talent comes down to offering opportunities across projects, clear progression and structured mentorship. From my experience, people stay when they see how their work contributes to a wider purpose. People join organisations for an opportunity, but they stay because they believe they can continue to grow. Future talent pipelines depend on adaptability. The skills we need in five or 10 years will differ from those we use today. Therefore, leaders need to develop people who can comfortably move across disciplines. Someone might start in a security operations centre and later transition into cloud security, architecture, governance, or advisory roles. AI is changing how cybersecurity teams operate by assisting with threat detection, security monitoring, incident response and data analysis. This efficiency enables teams to focus on higher-value, complex tasks. AI will not replace security teams, but it shifts the required skills. Demand is growing for specialists who understand AI governance, AI security, responsible adoption and risk management. With continued advancement, many roles can be supported by AI, but it does not negate the need for strong human technical talent. We see AI as a catalyst for new opportunities, and we are committed to building a future-ready workforce by developing AI-native talent. Some of the most valuable learning in cybersecurity comes from cross-sector collaboration. I work across multiple sectors myself and I often see organisations faced with similar challenges. The reality is organisations in financial services, healthcare, critical infrastructure and government all face similar threat patterns and operational hurdles. There is significant value in sharing knowledge, whether that is experiences or lessons learned. Stronger collaboration creates opportunities for professionals to learn from different environments, build broader networks and gain exposure to new ideas and approaches. Ireland has a strong cybersecurity community and continued collaboration between industry, academia and government will be important to build resilience across the sector and develop strong talent. Stay curious. Technology and threats change constantly and the professionals who thrive are those who never stop learning. Avoid narrowing your focus too early. Explore different areas of cybersecurity, gain exposure to multiple industries and focus on understanding the business context behind the technology. Build your network early, seek out mentors and engage with the wider cybersecurity community. Some of my best opportunities have come through collaboration and industry relationships. Strong networks and continuous learning build a durable career in cybersecurity. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. DoorDash reportedly close to buying Instacart at $17bn equity value

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
In July, Instacart bought Israeli AI start-up Arpalus that provides real-time visibility into supermarket shelves. DoorDash is in advanced talks to buy Instacart in a deal that could value the grocery-delivery platform at around $17bn, sources have told news publications. The deal could materialise as soon as tomorrow (28 October), reports further suggested. Last week, The Information reported that Instacart was looking to be bought up by DoorDash and Uber. The deal with Uber has, however, reportedly fallen through. Instacart shares jumped nearly 8pc at market close yesterday (26 October), valuing the company at $14.4bn, while DoorDash dropped more than 6pc to nearly $73bn. The deal comes as delivery platforms look to expand their business from just food to groceries and other retail products. Last year, DoorDash entered Ireland after acquiring its UK rival Deliveroo for £2.9bn. It entered Europe in 2021 after buying Finnish food and grocery delivery start-up Wolt, its first acquisition in the region. Instacart shares have risen more than 50pc over the past year. The company, in its latest quarterly earnings report, disclosed total revenue at more than $1bn – up 14pc year-over-year. In July, it acquired Israeli AI start-up Arpalus that provides real-time visibility into supermarket shelves. Chair and CEO Chris Rogers said that the acquisition, combined with Instacart’s approximately 600,000 shoppers, would allow the company to “drive additional fulfilment efficiency, enable more relevant AI-powered shopping experiences, and further strengthen our inventory intelligence”. Uber, popularly known for its ride-hailing service, meanwhile bought up Delivery Hero at an equity value of €13bn earlier this year, promising to leave the company’s Berlin headquarters and workforce as is until at least 2029. The company also promised to use “commercially reasonable efforts” to invest €2bn in Germany through 2031, with a focus on developing its local corporate workforce and launching autonomous driving services in the country. Late-stage funding average more than doubles in Europe in 2026
2026-10-27

Late-stage funding average more than doubles in Europe in 2026

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
The number of European unicorns is also rising, according to new research. Data intelligence platform Tracxn Technologies has published its latest ‘Europe Tech 9m’ report, which explores funding, investors, exits, unicorns and city-level activity across Europe’s technology ecosystem for the first nine months of 2026. Report figures show that between 1 January and 30 September, late-stage deals accounted for 53pc of all funding. The average late-stage deal more than doubled year-on-year from around $68m in 2025 to roughly $153m in 2026. Mistral, a Paris-based AI company, was among the organisations that made the largest deals, with its $3.5bn Series D round in September representing around 11pc of all late-stage funding – which the report defined as Series C and later, private equity and pre-IPO rounds – in Europe across the first nine months of 2026. Other large rounds included Helsing, a German defence technology company that raised $1.8bn in a Series E round in July, and German robotics maker Neura, which raised $1.4bn in a Series C round during March. Another key finding from the report is that Europe’s production rate of ‘unicorns’ – companies valued at $1bn or more – has risen. European technology companies included 41 new unicorns during the documented period this year, which is up from 16 in the first nine months of 2025. According to Tracxn, this is representative of around 21pc of the 200 new unicorns worldwide this year. The city of London accounted for 15 of the 41. Broken down geographically by city, London was responsible for raising $21.6bn between January and September, which was up from $10.2bn in 2025. Paris ranked second in Europe, raising $7.4bn in 2026 against $4.1bn in the same period last year. Stockholm raised $4.1bn against $1.1bn, and Munich followed with $3.4bn against $1.8bn. Berlin accounted for $2bn, against $1.7bn in 2025. Across the report, the type of organisations raising large sums of money included AI companies, which raised the most of any grouping ($15.7bn); high performance computing organisations ($7.7bn); military technology companies ($5.8bn); data centre infrastructure groups ($5.7bn); and companies operating in the drones sector ($2.7bn). Silicon Republic
What in-house legal leaders really think about AI, in their own words
2026-10-27

What in-house legal leaders really think about AI, in their own words

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
From left: Carlo Salizzo, Dentons; David Kirton, Dentons; and Ann O'Dea, Silicon Republic at a report preview event on 22 October. Image: Dentons Multiple surveys count how many lawyers are using AI. A new report set out to ask what happens to their workload when they do. AI isn’t replacing in-house lawyers, but the capacity it frees up is being absorbed by new work, much of it drafted by AI itself. That is just one of the pictures to emerge from interviews with legal and compliance leaders at companies that included Eir, Flipdish, Gilead Sciences, IMRO, Mastercard, Uniphar and Wayflyer for a new report, ‘From AI Adoption to AI Advantage’, produced by Dentons Ireland and SiliconRepublic.com. Rather than creating another survey full of statistics, we set out to ask what responsible and effective use actually looks like inside a legal function today, and the results often belied the received wisdom. Almost nobody we interviewed reported AI replacing a role or a function outright. Where it is paying off, it is mostly in repetitive work such as invoice review, contract checking and customer screening, rather than in the research, drafting and judgement calls at the heart of the job. Sergey Kinchin, senior counsel at Uniphar, said his own thinking had changed completely in a year. “Last year I would have thought it would probably reduce headcount. Now I’m seeing it more as unlocking unmet demand,” he said. “I think the complexity of the work that’s left after AI will increase. The bottom might shift up, but the overall pyramid of work will increase and grow.” It was a sentiment echoed by many of our interviewees. Máire Neary, who was VP of people and legal at Flipdish when she was interviewed, described treating AI “as a junior colleague, something that’s additive, that can challenge me, but you have to remember you’re the lawyer in charge at the end of the day”. The catch is that the same tools are now in everyone else’s hands. Producing a long, polished complaint, subject access request or grievance is close to cost-free today, and the report says AI-assisted drafting now seems to touch the majority of incoming disputes, subject access requests, grievances and customer complaints. Sorcha Nic Mhurchú, general counsel at Eir, said the cost lies less in reading that material than in sorting through it. “You end up going back and having to figure out, line by line, what is relevant,” she said. “So that adds a lot of volume and time to any response, and that’s a reality for everybody, not just us.” At IMRO, chief commercial officer Seán Donegan is glad he saw it coming. “Two years ago, I sat down with our licensing team and I said, guys, I’m going to make a prediction now,” he said. “The volume and tenacity of queries and complaints is going to increase exponentially with these AI tools.” And it’s not just incoming correspondence. Uniphar’s Kinchin described a call with a part of the business that had gone all-in on AI. “We joined a call and we had five people bring six AI-generated opinions to the call, on a question that touched a regulatory issue,” he said. “In trying to reconcile it, the AI had essentially confused itself.” Deirdre McCarthy, chief legal and people officer at Wayflyer, added the issue is quality as well as quantity. “AI can produce so much content that sometimes looks good, but may not always be good,” she said. “So you can end up with people spending a lot of time just trading red lines because they’re putting it into their AI tool.” Many of the interviewees remarked that this makes legal expertise all the more valuable. “In a world increasingly full of AI-generated legal content, genuine human judgement may become more valuable rather than less,” said Carlo Salizzo, head of technology and digital regulation at Dentons Ireland, speaking at a preview of the report last week. What AI costs is easier to count than what it delivers. Flipdish’s Neary was clear about the risk of experimenting for its own sake. “I think we’ve seen a lot of AI for AI’s sake, but every lawyer, every operator, needs to ask what the return on investment is,” she said. “It’s great if your team is enjoying building and experimenting and having fun, but what have you got to show for it, particularly once you’re looking at a high LLM [large language model] bill, or the cost of maintaining it down the line?” None of the interviewees were confident they could demonstrate a return beyond obvious gains such as efficiency and risk reduction. Rebekah Dobson, head of international legal operations at Gilead Sciences, said the premise of the question itself is flawed. “No one has proved ROI on AI as of yet because companies were not tracking the metrics prior to AI to have a starting point for setting KPIs and measuring impact of AI,” she said. “I did read an interesting article the other day that said ‘you don’t make Excel prove its ROI, it’s just there’, and I think that’s a really valuable comparison.” Legal teams have a particular problem here, according to David Kirton, partner, technology and digital regulation at Dentons Ireland. “We tend to record the work lawyers do. We are much less good at recording the work they no longer have to do,” he said. Cost, meanwhile, has so far only moved one way. As AI providers shift from flat licence or per-seat fees to consumption-based, token-metered pricing, bills become much harder to forecast. Nic Mhurchú said Eir feared being on a treadmill. “The licence fees and the token fees are so huge that we fear that if we start adopting and using and buying licences, we’re on a treadmill of just paying more fees, because these companies have to justify the high investment that they’ve been putting into these tools,” she said. Jessica Williams, vice-president of legal tech strategy at Mastercard, says she wants to see vendors offer more solutions and controls. “Budgets aren’t infinite and I’m going to have three or four people who are AI geniuses and they’re going to burn through tokens. “Then I’m going to have a bunch of other people who just do one or two things here and there … How do I balance all of that and create a predictable budget line for the company?” Cornelius Grossmann, Dentons’ global head of solutions development, expects AI costs to rise two- to fivefold within two years and warned of a coming ‘cost cliff’. “I think the whole token economics will mean we’ll probably hire people to monitor AI cost in a big law firm,” he said. “And others may need to do that too, because it could bankrupt you if you don’t pay attention.” Wayflyer’s McCarthy expects token spend to become the next big management question. “I think this is the next interesting wave, what we spend on AI and who gets to spend what, based on the tasks at hand?” she said. It was a sentiment echoed by Dentons’ Kirton. “Who gets access? Which tasks justify the cost? How do you allocate consumption across a business? And how do you prevent successful adoption from producing an unexpectedly large technology bill?” he asked, adding that many legal leaders are still working out how to manage that exposure without throttling hard-won AI adoption. Overall, interviewees described something more demanding of both sides of the client and law firm relationship: a renegotiation of who does what, who is trained for what and who pays for what, conducted in real time while the technology changes apace. “It is probably fair to say that the legal function has not been transformed by technology to quite the same extent as areas such as finance, sales or procurement,” said Dentons’ Salizzo. “AI may be the catalyst that changes that.” The full report is now live and can be downloaded here. Net loss is at $80.7bn, largely due to accounting adjustments related to financing agreements. Among several multibillion-dollar commitments, the company has agreed to spend at least $45bn with Microsoft between November 2026 and May 2033. Earlier this month, Anthropic also said that it will assign at least 5pc of its shares to a funding entity dedicated to making donations in aid of “the public good”. Solar Panels Are Now Producing So Much Power That They’re Leaving Nuclear Plants in the Dust

Solar Panels Are Now Producing So Much Power That They’re Leaving Nuclear Plants in the Dust

Futurism | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
As the world struggles to access oil as a consequence of the US war on Iran, the scramble for alternative forms of energy has become a mad dash. And contrary to the energy future some have imagined, the hunt for new power sources has supercharged the solar trade — leaving even nuclear power way back in the rearview mirror. According to new reporting by the BBC, photovoltaics will account for 10 percent of the world’s power generation by the end of 2026, a threshold which will see solar energy officially surpass nuclear fission. As professor of energy and climate policy at the University of Oxford Jan Rosenow told the British broadcaster, “solar is now the engine of the power transition.” Compared to nuclear energy generation — which has stayed largely flat over the past 26 years globally — the publication notes the volume of solar energy being generated has exploded, increasing some 2,700 times over across the same period. Much of the solar energy boom can be traced back to China, where massive supply is driving down the cost of photovoltaics to unprecedented lows. Though plenty of US-based startups have emerged with the goal of reviving the nuclear energy sector, their efforts to commercialize fission seem to be going nowhere fast. In fact, the cost of nuclear construction projects in the US have skyrocketed over recent decades, defying conventional norms around infrastructure, which hold that complex energy builds should get cheaper as scale increases. For the rest of the world, cheap solar power is increasingly becoming a lifeline, both in the literal day-to-day sense, and as a way to decouple from the long-standing oil economy. As Rosenow told the BBC, the issue going forward is no longer one of access to alternative energy, but in balanced development of infrastructure for underdeveloped and overexploited nations. “The bigger question,” Rosenow says, “is no longer whether solar grows, but whether our power systems and institutions can keep up.” More on energy: Decrepit Oscar Mayer Hot Dog Factory Is Getting a Major Fusion Reactor Facelift

Microsoft has similarly gone after vibe-coded reverse-engineering efforts. That’s despite continuously pushing for the in-house use of AI, with its subsidiary Activision using AI-generated slop in the artwork for its latest Call of Duty games. Microsoft’s own director of applied science Brent Hecht even privately called AI the “largest theft of labor in human history,” according to recently surfaced documents in the New York Times’ ongoing legal battle against OpenAI and Microsoft. Another internal document warned that generative AI could “significantly disrupt the employment of the very people who generated the data on which the foundation model was trained.” In short, it sure seems like both Epic Games and Microsoft are trying to have their cake and eat it. They’re actively trying to set rules for others they aren’t beholden to themselves — an infuriating hypocrisy that perfectly exemplifies the current state of the industry and the tech sector as a whole. Still, the scale of money being poured into AI and AI-related industries — with AI stocks taking up some 42 percent of China’s market capitalization, according to Goldman Sachs  — isn’t too dissimilar to what’s happening in Western economies, where the obvious lack of a return of investment becomes harder to ignore. Anthropic’s recent disclosure in its IPO filing of a staggering $80 billion loss — and plans to spend another $1 trillion on data centers over the next decade — will only further fuel fears that even the industry’s top players are still far from being profitable. According to Reuters, state-backed investors sold into market rallies, or periods of rapid gains, to keep those gains in check, as part of a push by the government to dampen volatility. The Duo is a major departure from years of Apple playing it safe, with mostly iterative improvements to each of the iPhone’s yearly releases that have increasingly failed to convince users to ditch their still-capable devices in favor of a new one. On top of it all are soaring consumer electronics prices triggered by the AI gold rush. Of course, chances are Apple will have an opportunity to iron out these issues for the next generation of the Duo. But that won’t exactly be reassuring to early adopters, who took a chance on the extremely expensive first-generation device. TechCrunch
Frontier AI labs say their models refuted the Exponential Time Hypothesis. What does that mean?
2026-10-27

Frontier AI labs say their models refuted the Exponential Time Hypothesis. What does that mean?

TechCrunch | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. OpenAI released solutions to three blockbuster math problems that have long vexed researchers yesterday. One was a surprise rejection of the Exponential Time Hypothesis (ETH), a durable assumption in computer science that plays a large role in how we understand what is difficult to compute. As with previous proof-drops from the frontier lab, scientific communities responded with a mixture of thrill and dismay. Anthropic mathematician Levent Alpöge suggested that OpenAI was basing its solutions from pre-existing work, and hours later, Anthropic subsequently released its own refutation of ETH. However, OpenAI’s process is evolving. This time, the lab structured its releases differently for each of the three disciplines involved: Mathematicians got the work dropped on GitHub; the computer scientists formed a working group, asked for the proofs to be shared publicly and OpenAI to fund a conference to discuss them; the physicists formed a working group that will determine how their result will be released. MIT Professor Ryan Williams is the computer scientist who is organizing his field’s response to the new finding. Williams, whose work focuses on these questions of computational complexity, has criticized OpenAI’s approach in the past, calling its proof dump earlier this October a “DDOS attack on mathematicians.” However, he agreed to collaborate with the frontier labs in order to help shepherd ideas that are vital to the field, he told TechCrunch. The lab asked him to help interpret the solution, along with UC San Diego computer scientists Russell Impagliazzo and Ramamohan Paturi, who first published the ETH in 1999. Their first step was to release an open letter calling for feedback from scientists interested in these problems and a discussion of the norms around AI contributions to their work. “Some parts [of OpenAI’s proof] are, maybe you could say routine, but then yes, there are two or three alien-looking parts, and it’s exactly these parts that that I saw that made me think, wow, this is something that the whole community needs to work on together,” Williams told TechCrunch. The ETH is part of a broader class of problems known as P vs NP, which has animated computer science and many other fields for decades. Reductively, researchers are trying to understand if any problem whose solution can be easily verified (NP) can also be easily solved (P). A simple example is the game Sodoku: Once a solution to a puzzle is in place, it can be quickly verified, but finding the solution is time-consuming. That dynamic is true across a range of important problems, and the insight underpins cryptographic systems: When you have the key to an encoded message, you can reveal it, but actually cracking an effective code takes too long to be useful. However, scientists have yet to prove whether that is always true. “P = NP is saying that, not only can you verify Sudoku solutions when they exist, you can just generate them,” Williams said. “P = NP is asserting that this is true for every single problem you can verify quickly — if you can verify it quickly, you can solve it quickly. So this just seems too good to be true.” That’s why most scientists assume that P ≠ NP. ETH provided evidence for that. It basically says that even the fastest computer algorithm needs exponentially more time to find a solution as the number of variables grow in a specific class of tricky logic puzzle. The claims from OpenAI and Anthropic is that their models have come up with algorithms that solve those puzzles in “sub-exponential” time, meaning faster than the speeds previously thought possible. If true, they may have proven the EFT to be false — refuted it, in scientific terms — and maybe opened up a path for algorithms that generally perform P = NP. “Every computer scientist should probably put a little more weight on the plausibility of P = NP, but one’s weight may vary,” Williams said. “I do think that it kind of calls into question a lot of the intuitions that we thought we had about … how tractable [hard NP problems] are. I also think that this is probably not the final answer either. I don’t think that OpenAI’s algorithm could possibly be the best algorithm you can get.” The durability of ETH has lead to it being included in many scientific papers over the past decade that will need to be revisited if the AI labs claims that they’ve proven it wrong holds up, Williams said. “This is partly why I was asking or pushing OpenAI to support a conference on this — it’s going to affect many researchers’ works, and we need to figure out, okay, what happens next besides understanding the algorithms. We need to understand, how should the foundations change compared to what we had before?” he said.Before scientific concerns, there are cybersecurity questions. Williams said that an improved algorithm could imply that cryptographic systems relying on certain key sizes could be considered broken. So one of the first questions he asked OpenAI science lead Dan Roberts “was if they had checked that this affects any crypto systems … is anything insecure now? And apparently, they checked, and they were reasonably confident that this didn’t … imply any breaks.” This new AI paradigm of scientific discovery is still a work in progress, but Williams says he is optimistic despite real concerns, particularly about training the next generation of scientists. Being a computer scientist who does high level math has perhaps made the adjustment easier for him than for pure mathematicians. Still, he says AI is “forcing people to be a little more ambitious about the kind of work that they do, and to think more deeply about, okay, if all the things I used to work on are now low hanging fruit for AI, how can I raise the bar for the kind of scientific questions I want to ask?” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Google’s Genie 4 world model adds audio, more realistic worlds, and real-time steering

Google’s Genie 4 world model adds audio, more realistic worlds, and real-time steering

TechCrunch | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Google DeepMind released its newest world model, Genie 4, in research preview on Tuesday. The latest model generates the most photorealistic and interactive worlds yet, allowing users to type prompts mid-simulation to change the scene. The model also generates real-time audio that matches the simulated world, including music, ambient noises, and interactive dialogue. The release of Genie 4 comes a couple of months after AI video startup Runway released its second world model, GWM Worlds 2, a playable model that responds dynamically to user inputs and includes audio generation. At the same time, the world model industry is beginning to consolidate. Fei-Fei Li’s World Labs is being acquired by AMD, Amazon is in talks to buy Decart, and Rivian just acquired Third Dimension to bring simulation capabilities in-house for self-driving testing. Genie 4 is Google’s latest move to stay ahead in the crowded field. Google released its predecessor, Genie 3, as a research preview a little over a year ago. Aside from being far more photorealistic and featuring audio, Genie 4 allows for freeform edits, like changing the weather or camera angle in real time or adding new people or objects. It can maintain a consistent scene for up to two minutes, double its predecessor’s limit, and offers better physics and more human-like characters. Gemini, Google’s AI model, is also more closely integrated with Genie 4, acting as the director that plans steps and checks frames while Genie creates the visuals. In a research experiment tied to Gemini Robotics, a Gemini 4 Argon model broke down tasks into steps that Genie 4 simulated in real time. The experiment shows a possible way to test robot scenarios in simulation before they happen in the real world. “What we’ll increasingly see in the future are these hybrid world models, where you have a combination of Gemini writing code and text, and Genie ingesting that in real time and simulating reality at different levels of abstraction,” Tejas Kulkarni, research director at Google, said during a briefing. As with other world models, the pitched commercial use cases include gaming, interactive learning, and robotics. One speculative example of interactive learning would be entering a painting of a historical scene and chatting to the painting’s subjects about the event portrayed. But “trusted testers” — the people who get first access to Google cutting-edge products — are pointing to a possible new use: prototyping films. “The real time aspect becomes most valuable in the areas where keeping a state of flow in the creativity space becomes incredibly useful,” Diego Rivas, a Google DeepMind engineer, said during a press briefing. “For example, in the ideation phase, in the storyboarding phase — anytime you’re kind of in need of rapid ideation.” Google also offers Veo, its AI video generation model that makes high-definition clips with built-in synchronized audio from text, image, or video prompts — a tool that has been growing in popularity among filmmakers. Rivas added that users could, eventually, rely on Gemini Omni, a multimodal large language model that can generate video from images, audio, and text, to turn those prototypes into creative assets. Though those capabilities, he says, are still a ways off. Throughout the briefing, Rivas stressed that it’s still early days for Genie 4, and that it’s not yet clear what the end-to-end product experience will be. The team also acknowledged that Genie 4 still has gaps: details and objects can drift the longer a simulation runs, and they still haven’t quite figured out how to ensure the audio will be accurate and any in-world text will be legible. Genie 4 is available now as a research preview for trusted testers. In the coming weeks, the model will roll out to Google AI Ultra subscribers in Project Genie, Google’s AI tool for generating interactive worlds from text or image prompts, and early access partners of the Gemini API and Google AI Studio. Meta AI director Rohit Patel leaves to take on Microsoft Office and Google Workspace
2026-10-27

Meta AI director Rohit Patel leaves to take on Microsoft Office and Google Workspace

TechCrunch | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. After eight years developing AI for Meta, Rohit Patel is departing the company, TechCrunch has exclusively learned. The former director at Meta Superintelligence Labs has launched Ndoc, a startup he’s pitching as an AI-native replacement for Microsoft Office suite or Google Workspace. Patel is the latest in a handful of high-profile departures from Meta following the exits of Chief Global Affairs Officer Joel Kaplan, AI researcher Andrew Tulloch, and Head of Product for AI-for-Work Emily Dalton Smith. Patel designed Ndoc to be more than just another AI tool layered onto an existing workflow. The goal is to replace the fragmented way people work across tools like Excel, Word, PowerPoint, Tableau, Replit, and “ChatGPT copy-paste” with one unified workspace that all colleagues can access, even if they’re not signed up for Ndoc. Ndoc brings three components together in one layout. The Stage tab is where presentations of key findings live, like slide decks, dashboards, simulations, or mini apps. The Narrative tab features analyses, numbered citations, generated figures, and generally the “why” behind the numbers. The Model tab is where underlying datasets and Python scripts are housed. The design reflects Patel’s background. During his tenure at Meta, Patel focused on reinforcement learning — a type of machine learning in which AI agents learn to make decisions through trial and error and maximize rewards — and evaluating whether AI output is trustworthy. Patel believes that if AI is going to build the spreadsheets and slide decks that companies use to make decisions, someone should be able to check its work. For instance, users can find the Python scripts used to create the documents. “Even if you don’t know any Python, you can ask [the AI chat] to explain, ‘Hey, what does this script do? Which line has my formula?’ And you can verify that formula,” Patel said. “There’s nothing hidden behind the curtain here.” In practice, users can upload a dataset and ask Ndoc to perform an analysis and write a narrative about the findings. They can also add document-specific context, including audience, tone, and memory. “It maintains a memory across your person, that particular document, your team, and your company,” Patel said. Patel also says Ndoc is built and fine-tuned using a mixture of open source models. Because the platform was developed from scratch, he says it costs considerably less to run than alternatives built on frontier AI models. “I thought that’s what people would be excited about, but people don’t care about that,” he said, referring to his early trial users. “What people are excited about is that they love seeing all their end-to-end in one place.” Patel’s larger ambition is to reinvent the file itself. He says documents can be exported as a single “.ndoc.html” file that can be opened in any browser, a format he plans to open-source so other developers can build apps that work with it without being locked into Ndoc the way so many have been locked into Microsoft or Google for years. His desire to open-source this project was partly why he left Meta and decided to go it alone. At a big company like Meta, getting everyone to agree on an open source format would undoubtedly require the input of “hundreds of people and opinions on how the format should look.” Patel wanted to move quickly. “Humans can only move at human pace, whereas…I can move at the pace of what AI can do.” Trying to become the platform where enterprise work happens is a huge bet for a founder with a team of three and no funding, especially when other AI labs are trying to own the same space. OpenAI, Anthropic, Meta, SpaceXAI, and Google have all come out with enterprise AI tools that allow users to connect to Google Workspace, Microsoft 365, and other workplace apps so their AI models can work directly in those tools. And last month, OpenAI launched Space, a shared workspace inside ChatGPT that’s a more direct competitor to those established tools. Patel acknowledges that Space could be a threat, but says the labs largely have the wrong idea. “Companies are basically attaching AI to spreadsheets and it fills your cells for you,” he said. “It just sort of kills my soul. This is like if you just figured out electricity, and then you decided the best use case is to put lights on my bulls that till my soil. No. Get a tractor.” There’s a reason the AI labs have tried to bring their models to where people are already working, though. It’s hard to change habits and replace entire workflows. Patel says he understands that, which is why he built connectors so customers can import existing data from the tools they already use. “My bar was that I’m not going to release anything I don’t love using,” he said. “I have very optimized AI workflows for everything I do. If I start using this tool, and I keep going back to my workflows, I haven’t built a good enough tool…I have a graveyard of tools that did not meet that bar.” Patel has a similar bar for gaining customer traction before he tries to raise funds. “I didn’t want to take money until I had some proof that people love the product,” he said. As of Tuesday, Ndoc is conducting an enterprise pilot and opening an alpha version to a limited number of customers who join the wait list. It’s free to sign up, but customers will pay for usage and tokens. He hopes to release a beta version in the coming weeks, with a full launch planned for next year. For Patel, moving as fast as possible is more important than having the perfect product from the get-go. “Execution is extremely cheap…so go execute on a suboptimal plan and worry about it later,” he said. “By the time you make the best plan, the execution will be done by somebody else.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Ford Fathom will be the cheapest American EV capable of powering a house
2026-10-27

Ford Fathom will be the cheapest American EV capable of powering a house

TechCrunch | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Ford announced two new EV chargers today that will allow its forthcoming Fathom pickup truck to serve as a battery capable of delivering power to the home or grid. The new chargers promise to be about half the cost of Ford’s existing equivalent products, which could make the technology available to more households. Ford said the Fathom itself will start at $28,350. The Fathom will be capable of delivering “over 10 kW” to a home, Ford said. That would allow the electric vehicle to substitute for a small standby generator. For most U.S. homes, it wouldn’t be enough to power everything, but it could keep a refrigerator and air conditioner operational for about half a day. Such capability has been touted as an advantage EVs have over fossil fuel vehicles, but not many EVs come properly equipped, and the chargers required to make use of vehicle-to-everything (V2X) services tend to be costly. Ford’s two devices offer similar but differing capabilities. The Home Power Station device can help customers shave their utility bill by charging the Fathom when power prices are low and sending electricity back to the home when prices are high. The automaker says it will cost around $650, about 50% less than its first-generation bidirectional charger. The other option, Home Backup Power, is a package that bundles the Home Power Station with a second device, the Home Backup Panel, to allow the Fathom to also power parts or all of a home when the power goes out. Ford says the package will cost around $2,100, about 60% less than its first-generation product. None of those prices include installation, which for existing V2X chargers can run up to $10,000, depending on how much electrical work needs to be done to prepare the home. Ford did not announce any partnerships with installers, though a spokesperson told TechCrunch that Ford would release more details at a later date. Which loads the Fathom can power will vary by household and the specifics of each installation. Things like air conditioners and refrigerators, for example, typically require a large surge of power to start up, after which point their load drops. So-called soft-start devices can help mitigate the surge, though they add cost to the installation. Both chargers will allow Fathom customers to participate in virtual power plants (VPPs), which use software to control a fleet of distributed energy storage devices, such as EVs. As strain on the grid grows due to AI data centers, utilities have been exploring whether VPPs can help alleviate congestion at the edges. Fathom owners who enroll and keep their truck plugged in can sell electricity back to the grid, often at a premium. Ford did not say how much Fathom owners can stand to make, but the payouts can be upwards of $1,000 per year, depending on the grid’s needs and the level of participation. Automakers, including Ford, have been diving deep into energy storage as demand for such services surges. The Fathom-powered VPP is Ford’s latest foray into the market, but it’s unlikely to be its last. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. US government says hackers are now exploiting critical PHP web bugs
2026-10-27

US government says hackers are now exploiting critical PHP web bugs

TechCrunch | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. U.S. cybersecurity agency CISA has confirmed that hackers are now actively exploiting a pair of critical security vulnerabilities in the widely used PHP web software and has ordered civilian federal agencies within the U.S. government to fix their systems within the next three days. The urgent new warning to patch comes less than a week after the makers of the decades-old PHP web programming language rolled out patches for the two bugs, which allow hackers to remotely break into a vulnerable system over the internet without the need for passwords. The PHP software is reportedly used by some 70% of web-facing servers, putting major websites and applications at risk. The severity of the bugs prompted some governments and big companies to take their sites offline to fix them. While the bugs are rated critical, meaning they can be highly damaging if exploited, security researchers said that developing a working exploit to hack into vulnerable systems was initially challenging — but not impossible. Confirmed in-the-wild exploitation now puts extra pressure on organizations to patch their systems to prevent malicious compromises, disruption, and data breaches. CISA did not provide details about who was exploiting the bugs in its Monday statement, but its statement comes soon after security researchers said they found evidence that the PHP bugs were being exploited. The bugs, known as PHP2Shell, exploit vulnerabilities in the software that went undiscovered for years. The bugs were initially uncovered two years ago but the nonprofit foundation that maintains the software said there was no practical way for hackers to exploit them, leaving the bugs unpatched. That was until earlier this year, when researchers at security research firm Calif uncovered a way to exploit the two dormant vulnerabilities with the assistance of an AI model. The two bugs in PHP are among countless vulnerabilities that have not yet been discovered or have been deprioritized for years but now pose a risk due to advances in AI models’ ability to find and exploit vulnerabilities, including by chaining multiple flaws together. PHP is one of many aging web technologies that help keep the internet and modern technologies running, but that have been under-resourced and under-funded for years, making it difficult for open source projects like PHP to pay for security reviews that might have uncovered flaws sooner.
