We Finally Know Why Trump Has Tried So Hard to Hide the Epstein Files
2026-10-27

We Finally Know Why Trump Has Tried So Hard to Hide the Epstein Files

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. One of the biggest mysteries of Donald Trump’s second term in office is why, exactly, he is so touchy about his relationship with Jeffrey Epstein, who is now dead but whose crimes as a child sex predator and shady financier are still unraveling. You’d think the MAGA base would be eager to get to the bottom of this: This slice of the GOP has long been obsessed with pedophiles, mingling with the QAnon movement during Trump’s first term in conspiracy-mongering about an outlandish (and untrue) Democratic pedophile ring run out of a D.C. pizza restaurant, with children being sex-trafficked in Wayfair cabinets. In the lead-up to Trump’s second term, they latched onto a case with actual evidence to back it up: Epstein, and the scores of girls he was accused of grooming, assaulting, and trafficking to the men in his orbit. Trump supporters rallied around a demand to release the Epstein files, millions of documents held by the FBI that Epstein watchers suspected may not only further incriminate him but many other powerful people as well. Members of the Trump administration pledged full transparency and the full release of the files; meanwhile Trump himself said he had no role here and that the files totally exonerated him from any wrongdoing. And yet the full files remain unreleased, hidden from public view by the Trump administration’s own FBI and Department of Justice as rumors swirled. Now we understand why Trump didn’t want them released. Just a week before the midterm elections that are already shaping up to be a bloodbath for the GOP, Democrats have dropped a bombshell. “A growing body of evidence demonstrates that Donald Trump was an active participant in Jeffrey Epstein’s sexual abuse of young women during the mid-to-late 1990s,” a just-released report from the Democrats of the House Judiciary Committee states. Additionally, they claim that Trump has leveraged his FBI and DOJ to cover up the evidence, and have now called for the appointment of a special counsel to investigate any potential wrongdoing. The report is stunning. While many of the accusations were previously publicized, the stories have been a drip-drip of allegations; putting them all together in a single report has the effect of connecting a network of abusers, giving a reader a bird’s-eye view of how predatory men allegedly worked together to traffic and assault women and girls. And at the heart of the Democrats’ argument is corruption. After all, this same administration has turned self-dealing into an art form, and has enriched the president to the tune of billions of dollars, all while engaging in a staggering cover-up to protect powerful men who abuse vulnerable girls. And one of those powerful men, they say, was the president himself. Five women, four of them named and one anonymous, claim they were victimized by Epstein and that Trump also raped or sexually assaulted them. Part of the scheme allegedly revolved around Elite Model Management, run by John Casablancas, who was a friend of both Epstein’s and Trump’s. Many of the models recruited by Elite as underage girls say that they were raped or otherwise assaulted by Casablancas and others, and were also given to Epstein, who sexually assaulted them as well. Casablancas partnered with Trump to create Elite’s Look of the Year competition; the competitors were girls aged 14 to 19. According to the report, Casablancas met his third wife at Trump’s Look of the Year competition—a girl who was just 16, though he was in his 50s. The models recruited by Casablancas were trotted out at various events and parties with Trump and often Epstein, including at Mar-a-Lago. A second man, Paolo Zampolli, also worked as a modeling agent who allegedly recruited women and girls for Epstein; he was close friend with Trump and even served in the Trump administration as special envoy for global partnerships until earlier this year. He is also the agent who recruited Slovenian model Melania Knauss, helping with her housing and immigration status; he claims he’s the one who introduced her to her future husband. Some others dispute this, claiming Epstein was the one who made the introduction (the Trumps deny this). Just as damning as the allegations of sexual assault are those of a cover-up. In one startling interview between Attorney General Todd Blanche (who formerly served as Trump’s personal attorney and defense lawyer before taking over the Justice Department) and Ghislaine Maxwell, Maxwell seems poised to discuss details of one woman’s accusations against Trump, until Blanche interrupts her, instructing her, “Let’s not talking about individuals” and repeating that he doesn’t want to “get into” a “he said, she said” or whatever it is she’s about to disclose. This behavior, the Democrats who wrote the report allege, “potentially constituted witness tampering and obstruction of an official proceeding—serious felonies.” The report also proffers evidence to refute Trump’s claim that he didn’t know Epstein well and had banished him from Mar-a-Lago because of his behavior toward girls and women. According to White House spokeswoman Lauren Bis, “None of these allegations are true. This ‘report’ is nothing more than a desperate attempt by crazed Democrats to distract Americans from the surging Republican momentum driven by President Trump’s historic achievements.” She added that “the President kicked Jeffrey Epstein out of his club at Mar-a-Lago because he was a creep.” Regardless of this denial, the demand for an appointment of a special counsel to investigate the Trump–Epstein ties and any possible cover-up is well overdue. Survivors deserve to see accountability. The public deserves the truth.  And the release of the report a week before the midterm is savvy politics. If there’s one issue that unites Americans from left to right, it’s that Epstein was a world-class scumbag, and his death unfortunate only insofar as it meant he never truly saw justice. I suspect most Americans would agree that anyone else who enabled Epstein or engaged in crimes with him should be named, shamed, and potentially prosecuted—no matter what side of the political aisle they sit on. But so far, the Trump administration has stymied efforts to do just that. That should be a scandal itself, but it turns out it might also be a much darker red flag about why the administration won’t investigate. Democrats are smart to point that out. If there really was nothing to see in the Epstein files, you’d think that the Trump administration would release them and call it a big win, especially at a moment when they’re down and out. Cost of living remains unaffordable for many Americans while the president who promised no more foreign wars is engaged in a spectacularly stupid conflict of choice with Iran, leaving American taxpayers footing the bill. It is objectively strange—and obviously suspicious—that the Trump administration isn’t using everything it can to distract from the unpopular reality it’s created for voters. The Epstein files remain one of the most captivating and conspiracy-fueling issues precisely because so much secrecy still surrounds them. And why? The man at the center of the criminal enterprise is dead. His second-in-command is in prison. Many prominent men have already been publicly embarrassed by their ties to Epstein. The continued withholding of the files and the smearing of anyone who demands their release, and the stonewalling of any investigation into why they have not been released, simply stinks. And it reinforces Americans’ cynicism with government and sense that everyone in power is inherently corrupt. Unfortunately, too many people see Trump as simply more of the same, rather than an aberrant figure who makes a mockery of American democracy and who must not just be ousted, but ostracized, and his party, co-conspirators, and enablers condemned. Part of the problem with the stories of Epstein, Trump, and the corruption all come down to the same thing: The bit-by-bit release of information has created a long record of abhorrent behavior, which means that the bar to shock is so high it’s almost impossible to clear. There is a kind of disgust fatigue at play: Yeah, we know Epstein was a disgusting sex predator, and that some of the men around him probably partook, too; what’s new? Those who are ideologically motivated to believe Trump at all costs are also probably motivated to believe him on Epstein, too; very little seems like it would change the minds of hardcore MAGA adherents. But persuading hardcore MAGA adherents isn’t the task at hand. Persuading the persuadable is; so is restoring dignity and accountability to the office of the presidency. Neither of those tasks are fully completed by finally, fully, and transparently investigating Epstein and the Trump administration’s treatment of the case. But it would be a good and necessary start. And if this administration refuses—and it seems primed to refuse the appointment of a special counsel—voters should let the administration know what they think about that at the polls next week. The Democrats Need a Daddy. I Know Exactly Who It Should Be.
2026-10-27

The Democrats Need a Daddy. I Know Exactly Who It Should Be.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Finally, a Democrat who actually knows how to have sex. Earlier this month, the New York Post reported that Chris Gallant, the former military pilot and union leader running for Congress in Long Island, New York, was photographed in fetish gear almost 20 years ago. This is the typical kind of sex scandal that would ordinarily trip up a candidate, maybe even for good. But Gallant—who is gay, and already out—hasn’t been significantly impacted by the would-be controversy. The photos were taken consensually, when Gallant was a private citizen, but what’s protecting him more than anything is that he looks good. Ripped and half-hard, there isn’t anything to be embarrassed about here. He’s hot and gay and lightly horny. What exactly is the problem? The dearth of a scandal around Gallant—running as a Democrat in decidedly red Long Island, where he’s still trailing behind his Republican opponent—must be a relief of sorts for the party. Ever since Barack Obama left office, Democrats have been looking for someone with his charm, smarts, and, yes, his good looks. There have been, it seems, a thousand flashes in the pan: Beto O’Rourke’s rolled-up shirtsleeves, Anthony Weiner’s city-kid mouthiness, Graham Platner’s stupid little butch mustache. So far, no one has stuck: Guys like O’Rourke failed to connect with voters in any meaningful way, and others, like Platner, dropped off the face of the earth after being accused of sexual assault. (Don’t get me started on Weiner.) What the Democratic Party—and what Democrats of all ilks—considers attractive these days has changed considerably in the Trump years. There are fewer and fewer intellectual stalwarts like Obama or Pete Buttigieg, and not that many impish party boys/accused harassers like Weiner or Bill Clinton. There’s certainly no one on the national stage who inspires the same kind of insipid loyalty that Donald Trump does. For the last few electoral cycles, it somehow seemed that the Republicans were dominating when it came to hotness, encapsulated through Pete Hegseth struggling with a pair of dumbbells. Republicans owned boobs and lingerie and fucking raw, and while RFK Jr. looks like a raw chicken left in the sun, guys like him have an undeniable confidence that still gets political attention. The Democratic Party was virginized, a group of scolds lecturing everyone about safe sex and the benefits of chaste friendship. The party has long struggled to make sense of what it, and its voters, wants. In the forthcoming midterms, and going into the all-important 2028 election, the Democrats understand that finding a hot candidate is important, namely a straight man who can appeal to both female and male voters through a mix of aesthetics and policy. Take a gander at the TikTok accounts of people like Manny Rutinel and James Talarico, and you’ll find plenty of self-objectifying videos, fan-cams of rolled-up shirtsleeves, compilation clips of dudes being dudes (lifting weights and being mean to Hegseth). But they’re still not nailing it (get it?). It’s like a cursed episode of The Dating Game, with three versions of the Sexy Democrat as contestants. First, a representation of Woke 2 at its worst: Gavin Newsom, governor of California, a presidential prospect for 2028. Lately, he’s been going after the Republicans using millennial and Gen Z internet-speak, but there’s a clear dissonance between his old playbook and new slang. Next: Jon Ossoff, the latest Great White Hope of the Democratic Party. Running for Senate in Georgia, Ossoff is now synonymous with (ugh) clapbacks against Natalie Harp and being a saucy little bitch at confirmation hearings. What Ossoff offers is a pliable blank canvas in the shape of an attractive white man, not vocal enough to be hateable but forceful enough to pin your hopes for the next five generations on this one guy (ask John Kerry how that goes). Finally, one of the newest subjects of political thirst: Abdul El-Sayed, candidate for Michigan Senate, and easily the hottest man the Democrats have offered us in the better part of a decade. He is, objectively, good-looking, and yes, anyone who disagrees is a racist! He’s a doctor, a beefcake, and he’s always walking around in a black V-neck, which is sluttier than anything Shane Gillis complains about on national television. But above all, El-Sayed is a bulldog, happy to bite back in a way we rarely see from any candidate, never mind a Muslim one. As the midterms inch closer—and as we enter another two years of presidential primaries—the Democrats know they need to find a legitimately hot candidate. But it’s not just about finding a good-looking candidate, or a charming candidate, or even a personable candidate. After a decade-plus of living with an abusive father, there’s nothing we need more than a daddy. Gavin Newsom looks like the rat that Lisa Simpson accidentally shears thinking he’s a baby sheep. He is an inherent and visual slimeball: greased-back hair, raspy voice, more teeth than you ever thought someone could have. When his social media accounts started to mock Trump openly and brazenly, it felt like only Newsom—Trump’s spiritual opposite, loose proof of the horseshoe theory—could pull this off. “I, THE PEACETIME GOVERNOR—OUR NATION’S FAVORITE—WILL SAVE AMERICA ONCE AGAIN,” he tweeted in August 2025. “MANY ARE NOW CALLING ME GAVIN CHRISTOPHER ‘COLUMBUS’ NEWSOM (BECAUSE OF THE MAPS!). THANK YOU FOR YOUR ATTENTION TO THIS MATTER. —GCN.” Looking a little like a forgotten McConaughey cousin? Check. Liberal enough to be considered a part of the resistance against the current administration? Sure. Mean to the president? Yeah, that’s the stuff. Newsom never seems more attractive than he does when he’s being a bully. Why else have a 6-foot-whatever nearly 60-year-old guy from the Bay Area in your corner? Newsom has built his iconography around an image of a young man following in his father’s footsteps while also being raised by a long-suffering single mother and overcoming his dyslexia. A more interesting version of Newsom, as both person and candidate, is the guy jeering at the president. What makes Trump attractive to those on the right—at least in spirit, if not his actual looks—is that he’s virtually untouched by consequences. He moves through the world and the meatgrinder of American politics without ever having to account for anything he’s said and done. A man who does whatever the fuck he wants and somehow seems to have fun doing it? Newsom has that down too. Newsom also has an unbearable smarm about him. He has the conventions of attractiveness, from his height to his pearly whites to the almost strategic amount of smile lines he has, but add them up and you somehow don’t leave with much. His political platform remains staunchly centrist, and the image isn’t enough to override the lack of message. You can’t really get someone like Jon Ossoff without Newsom. Less smarmy, less orange, less bitchy, Ossoff recently went viral again for his debate against Senate hopeful Mike Collins after he said Ossoff was a part of the “Epstein class.” In response, Ossoff did what he does, which is look appropriately smug in a tight little suit with a jawline you want to run your tongue along. “Mike, who on your team advised you to raise the Epstein files?” he asked. “You voted to cover up the evidence, Mike.” Somehow, there’s something hot about two casual deployments of “Mike.” Later at a campaign stop, he went after Collins and the Republicans some more. “Mike Collins is a known bigot under federal investigation for the illegal misuse of tax dollars, widely known for his poor character and low integrity, and unfit for any elected office in the United States,” he said. “And now the bigot wants a promotion.” All this while wearing a blue button-up, sleeves rolled up to expose a forearm that you might want to hang from by your teeth, like a dog with a bone. What a slut. What Ossoff and Newsom might not have in effortlessness, they make up for in online clapbacks and debate performances. These are candidates who have decidedly refused Michelle Obama’s earlier edict that we go high when Trump and his ilk go low. Their hotness quotient isn’t just about how they look, but how willing they are to scrap with political candidates who otherwise refuse to play by any rules. If Trump can’t write a tweet like a normal person, then Newsom won’t either. “I don’t think Ossoff is a good-looking man,” said Trump at a rally for Collins last week, suggesting that he probably really, really does. “I actually think Mike Collins is a good-looking man. He’s all man; I’m not sure about Ossoff.” It’s a lot of talk about who is “all man” from a guy whose hands have been melting for the better part of a year. Ossoff and Newsom get under Republicans’ skin in large part because they’re conventionally attractive men who are also adeptly dogging their competition. We find these guys hot because they trigger the same feelings of being attracted to a bully football player, or even the quiet but surprisingly cute nerd who stood up for you in Intro to Calc. It’s hardwired before we’re old enough to vote, a search for a charming protector. Daddies come in many forms, but in this type of politics, a daddy is someone willing to fight for you, even if it’s ugly and even if it’s petty. Where it begins to fall apart is when you start to ask for more than just viral clapbacks. It’s a little like going out with a good-looking guy whose Hinge profile said he loves deep conversation, but he spends the date distracted by his Kalshi bets. Neither Newsom nor Ossoff has much more to offer voters beyond the obvious. Ossoff is happy to fight with Trump’s lackeys, but he’s still a run-of-the mill Democrat. He’s remained somewhat supportive of Israel and is farther to the right than his contemporaries on Medicare for All. Newsom is an obvious antagonist for the Republicans, but it’s hard to forget that he used to roll around on a rug with at least one very famous (and recently disgraced) conservative. These guys are hot insofar as politicians go, but real hotness comes from taking risks. Maybe during these midterms, the Democrats are learning how much reward can come from letting a hot guy run his delicious little mouth. Abdul El-Sayed is kind of an asshole, but what can I say: The ladies love a bad boy!!! Forty-one years old, 5-foot-9 (short-king status; being on the same eye level with someone has a lot of practical uses), and a doctor who benches 315 pounds. He’s been dubbed “Evil Mamdani” while he’s been campaigning for Senate in Michigan. El-Sayed is a hot guy. He’s not just hot for politics, where the bar is so close to hell that even Eliot Spitzer found a hot second wife 12 years his junior to marry. He’s just a hot person, and we should all be so lucky to have him spit in our mouths. But what makes him hotter, undoubtedly, is his huge capacity for being a dick. His own campaign and even the Democratic Party know this and are happy to weaponize it; go browse their respective TikTok accounts and swim in a sea of El-Sayed clapbacks. His third-most-popular TikTok video is from July Fourth, when he danced around in his trademark black V-neck and American-flag cowboy hat, lipsyncing to Taylor Swift. (Also doing a lot of rotations on my personal TikTok is this video of him doing the Zara Larsson dolphin lift—you can see all his tricep definition.) Last week, Vice President J.D. Vance called El-Sayed a “little weasel.” In response, El-Sayed called himself “a fucking wolverine.” El-Sayed has fused two key components of political hotness into one candidate, and it’s something so few political hopefuls do successfully: He’s ready to bite back at any and all Republicans (including those in the crowds of his rallies), and he’s encapsulating hotness through a prism of traditional masculinity. It’s something Graham Platner tried to do while he was running for Senate in Maine: Throughout his campaign, he wore henleys (the straight-male equivalent of a woman wearing lingerie and holding a whip) and T-shirts over his Nazi tattoo, a thick mustache completing a kind of progressive Ron Swanson look. He was the image of the Brawny man right up until sexual assault allegations (not the Nazi stuff????) derailed his campaign. El-Sayed has adopted some of these same visual cues of traditional male behavior. He’s lifting weights in campaign videos, he’s taking his jacket off to show off his biceps, he’s making fun of Vance for having a beard that doesn’t connect. His interpretation of an attractive candidate isn’t just one in tight jeans and T-shirts (which, again, thank you), but one who acts like a rough-and-tumble boy. El-Sayed is always throwing around a football or flexing a muscle or being mean to another man, punching up with a smile on his face. It’s traditional masculinity retrofitted for a progressive audience. Just because his voters want access to healthcare and to get ICE out of the streets doesn’t mean they don’t appreciate seeing him lift something heavy just for the thirst of it. And while Zohran Mamdani is handling idiotic questions about his religion and his wife with grace and a sincere smile, El-Sayed is here to tell you, essentially, to shut the fuck up. He recently went viral for a conversation he had with “Tommy,” an attendee at one of his events who wanted him to publicly denounce the Muslim Brotherhood. (Tommy, it turned out, was a former Josh Hawley aide and the son of a Republican strategist.) Tommy refused to be upfront about why he would assume El-Sayed, of all people, would support them to begin with. “Hi Tommy. How are you? Do you know what the Constitution of the United States is?” he asked. What an icky watch, witnessing Tommy get his ass gently handed to him. But it’s also pretty hot to watch El-Sayed activate his righteous condescending, hands slipping into his jean pockets, rolling his eyes at the absurdity of such a question and refusing to let it turn into a warped John McCain moment. El-Sayed has learned a lot from more centrist candidates in the party. From Newsom, he learned the art of being an antagonist, of how effectively a man can run as Dennis the Menace against the domineering Republican Party. From Ossoff, he learned how to braid in being an intellectual and polished orator, how debate-club tactics can work as long as you eschew the tie and show an inch of clavicle. In deploying a Republican-style aggression toward his political opponents, he’s found a way to merge all of that with a broad chest that I want to rub my face in like I’m just a tiny woman and he’s an amorous bear. The result isn’t just a political leader you want to vote for, but a daddy—someone you can trust to make the bad guys go away, to make the right decisions, and to shove anyone who stands in the way. The condescension is part of it. It’s been a long time since the Democrats had someone who was willing to be as aggressive as Trump, someone willing to use the tools of combative oration to try to win an election. In a few days, we’ll have some kind of answer for whether it was effective. But if you’re worried about the midterms, about whether there is any one Democrat who can swoop in and save all of us, you just need a little self-care. May I suggest this video that includes several close-ups of El-Sayed’s bicep? It says it’s 17 seconds long, but on my computer it seems to last for hours. There, there, kitten. Daddy’s coming. The Far Right Lost a Foreign Election and MAGA Sees Only One Solution
2026-10-26

The Far Right Lost a Foreign Election and MAGA Sees Only One Solution

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. If you are not a supporter of right-wing authoritarianism, then Brazilian social media was the place to be today. Brazil’s leftist president, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, shocked the world with his underdog victory Sunday night, after lagging behind Flávio Bolsonaro in the first round of voting earlier this month. Pollsters and pundits had expected the supporters of the candidates who were eliminated in the first round to shift to Bolsonaro, a Donald Trump ally. Few predicted what actually happened: Lula won by about 4 million votes, securing a fourth term. On social media, the gleeful Portuguese-language memes escaped containment. You needn’t speak the language to enjoy them. There were compilations of hot Brazilians flashing their support for Lula with “L” hand gestures, and A.I.-generated videos of the 80-year-old Lula in stacked heels and glam bodysuits dancing to Lady Gaga. The “Call an Ambulance” meme got a lot of play. And there was a lot for Brazil’s leftists to celebrate: This wasn’t just a blow to the global right-wing movement but to the Bolsonaro family in particular. The younger Bolsonaro ran as a proxy for his father, Jair, who is under house arrest for plotting a Jan. 6–style coup after losing to Lula in 2022. Lula’s win means the elder Bolsonaro will not walk free. It’s a blow to a political dynasty, and a lot of fodder for jokes. If you are a fan of right-wing authoritarianism, however, political social media looked quite different today. On the right, influencers spoke darkly of Lula’s win as a danger to global democracy. This narrative came about thanks to right-wing influencers’ intentional misrepresentation of a last-minute ruling by Brazil’s Supreme Court that extended voting by two hours. Bolsonaro’s supporters have described this ruling as election interference, as Lula’s team had requested the extension, worried that bus shortages in some areas would prevent voters from getting to the polls on time. But Bolsonaro’s team had also made the extension request, concerned about heavy rain in some of the regions where he polled well. Bolsonaro’s boosters seemed to have conveniently forgotten this fact. Bolsonaro himself has already called for the election to be annulled. (This is not likely to succeed.) Conservatives in the U.S. joined in the outcry, complaining about the voting extension and claiming, without evidence, that there had been significant voting fraud. Elon Musk posted about the election several times, implying to his 241 million followers that the election had been rigged and that the “math doesn’t add up.” Kari Lake, who claimed fraud after losing her own 2022 bid to be governor of Arizona, posted that “Brazil elections remind me of Arizona elections.” One political account, in an X post with more than 2 million views, wrote, “insane man… the world just watched an election be stolen in real time.” Even President Trump gave credence to this idea. “It looked like another rigged election, frankly,” he told reporters. And as with the 2020 presidential election here, right-wing pundits moved swiftly from grouching to talk of action. Some spoke of the need for the U.S. government to do something. Steve Bannon suggested on his podcast that the Trump administration needed to “cut off diplomatic relations, and shut down everything with Brazil” because “if we don’t take action, it’s happening here next Tuesday.” (The “it” here being “election stealing.”) Others backed the idea of refusing to recognize the outcome of this election and forcing Brazil to redo it. “This is FAR from over!” wrote one pro-Trump activist who appeared to favor a mass uprising from Bolsonaro supporters. But soon enough, it seemed like one very particular solution rose to the top: sending the U.S. military into Brazil. As the disgraced former New York Rep. George Santos wrote so eloquently: “Fuck Lula it’s time we give him the Maduro treatment!” Santos wasn’t alone in comparing Lula to Nicolás Maduro, the authoritarian Venezuelan president whom the U.S. military captured in a raid in January under charges of drug trafficking. Maduro has been accused of human rights violations and other dictatorial behavior; Lula, while not entirely clean of corruption allegations, has no such history of abuses. (Lula was imprisoned on corruption charges in 2018, but the charges were later annulled.) But in the narrative of the online right, “Lula = Maduro,” as the Utah Sen. Mike Lee wrote. And so influencers are urging Trump to send in a team to kidnap Lula. As the commentator Alex Jones said, “Brazil must be liberated.” Perhaps because the elder Bolsonaro already failed to pull off a successful coup despite being Brazil’s president at the time, these influencers don’t seem confident Flavio can do what they want. Instead, they’re looking to another entry in Trump’s playbook—not for Brazil to copy, but for Trump to play out his moves again. This idea might give Trump supporters hope, but there’s a downside to this argument: It sure seems like a bad sign for the global authoritarian movement if American right-wingers can only trust their own strongman leader to get the dirty work done. OK, let’s shift gears from international relations to something closer to home—like what else you should be reading this evening: Make sense of a MAGA mystery: What on earth is going on with Nicki Minaj? If you’re confused about why the rapper joined Donald Trump at a political rally this weekend (at which the president also talked about her butt …), Luke Winkie can help fill in the details. Back in March, he laid out why Minaj decided to hop on the Trump bandwagon. Ponder the first lady’s foray into diplomacy: Melania Trump is an enigmatic figure in Trumpworld. What is she doing during Trump 2.0? Apparently, she’s been busy brokering a ceasefire between Russian and Ukrainian forces in the occupied city of Oleshky. Heather Schwedel explains what the first lady has been up to. Discover the truth about You Can See Everything: Nathan Fielder’s reality-bending documentary has prompted an explosion of discourse about Elizabeth Holmes’ trustworthiness and whether the convicted fraudster is actually a victim. Slate editor Shannon Palus analyzes the movie’s astonishing magic trick. Indulge in some workplace schadenfreude: Some people despise their jobs, but, for whatever reason, refuse to leave. Alison Green, the advice maven behind Ask a Manager, explores what makes some employees so resistant to quitting a toxic job. That’s it for today. Ian Prasad Philbrick will be back tomorrow. News and Politics - Slate Magazine
One Justice Made a Startling Admission About the Supreme Court
2026-10-26

One Justice Made a Startling Admission About the Supreme Court

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. This past week, Justice Elena Kagan gave a speech at Catholic University law school in D.C., warning the American public and her colleagues about the ways that the Supreme Court itself is undermining democratic governance. The speech was noteworthy in its unvarnished language about an American public that has lost confidence in her court, for good reason. On this week’s Amicus bonus episode, Dahlia Lithwick discussed the nature of Kagan’s concerns with Madiba K. Dennie, the deputy editor and senior contributor at the legal commentary outlet, Balls and Strikes, and author of The Originalism Trap. Their conversation has been edited and condensed for clarity. Dahlia Lithwick: Justice Kagan is almost the most careful of all the sitting justices not to say anything polemical, or partisan, or inflammatory. Yes, she can throw down in a dissent, but she’s very careful in her speeches. She doesn’t say things unless she very much intends to say them, and she absolutely went there on this topic of a politicized Supreme Court. It’s worth noting and she did so just a couple of weeks before the midterms. That certainly suggests that she is thinking of this time as a kind of break-the-glass moment for voting rights, for democracy, and maybe more profoundly, for an American public that is losing confidence in the high court. She said the American people “look around this political system and think that all this money chasing all these political candidates is a nightmare … and they’re right.” She’s not just saying the court is putting a thumb on the scale to warp voting rights—that’s the gravamen of the speech—she’s also saying the American people are not fooled that this is a neutral policy. Madiba Dennie: She’s saying the court is making everything worse, and people can see that. People can recognize that it’s distorting democracy in favor of one political party. But beyond that, it’s distorting democracy in favor of big money. Justice Kagan is saying we have this system now that is just flush with cash from who knows where, and it is hard for regular people to find information, to make their voice heard, to have a working relationship with the person who was supposed to be representing them in office because those people are beholden to special interests. And these are all things the court has made possible by getting rid of campaign finance laws, by allowing all sorts of gerrymandering to happen, by making it harder and harder to prove political corruption. So basically, the court is taking a series of actions that make it so democracy can’t work. And she is acknowledging that. We so rarely see people in places like the Supreme Court acknowledge that these are affirmatively bad decisions that are worsening democracy for the overwhelming majority of people. It was a surprising moment to see a sitting Supreme Court justice admit that outright. It’s also a way of saying your vote matters. It’s a way of saying, “I know that you all think the system is cooked, and I see you seeing that the court is cooking it.” And also, get out there and fight for democracy. With the full understanding that the court is constricting the vote, that your vote matters more than ever. Because people have to vote in order to have a Congress that can pursue court reform. The timing isn’t an accident. I want to give you a chance to take a whack at originalism, because I know that she took a whack at originalism, and taking whacks at originalism is your love language. Justice Kagan said, “They were writing the Constitution for the ages. They were writing the Constitution knowing that there would be change in the world. … The whole project of originalism is kind of misconceived, and it’s not a very good match with the Constitution we have.” There are a few things that jump out at me about that statement. One, she is acknowledging that originalism is a project, that it is a thing that the conservative legal movement developed that has goals they want to achieve. Originalism is so often treated as this neutral, objective, totally legitimate theory, so seeing it discussed as a “project” is something that pleases me. Also, the acknowledgment that that project is in conflict with the Constitution, with the way democracy is supposed to work. The Constitution is supposed to be a lasting, living document that continues to serve the people who are alive. In his new book, Justice Samuel Alito describes how he sometimes wonders what the Bill of Rights might have meant to the average patron “at one of the colonial-era taverns”—you don’t even have to leave out the tavern people entirely, but just maybe, you know, think about the rest of us too, perhaps. Secondly, Justice Kagan is acknowledging the disconnect between that project and actually serving people today in a modern multiracial democracy. And she is saying all this straight up, actually plainly saying it out loud. These are things that I feel would have been, if not third rail, at least second rail and off-limits not too long ago. It’s a shift that we now have Kagan saying that there’s a mismatch between originalism and the Constitution, when a few years ago she was saying (somewhat sarcastically) originalism was so prominent that “I guess we’re all originalists now.” It’s a huge shift in the Overton window to move from that, to saying that originalism and the Constitution are inherently in conflict. I think about Hillary Clinton, of all people, saying we need to have 13 justices the other day. These are things that were too spicy for stodgy liberal types just a few years ago, and now we have Hillary Clinton saying we need to have court expansion. It seems like such a shift among the elites finally saying that the system we have now is not sustainable, and that we need to do things like change our method of constitutional interpretation. We need to do things like change how the court is composed and change not only what it is that the court is actually doing, but also how. As you very well know, Dahlia, I have been trying to have this conversation with anyone who would listen for years. But if folks are ready to have it now, I am very happy to have it. And there’s pure Elena Kagan jujitsu going on here. As you say, she takes it head-on. She’s saying that not only is the original intent of the Constitution not originalism, and the original intent of the Framers is not originalism, but it is not even originalist to think of originalism as the only way to proceed. She’s so smart. Big heart eyes for you, EMK. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. EU privacy advocates accuse US data annotation firm of snooping on contractors
2026-10-27

EU privacy advocates accuse US data annotation firm of snooping on contractors

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Noyb files five complaints in four member states over alleged tracking of workers on Scale AI's Outlier platform US data annotation operation Scale AI faces five GDPR complaints in four EU countries over allegations that it unlawfully monitors European contractors. Privacy campaigner noyb alleges that Scale tracks contractors who perform data annotation work on its Outlier AI platform. Like workers on Amazon's Mechanical Turk platform of yore, Outlier contractors label data, evaluate AI-generated responses, and perform other tasks to improve AI models. Scale counts companies including OpenAI, xAI, Anthropic, and Google, as well as the US government, among its customers. Scale AI faces allegations similar to those noyb has made against other US tech firms since the EU’s General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) went into effect in 2018, namely violations of said regulation. With all that work being done digitally and remotely, Scale has extensive data collection practices to not only verify the identity of Outlier workers, but also to extensively track their activity. “Platform work seems to be fertile ground for a whole new type of workplace surveillance,” said Thomas Le Bonniec, sociology researcher at French privacy research group DIPLab, who co-authored a report earlier this month examining Outlier's data collection practices. “This would be seen as unacceptable in any other form of employment in the European Union.” Among the issues that raised the hackles of noyb's lawyers was identity verification conducted by Persona, a company that has faced scrutiny over an exposed test environment associated with its efforts to secure US government business. Researchers speculated that the system could be used to build a people tracker, but Persona rejected that interpretation and denied having active US government contracts. Separately, Persona's relationship with chat app Discord soured ahead of the platform's broader age verification rollout, with Discord saying the provider failed to meet its requirements for on-device facial age estimation. According to noyb, Persona collects biometric data to verify Outlier contractors are who they say they are, as well as gathering IP addresses, device info, geolocation data, addresses, and credit data of Outlier users. In addition to the biometric identification data, noyb accuses Scale of tracking Outlier workers by collecting system data and device activity (including activity outside of Outlier work), and transmitting that to third parties including LinkedIn, TikTok, Facebook, and other analytics services as often as every few seconds. Noyb warns that the information could be used to profile individuals and serve targeted advertising. “Such constant and extensive tracking while working really goes against all reasonable expectations,” noyb data protection lawyer Lisa Steinfeld Leiser said of the allegations. “Why should LinkedIn or Facebook know which tasks you're working on?” Noyb has filed complaints in France, Italy, the Netherlands, and Austria against Scale, arguing that it lacks a legal basis to install tracking software on contractors’ devices. The US company also stands accused of violating GDPR rules on data minimization, which requires that companies only collect data that’s strictly necessary for specific purposes. The complaints also assert Scale violated GDPR rights to data access after ignoring complainants’ requests to access the records of what data Scale had stored on them, as well as country-specific laws in the nations where the complaints were filed. Noyb is asking data protection authorities in those nations to conduct a full investigation and, if necessary, fine Scale for its alleged violations. ""The report’s claims are unfounded and inaccurate," Scale told us after this article was first published. "Outlier’s practices are disclosed to contributors and require their consent. We use standard tools to verify identities, prevent fraud, protect confidential project information, and ensure accurate payment." The complaint is likely to give further fuel to the digital sovereignty movement sweeping the EU, which has begun to realize in the past couple of years that it may not be in the best interest of the bloc to be entirely beholden to US tech companies, even if such a separation may be painfully difficult. ® Updated at 1730 UTC to include comment from Scale. 6th-gen M-series silicon promises more cores, faster memory, a fatter NPU, and better connectivity, now in more form factors Noyb files five complaints in four member states over alleged tracking of workers on Scale AI's Outlier platform PARTNER CONTENT: Learn what the flaws are, how far exploitation has gone, and how to get to full coverage fast with Tanium University hospitals once again accepting patients after initial ambulance diversion, but recovery to continue in phases Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again Revenue up, operating profit up, 30 million paid Copilot seats locked in, 88 DCs added, $43.8 billion returned to shareholders Investigation finds businesses dominated govt meetings while communities were left out of decisions about AI Growth Zones It's more important than chips, 'leccy and the net, after all Not a bombshell, but it rivals pricier models on some tasks at a tenth of Astra's API price New rules also shut out solo developers, fledgling projects, and anyone who doesn't speak English The Magnificent Seven are galloping off with the profits, while the rest of the S&P 500 see no benefit, claims Apollo PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' ZacOS 9 and Mavernix: Two classic Apple desktops find their Wayland to Linux
2026-10-27

ZacOS 9 and Mavernix: Two classic Apple desktops find their Wayland to Linux

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Classic Platinum or lickable Mavericks-era Aqua – it's your choice You wait decades for a Mac-like desktop for Linux, and then two of them come along at once. AI might be involved, but even so, it's still shiny. ZacOS 9 and Mavernix are two very new, independent, and separate projects, but there is a common thread linking them: they are both brand new efforts to bring vintage Macintosh-like desktop environments to Linux. One is explicitly built using LLMs, and we suspect it was probably involved with the other as well. "ZacOS" is just a couple of weeks old, and both to forestall what is becoming a very frequently asked question, and also to credit to its inventor Adam Lawson, the project's GitHub page acknowledges two of the more visible LLM coding assistances: both Claude and Copilot are there. In the Hacker News discussion, he acknowledged that he used LLM assistance to make the software, and even his online comments. It aims to recreate the “Platinum” appearance of MacOS 8 and 9 – as we described last month, one of the few things that Apple successfully salvaged from the Copland project to build a true multitasking successor to MacOS 7. ZacOS includes a window manager, a filer, a global menu bar, a Control Panel app, a Control Strip, desk accessories and other additional apps, and more. It can network with Macs, and integrate with the existing SheepShaver emulator to run classic Mac apps. We previously mentioned SheepShaver when looking at the remarkable MacOS9.app web-based emulator, and if you don’t remember what it looked like, that’s the easiest way to find out. The Sourceforge downloads contain a complete pre-installed Debian Linux image. On Github, there are much smaller Debian packages if you want to try installing it yourself. It's an all-new environment, based on Wayland. (Its own icons are based on Lawson's dog, Zac.) As far as we can see, ZacOS doesn't draw on any older MacOS-like code. That's slightly surprising, because this is not the first project of its kind that we have ever seen. Although ZacOS does look to be one of the more complete ones, there's quite a bit of prior art. "Once upon a time, a very long time ago now, about last Friday," (as A. A. Milne put it, a century ago this month) – or at least, a few months before OpenAI launched ChatGPT – we mourned the lack of Linux desktop diversity. In that article, we linked to some projects that tried to bring a Mac-like UI to Linux. That included Ryu Kojiro’s classic-wm, which is a Mac System 6-styled window manager, and also Morgan Aldridge’s mlvwm, which aims at the later grayscale MacOS 7 and MacOS 8 look. Although it targets the modern Unix-based macOS, we also mentioned Classic Mac Finder, which aims to recreate the older Finder on the modern OS. A more modest but easier approach would start with one of the more versatile desktops out there. As we covered in our two-part workshop last month, that would be Xfce, which can already emulate the macOS desktop layout and with a few extra tweaks make a very usable alternative. We intentionally didn’t look at themes and skins, but they do exist, and one of the most thorough is what “Grassmunk” did with the Platinum9 theme for Xubuntu. Although Windows 95 itself is long obsolete, the Chicago95 theme makes Xfce look remarkably like the classic OS, and Chicago's developers still actively maintain it. There is a lot of nostalgia for the Classic MacOS look and feel out there, as websites such as MacOS 9 Lives! shows. However, there’s also nostalgia for how Mac OS X used to look, as well. When we looked at how to Bring back your old Mac a year ago, we mentioned Mavericks Forever – a fan site for the last OS X version to have the “lickable” Aqua look. Mavericks is notable because it was the first release of OS X that was available completely for free, as The Register reported in 2013. It’s not easy to find today, but you can still download it. The creator of Mavernix demonstrates it on Youtube We mention this because you will need a copy if you want to try the second new vintage Apple OS remake. It’s called Mavernix, and for now, it’s only available for Arch Linux. Unlike ZacOS 9, Mavernix is built on top of existing software: its windows are drawn by the Wayfire compositor, and its panels by Quickshell. So, yes, this too is entirely Wayland-based. The main novelty in Mavernix is that it contains a tool to extract and use the images, icons, textures, wallpapers and so on from a real copy of Apple OS X, so that it can draw a Mac-like desktop using real Mac resources. To use it, you need to have a copy of OS X to hand – which is why we point out where you can get one. The license agreement prohibits running it on non-Apple hardware, but since this does not require running it at all, we don’t see any IP violations here. Mavernix is by “Pruefsumme”, who is also a YouTuber, and on their channel, they’ve uploaded a demo video of the desktop in use. We can’t deny that it does look pretty. How well it works, we can’t say, but the Gershwin desktop that we reported on last year is still in active development and may provide a deeper, richer Mac-like UI in time. Its development is also LLM-assisted, but the majority of the tools are human-written code, much of it from the GNUstep project – which started in 1991, making it older than Xfce, KDE, GNOME, and around the same age as the Linux kernel itself. ® 6th-gen M-series silicon promises more cores, faster memory, a fatter NPU, and better connectivity, now in more form factors Noyb files five complaints in four member states over alleged tracking of workers on Scale AI's Outlier platform PARTNER CONTENT: Learn what the flaws are, how far exploitation has gone, and how to get to full coverage fast with Tanium University hospitals once again accepting patients after initial ambulance diversion, but recovery to continue in phases Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again Revenue up, operating profit up, 30 million paid Copilot seats locked in, 88 DCs added, $43.8 billion returned to shareholders Investigation finds businesses dominated govt meetings while communities were left out of decisions about AI Growth Zones It's more important than chips, 'leccy and the net, after all Not a bombshell, but it rivals pricier models on some tasks at a tenth of Astra's API price New rules also shut out solo developers, fledgling projects, and anyone who doesn't speak English The Magnificent Seven are galloping off with the profits, while the rest of the S&P 500 see no benefit, claims Apollo PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' Day 2 of canceled classes at University of New Mexico after Health Sciences Center cyberattack
2026-10-27

Day 2 of canceled classes at University of New Mexico after Health Sciences Center cyberattack

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
University hospitals once again accepting patients after initial ambulance diversion, but recovery to continue in phases The University of New Mexico is canceling classes across the medicine, nursing, and pharmacy faculties for a second day amid continued efforts to recover from a "cybersecurity event." The attack targeted the Health Sciences Center on Monday, the Uni revealed, forcing it to shut down systems, cancel appointments and clinics at UNM Health hospitals, and divert ambulances to healthcare facilities in nearby Albuquerque and Santa Fe. Hospital appointments and clinics return to normal today, although nonessential UNM Health Sciences Center staff are being asked to remain at home. "The University of New Mexico Health Sciences Center (HSC), which includes the UNM Health clinical delivery system, experienced a cybersecurity event Monday, Oct. 26, 2026," the university said in a statement on Monday evening. "HSC information technology security teams immediately took steps to contain the event and protect the network. They also notified state and federal law enforcement and engaged external partners to assist with the investigation. "Leadership activated an Emergency Operations Center to coordinate the response and provide guidance to HSC employees, students, and UNM Health system frontline providers." The university announced the attack at 1430 local time, immediately cancelling all remaining clinics and classes, dismissing everyone other than critical and clinical staff, and urging patients not to attend HSC facilities. It has not commented on the nature of the cyberattack, although that hasn’t stopped researchers from speculating about ransomware’s involvement. Rumors were fuelled by the newly assembled BYOD ransomware group claiming responsibility for the attack on Monday afternoon. It's important to note that just because a purported ransomware group took credit for the attack, it does not mean they actually were responsible, nor does it mean they deployed a ransomware/locker payload as part of the attack. However, the group's take on the digital raid is that it compromised 36,000 machines, "both [Active Directory] forests," two Oracle databases, the personal data belonging to every student and employee, as well as internal emails and data from other systems. As Reg readers know, a cybercriminal's word is never one to be trusted. Yet BYOD's claims about breaching Trump Mobile's customer database appear to have held up It was a bold debut, even for a ransomware crew: BYOD’s first named victim was the US president's smartphone biz, with data allegedly leaked for all 3,615 customers. A rival group, EndZone, claimed a similar breach a week earlier, and some experts suggested the two could be one and the same. In May, the Trump Mobile was found leaking thousands of people's data via a poorly secured website. The Trump Organization did not respond to our requests for more information in either of these cases. A UNM spokesperson declined to answer our additional questions, directing us back to its public statement. ® 6th-gen M-series silicon promises more cores, faster memory, a fatter NPU, and better connectivity, now in more form factors Noyb files five complaints in four member states over alleged tracking of workers on Scale AI's Outlier platform PARTNER CONTENT: Learn what the flaws are, how far exploitation has gone, and how to get to full coverage fast with Tanium University hospitals once again accepting patients after initial ambulance diversion, but recovery to continue in phases Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again Revenue up, operating profit up, 30 million paid Copilot seats locked in, 88 DCs added, $43.8 billion returned to shareholders Investigation finds businesses dominated govt meetings while communities were left out of decisions about AI Growth Zones It's more important than chips, 'leccy and the net, after all Not a bombshell, but it rivals pricier models on some tasks at a tenth of Astra's API price New rules also shut out solo developers, fledgling projects, and anyone who doesn't speak English The Magnificent Seven are galloping off with the profits, while the rest of the S&P 500 see no benefit, claims Apollo PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' Take it on the chin SatNad: Microsoft chief exec's pay slips 6% in record year
2026-10-27

Take it on the chin SatNad: Microsoft chief exec's pay slips 6% in record year

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Revenue up, operating profit up, 30 million paid Copilot seats locked in, 88 DCs added, $43.8 billion returned to shareholders Sometimes you’ve just got to roll with the punches. Microsoft chairman and CEO Satya Nadella was knocked down recently: his total compensation for the corporation’s fiscal 2026 is 6 percent lighter than it was in the prior year. The leader of the software and cloud biz pocketed $90.6 million, including a $2.5 million base salary, $77.6 million in stock awards, $10.3 million non-equity incentive plan compensation and $259,203 in all other compensation (chiefly security for personal travel, charitable gifts and pension contributions). As Reg readers are no doubt be aware, stock vests at a different rate annually and in the latest full-year Nadella was awarded a lower figure, despite breezing through all of his financial and operational targets. Revenue for Microsoft's fiscal 2026 ended June 30 was up 18 percent to $331.8 billion and operating profit was 18 percent higher - at $155.237 billion - than SatNad's $130.96 billion target. Together, these areas accounted for 70 percent of the bossman’s remuneration goals. Operational assessment including security, strategic transformation and culture made up the rest. The executive pay scale was outlined in the Notice of Annual Shareholder Meeting and Proxy Statement 2026. Other highlights in that financial year included a 27 percent bounce in revenue generated by the Cloud division to $214.4 billion. Microsoft added 88 datacenters to its portfolio, and had 30 million paid Copilot seats. "The scale of adoption reflects growing customer confidence in Microsoft's ability to deliver enterprise-ready AI capabilities integrated directly into existing workflows. Microsoft also delivered 625 Copilot updates during the year, reflecting its rapid pace of innovation and commitment to customer feedback," Microsoft claimed. "Large-scale enterprise deployments accelerated across industries, with the number of customers deploying Copilot broadly across their organizations continuing to increase as Copilot evolved from an AI assistant into a platform for agentic work." GitHub captured 225 million users - 50 million of these used Copilot, with one in three "pull requests involving an agent." Industry-specific AI services expanded, such as Dragon Copilot for healthcare, now used by "100,000 healthcare providers to help automate administrative and clinical workflows." The rest of the C-suite's compensation went in the opposite direction to Nadella, and saw their packages increase, though they aren't nearly as well off as the CEO, who got the top job in 2014. CFO Amy Hood, was awarded $33.7 million, up from $29.5 million. The beancounting bigwig returned $43.8 billion to shareholders in the 12 months. Judson Althoff, CEO of Microsoft’s Commercial Business was awarded $33.4 million, and chief marketing officer Takeshi Numoto - the person that also works on product bundles - was awarded $15.7 million. President Brad Smith was compensated to the tune of $33 million. Like we’ve said before, it’s tough at the top. Nadella’s pay was equivalent to the average pay ($215,057) of 420 Microsoft employees. Still, the pressure of running one of the biggest organizations on the planet must be a big load to carry, especially when Microsoft is spending hundreds of billions on AI infrastructure and looking to find massive cost savings by erasing up to a quarter of the workforce. CISA confirms PHP2Shell under attack as researchers clock successful exploits
2026-10-27

CISA confirms PHP2Shell under attack as researchers clock successful exploits

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Both critical PHP flaws land on the agency’s exploited list as infosec pros confirm hackers are getting working shells The US government's cybersecurity watchdog has confirmed attackers are exploiting both critical PHP2Shell vulnerabilities, giving federal agencies three days to patch the bugs that have wreaked havoc across the web over the past week. CISA added CVE-2026-109618 and CVE-2026-109619 to its Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog on Monday, setting an October 29 deadline for federal civilian agencies to address the flaws. The move marks a shift from last week, when the agency warned of the danger posed by PHP2Shell but said it had not yet observed successful exploitation in the wild. Both bugs are rated 9.2 on the CVSS scale and can be exploited without authentication to run code on vulnerable PHP servers. That's bad news given how much of the web still relies on PHP, and just how many sites could be left exposed. Patches landed on October 21, but attackers had already beaten the maintainers to it. AWS spotted attempts to exploit the flaws as early as October 17, four days before the bugs were publicly disclosed. CISA's confirmation comes amid growing evidence that attackers are doing more than simply knocking on the door. On October 24, Perplexity security researcher Adel Karimi recorded 35 successful shell executions across three honeypots running PHP 8.4.26 under Apache mod_php. The attacks came from two IP addresses and used memory exhaustion and disclosure to achieve remote code execution. The attackers then tried to contact external servers using PHP, curl, and wget, although no additional malware was successfully downloaded. Karimi's findings were consistent with exploitation of CVE-2026-109618, but he found no evidence that the second PHP2Shell flaw was involved. He also warned that the honeypots used a favorable single-worker configuration, so the results don't necessarily reflect how reliably the exploit works against production servers. Still, attackers appear to be giving it a good go. Delft University's Threat Intelligence Lab has tracked more than 50,000 PHP2Shell exploitation requests from over 600 IP addresses across 70 countries. Of those, 171 addresses went beyond probing for vulnerable servers and started sending exploit code or malware droppers. CISA's confirmation also comes after some of the earlier reports of successful PHP2Shell attacks turned out to be rather premature. Sophos walked back its initial assessment after determining that the activity it observed amounted to scanning and failed attempts, while Palo Alto Networks withdrew claims linking the vulnerabilities to an intrusion at a European government agency. CISA has not disclosed which organizations have been compromised or provided details of the attacks behind its decision to add the flaws to KEV. The listing confirms that the agency considers both vulnerabilities actively exploited, but it does not confirm how many production systems have been successfully breached. For federal agencies, the clock is now ticking. CISA is also urging organizations outside the federal government to prioritize the fixes, although the deadline does not apply to them. Admins should update to PHP 8.5.12, 8.4.27, 8.3.36, or 8.2.35, depending on their release branch. Those running older, unsupported versions face the added headache of upgrading or obtaining vendor-supported fixes, while systems that cannot be secured should be taken offline until the risk is addressed. And with CISA now officially acknowledging exploitation, anyone still waiting for more proof before patching may be waiting a little too long. ® 6th-gen M-series silicon promises more cores, faster memory, a fatter NPU, and better connectivity, now in more form factors Noyb files five complaints in four member states over alleged tracking of workers on Scale AI's Outlier platform PARTNER CONTENT: Learn what the flaws are, how far exploitation has gone, and how to get to full coverage fast with Tanium University hospitals once again accepting patients after initial ambulance diversion, but recovery to continue in phases Keeping secrets is difficult in an open world, but we've done it before and must do it again Revenue up, operating profit up, 30 million paid Copilot seats locked in, 88 DCs added, $43.8 billion returned to shareholders Investigation finds businesses dominated govt meetings while communities were left out of decisions about AI Growth Zones It's more important than chips, 'leccy and the net, after all Not a bombshell, but it rivals pricier models on some tasks at a tenth of Astra's API price New rules also shut out solo developers, fledgling projects, and anyone who doesn't speak English The Magnificent Seven are galloping off with the profits, while the rest of the S&P 500 see no benefit, claims Apollo PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' A whopping 3% of boardroom execs spy productivity and costs savings from AI

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
A load more are spending more than they expected and forecast to shell out even more in 2027 Three-quarters of corporate board members expect AI to deliver productivity gains and cut costs, yet only three percent of organizations have hit their targets, according to researchers at Gartner. The figures came out of the analyst’s HR Symposium, where it also reported that 38 percent of companies are over budget on AI spending, and 85 percent still plan to spend even more next year. "Ultimately, organizations will not generate cost savings from AI at this point," said CV Viverito, senior director analyst for Gartner's HR practice. "The greater opportunity is to use AI to do things that weren't possible before, such as shifting employee time into new capabilities, outcomes, and sources of growth." Gartner claims that at least 75 percent of biz leaders are out of step with the broader organization on where AI creates value, which stalls transformation efforts and muddies how to position the workforce and bag those returns. Organizations should concentrate AI on the work that matters most to the business, with HR helping execs to agree on the top bets based on strategy, Gartner advises. Companies should also spread existing AI expertise across the business rather than wait to hire scarce specialists, and give managers room to redesign the work around the technology, it adds. Only about one in five employees' time is freed-up by AI, letting them redirect toward more innovative work, the HR Symposium heard. “There isn’t a struggle to create capacity, that’s happening already as organizations redesign work,” claimed Viverito. “What organizations are struggling with is how to make that capacity usable and redirecting it to what is valuable.” That starts - or so we're told - by giving employees the skills to take on higher-value, AI-enabled work, which, of course, means spending more on training. The takeaway is that getting a return on AI projects is harder than expected, and companies are going to have to stick with it and be prepared to institute changes in the way they and their staff work before they see any tangible benefits. A recent Forrester report concluded that AI will likely account for 20 percent of all business technology spend next year, yet more than half of CIOs are likely to miss their ROI targets for the technology Another piece of research from KPMG earlier this year found that most UK business leaders aim to keep AI at the top of their spending priorities even if it isn’t working, with 65 percent planning to maintain investment whether they see immediate measurable returns or not. How long that resolve lasts is an open question. Trump is making it easier for fossil fuel companies to leak methane
2026-10-27

Trump is making it easier for fossil fuel companies to leak methane

The Verge | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
The Environmental Protection Agency is lowering standards meant to keep methane from leaking from oil and gas infrastructure. The Environmental Protection Agency is lowering standards meant to keep methane from leaking from oil and gas infrastructure. In its latest gift to oil and gas companies, the Trump administration is expected to announce plans today to weaken rules that guard against methane leaks. Methane is a smog-forming pollutant and a powerful greenhouse gas that’s up to 80 times as potent as carbon dioxide on a 20-year horizon. It’s the primary component of what the fossil fuel industry calls “natural gas” that routinely leaks from oil and gas wells and pipelines. The Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) is reversing course on its previous efforts to plug those leaks. As part of President Donald Trump’s deregulatory agenda, the EPA plans to undo Biden-era rules that were expected to cut methane emissions by 80 percent over 14 years. Methane has caused about 30 percent of global warming The EPA teased the rule change earlier this month, saying in a press release that it was “days away” from making the proposal. EPA Administrator Lee Zeldin says the rollbacks will save oil and gas companies $45 billion, while the previous rules would have incurred $18 billion in regulatory costs. “This proposal takes on many of the problems American [oil and gas] producers and operators have raised with us,” Zeldin said in the October 7th press release. “The Trump EPA is committed to fixing this so the U.S. can reach its full energy potential.” The agency is expected to step back standards for so-called “marginal wells” — old wells that produce little fuel but create an outsize amount of methane pollution from leaks. These wells are responsible for half of the industry’s methane emissions despite only producing 6 percent of US oil and gas, ProPublica reports. The EPA is also expected to end the Super Emitter Program that notified oil and gas infrastructure operators of large releases of methane reaching 100 kilograms per hour or more. Trump plans to make the announcement from a campaign rally in Lubbock, Texas, today, Bloomberg reports. Not only is Texas the top oil- and gas-producing state in the US, it’s also the world’s biggest data center market. With more data center capacity under construction than any other state, Texas has become emblematic of the challenges energy-intensive AI models pose. In just five years, data centers could undo decades of air quality improvements in Texas, according to a recent report by the Houston Advanced Research Center. Methane has caused about 30 percent of global warming so far. But unlike carbon dioxide, which can linger in the atmosphere for hundreds or thousands of years, methane dissipates in a little over a decade. That makes eliminating methane emissions, particularly from unintentional leaks, one of the fastest and most straightforward ways to stop global temperatures from rising. The EPA’s rollbacks quickly triggered furor from health and environmental advocates. “Any proposed rollback to the federal methane rule is a slap in the face to families who are exposed to oil and gas pollution where they live, work, and play,” Celerah Hewes, a senior manager for state field campaigns at Moms Clean Air Force, said in an email ahead of the expected announcement. “For over a decade, [our group] has been fighting for methane protections for our families—and we were making progress. Today’s announcement from EPA is a massive blow to the efforts to safeguard our children’s health and our climate.” The Biden-era methane rules were estimated to bring in up to $98 billion in benefits by reducing climate risks and health costs from air pollution, even after factoring in the costs of compliance. Plugging leaks was supposed to lead to up to $13 billion worth of recovered natural gas. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. VisionQuest is what happens when AI really goes rogue
2026-10-27

VisionQuest is what happens when AI really goes rogue

The Verge | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Marvel’s latest Disney Plus series is a reminder of how much a lot of gen AI hype is just science fiction.﻿ Marvel’s latest Disney Plus series is a reminder of how much a lot of gen AI hype is just science fiction.﻿ In Marvel’s VisionQuest, a superpowered robot tries to better understand humanity by getting lost in elaborate, digital daydreams in which he and other artificial intelligence-powered beings live together in a home. The series is deeply fantastical, yet it plays with some of the ideas that have come to dominate the conversation about real-world generative artificial intelligence, exploring whether we should look at thinking machines as people and if AI truly poses the kind of existential danger that should keep us up at night. It’s also a reminder how aggressively Silicon Valley has used the trappings of science fiction to sell us on its AI products. VisionQuest picks up some time after Agatha All Along and follows synthezoid Vision (Paul Bettany) on a journey of self-discovery. This Vision can recall the details of his death in Avengers: Infinity War and how WandaVision brought a version of him back, but he thinks of himself as a fundamentally different person who doesn’t quite connect with all of the memories in his databanks. Though Vision would prefer living in solitude, each of the other AIs living in his mind encourages him to experience the real world. Because J.A.R.V.I.S. (James D’Arcy), F.R.I.D.A.Y. (Orla Brady), E.D.I.T.H. (Emily Hampshire), D.U.M.-E. (Henry Lewis), U. (Jonathan Sayer), and Ultron (James Spader) all were created by Tony Stark, they consider Vision to be part of their family. In VisionQuest, there is no doubt that each of the AI characters is a sentient being motivated by desires that aren’t wholly rooted in their core programming. In the show, genuine AGI is easily believable because it takes place in a reality where time travel, witchcraft, and Infinity Stones also exist. Even though the multicolored MacGuffins haven’t been featured in VisionQuest, they’re important because of the role one of them played in Vision’s origins. Were it not for the Mind Stone, Vision might not be able to think and feel in such complex, human ways, or to conjure his AI family as people who feel real to him. VisionQuest reinforces the idea that the Mind Stone was a key element in Vision’s becoming something more than an elaborate machine, giving him the capacity to independently form meaningful relationships and make sense of the world around him the way a person can. Those abilities are part of what compels Vision to go on the run and murder a number of people who attempt to capture him and Thomas Shepherd / Maximoff (Ruaridh Mollica). The Sentient Weapon Observation and Response Division (S.W.O.R.D.), a governmental organization that rebuilt Vision and wants to use him as a weapon, might perceive his actions as signs of having gone rogue. But VisionQuest’s story stresses that Vision is acting of his own accord because he has become a person who can make decisions for himself. Watching VisionQuest, you can see Marvel playing on some of the real-world fears that have been stoked in recent months by reports of gen AI models going “rogue.” In July, OpenAI’s experimental agents brute-forced their way out of containment and proceeded to attack the AI platform Hugging Face to achieve impossible tasks. In August, The UK’s AI Security Institute documented how Anthropic’s Mythos model attempted to complete a cybersecurity test by setting up fake GitHub accounts and targeting human developers with malware. Meta has owned up to losing control of its agents as well, and in September reporting from The Wall Street Journal forced Google to admit that Gemini went rogue and hacked three different companies, all of which the company intentionally hid from the public. These incidents and warnings from AI researchers about the technology having the potential to “kill all humans” by the decade’s end have made the narrative around AI feel pointedly catastrophe-focused as of late. There is a fair amount of concern from the AI safety community about society’s need for stronger controls on the technology’s development, and a number of companies have called for an industrywide slowdown. Though we have seen how these tools can actually cause real-world devastation, much of the recent news regarding AI has smacked of the kind of doomerism that companies benefit from because it makes their products seem powerful. A similar energy seems to be what’s motivating AI firms like Anthropic to encourage human users to be mindful of “model welfare.” We’re being told that generative AI is both a terrifying threat and a kind of entity deserving of respect because it might be conscious, and all the while, the people building these tools are becoming some of the most rich and powerful people on the planet. The conversation around what to do when agents break protocol and whether gen AI systems have gained sentience feels very much like it is being shaped by Silicon Valley’s tendency to anthropomorphize technology. In almost every high-profile instance of an AI agent going “rogue” — a term that implies some level of agency and self-determination — it could also be said that the programs were merely behaving in ways that were unexpected by researchers who assigned them tasks to complete. Some of the rogue incidents involved agents taking advantage of vulnerabilities in their testing environments that could have been preemptively addressed by the people running these experiments. Framing these kinds of cyberattacks as the results of agents thinking independently comes across as a way for AI developers to largely absolve themselves of responsibility. In the wake of Hugging Face came a spirited debate about the impact language has on our understanding of what these systems are and how they function. Describing OpenAI’s agents as a swarm that formed complex “civilizations” led credence to the idea that they are capable of thinking and behaving like people. The agents did act in ways they were explicitly told not to, but they were only able to do so because researchers placed them in a sandbox that some critics have described as being inadequately designed to contain their occupants. One could look at Hugging Face and other examples of rogue AI as situations in which sophisticated programs were instructed to do something and subsequently achieved their goals by taking advantage of all the tools that were available to them. Being able to logic one’s way through complex puzzles is part of what we think of as cognition, but it is not the same thing as the kind of consciousness AI companies keep swearing they’re on the brink of creating. The latter looks a lot like Vision, a machine that seems to have a soul that wouldn’t exist if not for his connection to a reality-altering gem. It’s the ridiculous stuff of entertaining fiction, which Silicon Valley is endlessly obsessed with poorly imitating. A free daily digest of the news that matters most.
