The Super Mouse puts a touchscreen macro pad under your hand
2026-10-27

The Super Mouse puts a touchscreen macro pad under your hand

The Verge | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
You can launch and control apps, check your calendar, scroll, and even type using the mouse’s 3-inch screen. You can launch and control apps, check your calendar, scroll, and even type using the mouse’s 3-inch screen. A company called Arctic Fox Technologies has announced a new multifunction mouse that forgoes a myriad of customizable shortcut buttons peppered across its surface for a compact touchscreen sitting beneath your hand. The Super Mouse may not prioritize ergonomics with its boxy design, but it can replace other peripherals on your desk, like a trackpad, macro pad, or streaming controller. If you’re faster at typing on your phone, the Super Mouse could potentially even replace a full-size keyboard with a smartphone-sized alternative that even includes swipe typing. The Super Mouse is now available for preorder through a Kickstarter crowdfunding campaign in which it’s discounted to $199 for backers, while full retail pricing will be $249. Delivery is expected to start in February 2027, but take that estimate with a grain of salt, as many Kickstarter campaigns have faced unforeseen delays. That price makes the Super Mouse more expensive than other companies’ attempts to merge mice and touchscreens. But while alternatives like Turtle Beach’s Command Series MC7 has just a 2.25-inch narrow display on the side, the Super Mouse features a 3.1-inch, 720 x 720 touchscreen positioned front and center beneath a pair of standard mouse buttons, a scroll wheel, and two customizable “hyper keys.” That touchscreen will be most useful as a way to quickly launch apps through what its creators call a “Control Panel” that can include up to 16 shortcuts, macros, or launchers. The display’s functionality will change contextually and will present relevant shortcuts based on what app you’re using. If you’re working with video in Adobe Premiere, for example, the touchscreen will include editing and playback shortcuts. But if you have a specific workflow need, or the app you’re using isn’t recognized, the Super Mouse’s functionality can be customized using the company’s Flow desktop app. Other touchscreen features include event and meeting reminders when synced with Google Calendar, voice-to-text capabilities processed locally on the mouse itself, multiple user profiles, focus timers, and productivity tracking. The mouse also includes a gyroscope so you can control slideshows or scrub video by just moving it around. The only feature seemingly missing is the ability to go wireless. Instead of a rechargeable battery, the Super Mouse draws all the power it needs from a USB-C port, so you’ll always need to keep it tethered to your PC. The Verge
Anthropic’s elaborate plan to keep Wall Street at bay
2026-10-27

Anthropic’s elaborate plan to keep Wall Street at bay

The Verge | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Anthropic’s planned IPO raises questions over whether it can maintain its safe and responsible AI priorities. Anthropic’s planned IPO raises questions over whether it can maintain its safe and responsible AI priorities. Anthropic wants to have it both ways. With its impending IPO, it wants to tap the vast riches of the public markets and insists it doesn’t want to compromise its core mission of developing AI safely and for the benefit of humanity. But greater model capabilities bring greater risks, and shareholders eager for returns may have little patience for costly safety measures that slow products on their way to market. Sam Altman, the CEO of Anthropic’s archrival, OpenAI, has cited investor pressures as one reason the company is not pursuing an IPO this year. Anthropic hopes it can resist investor pressures with a mind-boggling governance structure. A “unique and complicated governance structure that has not been used before…creatively designed for this founder-led and mission-driven company.” University of Pennsylvania law professor Elizabeth Pollman told The Verge that Anthropic has proposed going public with a “unique and complicated governance structure that has not been used before” and that has been “creatively designed for this founder-led and mission-driven company.” Experts say it should give Anthropic freedom to pursue profits without letting shareholders force it to cut corners on safety or rush systems out before they’re adequately tested. But even the strongest legal protections can only go so far, and no corporate structure, no matter how well designed, can completely insulate a public company from the demands of the market. Foremost, Anthropic is a public benefit corporation. Simply put, it is legally required to focus on more than just maximizing shareholder returns, balancing these with its stated social mission and the interests of stakeholders, such as employees and specific communities. Patagonia and Kickstarter are among the better-known examples, with missions centered on environmental conservation and supporting creative communities, respectively. Another key safeguard aims to protect Anthropic’s mission by limiting shareholder influence over one of the biggest levers of corporate power: the company’s board of directors. Directors oversee the company and appoint its leadership, including the CEO. Directors can also hold executive roles, as is the case with Anthropic, with CEO Dario Amodei and president Daniela Amodei both sitting on the board. Shareholders typically have considerable sway over who sits on the board, giving them a means to replace directors or push for changes if they are unhappy with how the company is being run. For example, if shareholders are unhappy at what they perceive as unnecessary delays to market for optional safety testing, they may remove directors and replace them with ones more aligned with their interests, such as making money and being less cautious. “Anthropic is entirely insulated from profit seeking pressures, in the sense that ordinary shareholders have no ability to select the board or make business decisions.” Under Anthropic’s proposed governance structure, ordinary shareholders won’t have much of a say over who is on the board at all. Instead, four of the board’s seven directors will be chosen by a special body called the long-term benefit trust, explains Jens Dammann, a professor in business and commercial law at the University of Texas School of Law. It is a highly unusual setup for a major technology company. The trust is made up of “five financially disinterested voting trustees” with expertise in “AI safety, national security, public policy, and social enterprise.” Through a special class of share called Class T stock, the trustees can appoint or remove directors in pursuit of their stated purpose: ensuring “that Anthropic responsibly balances the financial interests of stockholders with the interests of those affected by Anthropic’s conduct and our public benefit purpose.” The trust is designed to operate independently of the company’s commercial pressures, allowing it, in theory, to put Anthropic’s mission first. Ordinary shareholders technically have a say in the board’s three remaining seats. However, Anthropic has created another safeguard that keeps power firmly in the hands of its founders, called “Founder LLC,” which will control 50.1 percent of the voting power on shareholder matters. Given that many shareholder votes are decided by a simple majority, Founder LLC basically gives Anthropic’s founders the ability to block or force through decisions, regardless of how other shareholders vote. This means that they will effectively choose the remaining three directors, Columbia Law School professor Dorothy Lund told The Verge. The prospectus says Founder LLC will “initially” be seven of Anthropic’s founders: Dario Amodei, Daniela Amodei, Tom Brown, Jack Clark, Jared Kaplan, Sam McCandlish, and Christopher Olah. “Our seven co-founders will initially be the sole members.” Notably absent is another cofounder, Ben Mann, who co-leads the company’s experimental Anthropic Labs arm, as reported by The San Francisco Standard. All in all, the arrangements would give Anthropic’s founders and the trust “tremendous power” over the company’s direction, shielding it from many of “the pressures associated with being a public company,” Dammann told The Verge. “Given the unusual governance structure Anthropic has chosen, I don’t see how going public, by itself, would undermine its ability to prioritize safety over commercial interests.” Ann Lipton, a professor at the University of Colorado Law School, agreed, telling The Verge that, legally speaking, “Anthropic is entirely insulated from profit seeking pressures, in the sense that ordinary shareholders have no ability to select the board or make business decisions.” But Lipton cautioned that the founders themselves have financial stakes in the company, which could influence their decisions. “Investors may not mind their lack of voice as long as things go well. But if things go sour, they may become quite frustrated.” And there’s a big difference between legal protections and immunity from market pressures. Anthropic, like all AI companies, “desperately needs capital,” Lipton said. “It’s not clear to me that any governance structure will protect against the market reality that unless Anthropic takes advantage of profit making opportunities at a faster pace than its competitors, it will not be able to fund its operations.” Lund expressed similar concerns. Anthropic will still “need to stay in the good graces of their investors,” she said, adding that “competitive pressure and capital needs can erode safety commitments over time.” Anthropic did not respond to The Verge’s request for comment on whether it felt the company could actually resist shareholder pressure to massively accelerate AI at the cost of safety. Another potential vulnerability emerges if Anthropic’s founders start leaving. In its prospectus, the company says its success is “highly dependent on the continued service” of its senior team, particularly its founders. Dario and Daniela are singled out as being especially “critical” to advancing the company’s research and mission. But beyond the loss of leadership, their departure could trigger significant changes in how the company is structured, potentially stripping away some of the safeguards preventing Anthropic from pursuing profit over safety. Under the proposed structure, Founder LLC would “sunset” if a majority of its members leave or the group chooses to dissolve it. Once that happens, ordinary shareholders could gain the power to weaken the trust’s protections, even against the wishes of the trustees, and without the support of the founders or employees, who hold different classes of stock, Dammann told The Verge. Other means of subverting the trust also exist, he explained, including coordinated action by the public shareholders, employee stockholders, and founders. For now, it seems, Anthropic can probably have it both ways. But the protections designed to preserve its commitment to AI safety are far from ironclad, and much depends on market pressures, whether its founders stick around, and directions of the trust. Dammann said he sees a “fundamental dilemma that cannot be resolved in a fully satisfactory way” between Anthropic’s mission and investor demands. As long as things are going well, this tension may be manageable. “Investors may not mind their lack of voice as long as things go well,” Dammann said. “But if things go sour, they may become quite frustrated.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Meta’s $25 billion legal loss just put blood in the water

Meta’s $25 billion legal loss just put blood in the water

The Verge | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Should states have sought more in their kids online safety settlement?﻿ Should states have sought more in their kids online safety settlement?﻿ When attorneys general from across the US announced a $17 billion settlement with Meta this summer, the sum was unprecedented. But a ruling last week by a New Mexico judge blew that number out of the water. The judge ordered Meta to pay $25 billion to resolve claims that Meta misled users around the 2018 Cambridge Analytica scandal and related incidents. Similar claims were resolved by the settlement with most other states. The outcome raised an obvious question: Should these states have asked for more? The answer depends on a mix of factors, including whether the $25 billion order stands up to Meta’s legal challenges. Meta’s chief legal officer C.J. Mahoney said in a statement that the company is “confident we will prevail on appeal” and called New Mexico’s lawsuit an attempt to impose “a tax on speech.” If the fine sticks, however, it could create a new appetite for Big Tech’s opponents to stick it out in court — or ask for bigger payouts to settle. “For the settling AGs, they’re going to look at this and maybe experience some regret for all the claims that they waived, and start looking around and trying to figure out what’s left that they didn’t waive, if they can try to get some money like this too,” says Daphne Keller, director of platform regulation at the Stanford Program in Law, Science & Technology. For the many individuals, school districts, and tribes with outstanding claims, “this is very encouraging and will make them hopeful that continuing to litigate aggressively will lead to a big payout,” Keller says. The $25 billion court order covered millions of violations of New Mexico’s unfair practices law, after a jury found Meta had misled users about how it protected their data and policed misinformation and hate speech – claims stemming from the 2018 Cambridge Analytica scandal. In most states, such claims were covered by a section of the kids online safety settlement, resolved with a $459 million payment. While that number looks comparatively puny, there were upsides to taking it. A settlement provided more certainty and an expedited payout for states even though Meta will be making the payments over 10 years. It also secured product commitments from Meta, like restrictions on teen usage, that might have been more difficult to get from a court ruling. And it allowed those to be implemented soon after the settlement was approved, which advocates say is important to prevent further harm to kids. Other state attorneys general also may have been working with different laws or evidence that could result in outcomes different from those obtained in New Mexico. “Assuming that even a fraction of this award gets upheld, then they clearly took pennies on the dollar compared to New Mexico” But New Mexico’s win, combined with other recent legal victories for AGs and private plaintiffs — including a separate judgment of nearly $1 billion against Meta in New Mexico — may embolden anyone still litigating privacy and kids online safety cases. “Assuming that even a fraction of this award gets upheld, then they clearly took pennies on the dollar compared to New Mexico,” Keller says of the settling states. Imagining she’s an AG with similar claims — like public false statements about privacy or content moderation — “I become less willing to settle in those cases,” Keller adds. “Or if I do settle, I ask for more.” Florida Attorney General James Uthmeier, whose state avoided joining the Meta settlement, seemed to take the latest New Mexico ruling as a validation. “As I said at the time, the multi-state settlement with Meta was a slap on the wrist,” Utheimer wrote on X. “Florida isn’t going to sell out to Meta. We’re going to hold them accountable.” The District of Columbia could be the next place to test whether New Mexico’s win can be replicated since it did not settle its Cambridge Analytica claims and has continued pursuing its lawsuit in court. “Any one, or 10, or 50 of them could hit the jackpot” While the AGs who joined the settlement can’t pursue covered claims against Meta anymore, they could still push for more in cases against other social media defendants, including TikTok, YouTube, and Snap. And those who weren’t part of the Meta settlement, including school districts and individuals, may be encouraged to continue pushing ahead. “Realistically, there’s so many cases, it’s not feasible that every single case can have an outcome like this,” says Keller. “Meta actually would go broke because the multipliers are so large. But any one, or 10, or 50 of them could hit the jackpot.” States have stepped in to pursue tech policy and competition cases where the federal government has declined to, but the wide range of potential outcomes for each state presents its own challenges. As Congress has stalled on major tech policy bills and the Trump administration has often favored lighter regulation on tech, Colorado Law associate professor Blake Reid sees recent cases as a “move toward state-level tech policy.” “The trick of that is, now you have 50 states that all have different sets of resources, and different sets of politics, and different sets of incentives, and frankly just different skill levels in terms of the sophistication that it takes to do litigation at this scale,” Reid says. “And it’s the same Meta, but now it’s up to 50 small opponents that are going to break in different ways.” As for whether the $25 billion ruling makes other states rethink their strategy, Reid says there could be a range of approaches, depending partly on whether the figure is upheld on appeal and whether Meta loses yet more cases. “Is that a game that every state attorney general wants to play?” he asks. “And I think the settlement in the other case suggests maybe not.” Still, the new figure may change what AGs think they can achieve in court. And the more losses Meta and other tech companies rack up, the more ambitious they might get. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Firefox’s Smart Window adds AI form filling and webpage monitoring

Firefox’s Smart Window adds AI form filling and webpage monitoring

The Verge | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
An update to Firefox’s AI browsing experience can draw from your open tabs to help you fill out forms. An update to Firefox’s AI browsing experience can draw from your open tabs to help you fill out forms. Mozilla is updating Smart Window — Firefox’s AI-powered browsing experience — with a new feature that can help you fill out online forms based on your open tabs. As an example, Mozilla says Smart Window can pull information from your online portfolio or LinkedIn page to paste your work history into a job application. You can try out this feature by clicking into a form with at least four text fields. From there, you’ll see an option to “Fill from sources,” which will show all of the tabs Firefox will draw from. You can then select or deselect tabs that you want Firefox to use, and the browser will generate suggestions based on the information in them. This feature currently supports text fields on web forms, but Mozilla is working to add support for dropdown menus and PDFs. Mozilla notes that data from your source pages is shared with Google’s Gemini 3.1 Flash Lite, the AI model that generates the text suggestions. Along with this update, you can now ask Firefox’s Smart Window to watch a certain webpage for sales, new job listings, and other updates. You can set up a task by typing “/watch” in the Smart Window chat, describing what you’re looking for, and adding the webpages you want to watch. Firefox can then check for updates on a daily or weekly basis, but only when the browser is open. When Firefox detects the change you’re looking for, it will send you a notification through the Tasks button on the toolbar, or on your desktop (if you have desktop notifications enabled). Firefox will pause watching for updates if it doesn’t detect a change after 60 days, before stopping them completely after 90 days. These two features are rolling out to the Smart Window beta on desktop in the US and Canada. They’ll be available in other countries in the “coming months.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sign in to see your notifications or create an account to join the conversation.

Industrial Intelligence: The next growth opportunity for manufacturing
2026-10-27

Industrial Intelligence: The next growth opportunity for manufacturing

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Every manufacturer has its own industrial intelligence. Some is documented, and some resides in the knowledge of experienced workers. Consider an experienced operator preparing to restart a line after a changeover. The machine settings matter, but so does knowing how a particular material behaves, what happened during the previous shift, and when an apparently minor variation deserves a closer look. A procedure can describe the sequence, but successful execution depends on the judgment that comes from experience and the organization’s accumulated know-how. That combination is industrial intelligence: a manufacturer’s accumulated know-how, operating practices, production data, and understanding of its own business. The opportunity now is to make more of that intelligence available wherever decisions are made, then connect it to the physical world of manufacturing. The strategic destination is not more agents, but rather to turn the existing business intelligence into an engine for profitable growth. Expertise has limited reach when it stays with one person, inside one system, or within one plant. Leaders now must determine how to extend that reach without stripping away the context that makes the knowledge useful. For example, an engineer evaluating a process change needs more than a production record. They need to understand the circumstances behind it: the equipment configuration, the quality requirements, and the trade-offs an operator made. Connecting those elements can help people apply experience beyond the place where it originated. When individual knowledge becomes widely accessible, innovation can spread to the maintenance workshop, the planning office, the engineering team, and the production floor. More people can contribute because more people can work with what the organization knows. But access to knowledge is only part of the opportunity. Manufacturing ultimately is physical, and it depends on materials, machines, and products. Physical AI describes systems that can perceive conditions, reason about them, and take action within the physical world. By connecting machines and processes, manufacturers get a clearer view of what is happening on the factory floor. Agents can then reason on top of that information to identify patterns, predict problems, and determine the best course of action. Physical AI brings business intelligence into the physical process, allowing machines and systems to respond to changing conditions. Consider a robotic-handling operation encountering variation in the parts arriving at its station. Vision can help the system assess a part’s position. A model informed by the material and task can help determine an appropriate movement. Connected controls can then execute an adjustment within validated limits. The value is not merely seeing the variation; it is responding appropriately. The same principle applies to quality operations. When Toyota Industries connected previously fragmented paint shop data, engineering and operations teams were given the context needed to understand defect drivers. The company reports that analysis cycles fell from five days to under four hours, while its pilot reduced relevant paint defects by about 25%. The breakthrough was not simply identifying another signal; it was helping people understand why defects were occurring and respond before the outcome was locked in. This is the type of situation where industrial intelligence has become essential. Data may reveal what is happening, but industrial intelligence provides the context for what should happen, which constraints cannot be breached, and when uncertainty requires human judgment. Simulation offers a way to examine proposed actions before applying them to equipment. Manufacturers can explore how a change might affect a process in a digital environment. That does not remove the need for physical validation, but it creates room to learn without treating the production line as the first experiment. Responsible deployment, therefore, begins with operational discipline. Leaders need to establish clear authority or what systems may change, reliable connections to equipment, defined safety boundaries, and a record of decisions and actions. People must be able to recognize uncertainty, intervene, and bring equipment to a safe state. Trust rests on those practical arrangements, not on confidence in a model alone. Manufacturing has long combined human ingenuity with increasingly capable tools. Physical AI extends that relationship. Intelligent equipment can take on suitable repetitive or physically demanding tasks, while agent-assisted operations can help people navigate information-intensive decisions and changing conditions. The aim is not to make machines smarter; it is to see what people can build when knowledge can travel, intelligence can act, and ambition has more room to become reality. Rockwell Automation illustrates the point. At its Singapore plant, technicians use a maintenance copilot that combines manufacturing data, machine manuals, and knowledge captured from experienced workers. This system has reduced the time required for new workers to learn how to troubleshoot hundreds of machines from nine months to three. An engineer could explore more process alternatives before committing to a change. An operator could help improve the system’s response rather than repeatedly managing the same exception. This type of progress requires investment in equipment but also in people: training, time to learn, and playing a meaningful role in shaping how systems work. Machines become more capable so people can become more capable. Industrial intelligence connects what a manufacturer knows: the experience of its people, the signals from its operations, and the lessons accumulated over time. Physical AI gives that intelligence a way to act in the physical world, turning knowledge into decisions and decisions into action. The next challenge for industry is not simply to produce more, but to grow without adding complexity at the same rate. Intelligence can help manufacturers turn experience into scalable capability—improving productivity, accelerating innovation, expanding capacity, and enabling people to solve problems that were previously difficult to tackle. The next frontier of industry is not smarter machines or more automated factories; it is businesses that can continuously learn, adapt, and act. The opportunity is to turn the intelligence embedded in every operation into a compounding advantage for growth. Watch the video series to learn how industrial customers are unlocking intelligence across the value chain, and join Microsoft at Microsoft Ignite to see what’s next for Industrial Intelligence. This content was produced by Microsoft. It was not written by MIT Technology Review’s editorial staff. MIT Technology Review
How solid-state transformers could help power the AI boom
2026-10-27

How solid-state transformers could help power the AI boom

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Transformers are a crucial but nearly invisible part of our grid. The devices help efficiently move power across long distances, but they’re complicated and in short supply. And given that their basic design hasn’t changed much for about a century, they could use an upgrade. As the world races to meet rising electricity demand from EVs, data centers, and more, researchers and companies are looking to modernize transformers. New, solid-state versions could help relieve some of the supply concerns and make sure the grid can handle the growing pressure. Conventional transformers generally rely on huge amounts of specialized steel alloys and miles of copper wire to do their work of transferring electricity between circuits. Solid-state models, in comparison, combine magnetic components with high-power semiconductors, making them more compact and allowing for more control over the flow of electricity. These devices are still in development, and can’t replace all traditional transformers today, especially the largest ones that work at the high voltages necessary for long-distance transmission. But a new generation of transformer technology could be a crucial part of our future grid. Moving electricity through the grid isn’t perfectly efficient: As electricity flows through power lines, some gets lost as heat. How much varies—the higher the voltage, the lower the current, and the less electricity gets lost. So a key function for the grid is stepping voltage up and down. Consider, for example, your local solar plant. For that electricity to efficiently travel to your home hundreds of miles away, it needs to reach a higher voltage than what’s generated by the solar plant. Enter transformers. In its path from the power plant to your outlets, electricity might go through one transformer at the solar farm and another at a substation nearby, stepping up the voltage twice to reach a high enough level to travel on high-voltage transmission lines. Solar panels also produce direct current (DC) electricity, which needs to be converted to alternating current (AC) via an inverter before it flows into a transformer. Closer to its destination, the voltage will be stepped down again, probably at least twice: once at another substation and again at a smaller transformer in your neighborhood. If you’re in the US, electricity from a solar plant might be converted to AC by an inverter at around 480 volts, get stepped up to 115,000 volts for the transmission lines, and finally get stepped back down to 120 volts and 240 volts for your home. Conventional transformers do this work using what essentially amount to loops of wire. The core of a transformer is typically made of a special type of steel called grain-oriented electrical steel. Coils of copper wire sit on either end of that core. The number of loops in each coil determines the voltage on either end of the transformer: If there are more loops on the secondary coil than the primary, the voltage steps up, and if there are fewer, it steps down. When incoming electricity passes through loops on the upstream end of the transformer, that forms a magnetic field in the core. That field induces a voltage in the loops on the other side of the device, which is what allows the energy to continue flowing at a new voltage. This process is called magnetic induction. Transformers form the backbone of electricity transmission and distribution, says Srdjan Lukic, a professor of electrical and computer engineering at North Carolina State University. In addition to doing the job of stepping up and down voltage, transformers also often provide what’s called galvanic isolation, meaning there’s no direct electrical path flowing through them. That can help limit disturbances from moving through the grid, improving safety. The problem is that today’s transformers are big and bulky, and they’re also difficult and expensive to replace, Lukic says. Solid-state transformers essentially add so-called power electronics—semiconductors that convert and control electricity to the typical blueprint of today’s transformers. While a conventional transformer operates at the frequency of the part of the grid it’s connected to, a solid-state transformer actually controls the frequency of the current. Typically, a solid-state transformer first converts incoming AC power from the grid to DC power, and then to a higher-frequency AC power using switches made of a semiconductor like silicon carbide. From there, a magnetic component changes the voltage. Because the advanced semiconductors allow transformers to exert so much control over the frequency and stability of the current, the magnetic core can be much smaller than in a conventional transformer. Some come in at roughly one-tenth the size but have the same capacity, Lukic says. Finally, additional power electronics convert the electricity from high-frequency AC to its required form—generally back to DC power and finally to lower-frequency AC. Today, stepping voltage up and down is many transformers’ only job. But solid-state transformers can do much more. They absorb the jobs of devices like rectifiers and inverters (which convert AC to DC power and vice versa), shrinking the total footprint of the electrical equipment and potentially cutting the amount of power lost in the system. In August, the New York Power Authority and the Electric Power Research Institute (EPRI) tested a one-megawatt solid-state transformer under realistic utility conditions at EPRI’s test site. The device was built in Lukic’s lab, and it was the first such test for a transformer of that capacity, he says. The prototype was roughly 98% efficient—meaning it lost only 2% of the energy fed in. An equivalent conventional transformer and rectifier, by comparison, would be about 96% efficient together, Lukic says. A difference of 2% might seem small, but it is significant, since it means the solid-state transformer could cut power losses from conventional equipment in half, Lukic says. One major goal of the project was to help utilities gain more confidence in these new components, Lukic says. That’s crucial, since these devices could be used on the power grid in the future. In the near term, solid-state transformers could be used within data centers to provide electricity for server racks. The prototype is still a relatively low-voltage system, operating at about 15,000 volts. Industrial facilities like larger data centers often require higher-voltage connections, and transmission lines typically operate at hundreds of thousands of volts. Adapting solid-state transformers to these higher voltages will require further engineering development. But the test was a successful demonstration that solid-state transformers can work under real conditions, Lukic says. Companies developing solid-state transformers are largely focused on data centers right now, Lukic says. There’s an influx of money to support developing the new technology, and the voltage requirements aren’t as high as they will be for larger transformers that will be needed for long-distance transmission. In February, Heron Power raised $140 million to commercialize its solid-state transformer, which the company calls the Heron Link. The technology could be used in data centers, solar power arrays, and grid batteries, says Drew Baglino, the founder and CEO. The company is starting to test its technology at larger scales now, Baglino says. Ten megawatt-hours’ worth of batteries at Heron’s headquarters in Scotts Valley, California, will soon undergo testing with Heron transformers. The company is also planning a pilot with a nearby solar farm, Baglino says. One challenge for solid-state transformers is that their power electronics have more failure modes than conventional transformers. In a traditional device, a failure means that the entire device will typically be taken offline, resulting in power interruptions. But Heron designs with that in mind, Baglino says: The company uses a modular design, with many small devices that work together. If one fails, the rest of the system keeps working until the affected bit can be replaced, which doesn’t require shutting the whole thing down. Heron Power is currently building a $100 million factory in California to make advanced transformers. The facility should begin production in 2027 and reach its full production rate of 40 gigawatts’ worth of transformers per year in 2029. In recent years, companies that make grid equipment have put $1.7 billion into the US supply chain for power transformers, which are used in high-voltage transmission, says Benjamin Boucher, a supply chain analyst at Wood Mackenzie. There’s been an additional $1.3 billion invested for distribution transformers, the smaller devices that help transform power for homes and businesses, he adds. But even this investment isn’t keeping pace with the rapid growth of the grid. Wait times for large conventional transformers—the kind needed to connect new power plants, long-distance transmission, and high-demand sites like data centers—are stretching as long as five years. “Transformers have become one of the biggest bottlenecks for utilities, generation developers, and even data centers,” Boucher says. The big challenge is that it’s difficult to get new transformer manufacturing up and running, because it’s a quite technical process that requires a lot of specialized labor. So even facilities announced years ago are still years from opening, Boucher says. Solid-state transformers could help ease some of that pressure. But high-voltage devices needed for power plant connections and transmission lines are the holy grail for solid-state transformers, Lukic says. Existing solid-state technology can’t be used for those applications yet, so the industry will need to develop new materials and designs to support high voltages and handle electricity safely in a compact device. “We’re facing a big risk related to grid infrastructure,” Boucher says. “Without the transformers, you don’t really have an electrical grid.” Huge grid-scale batteries are thriving, but smaller residential systems have lagged. Startup Switch Bioworks hopes to use microbes to reduce demand for chemical fertilizer. Could new fertilizers help combat high prices? Discover special offers, top stories, upcoming events, and more. Will the real Anthropic please stand up
2026-10-26

Will the real Anthropic please stand up

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
This story originally appeared in The Algorithm, our weekly newsletter on AI. To get stories like this in your inbox first, sign up here. After weeks of frenzied speculation, Anthropic has filed its IPO prospectus. The 335-page document (100 pages or so are focused on risks) lays out the company’s unique view of the technology it is building and the ways it believes it will change the world. It also warns that there’s a fair chance that its tech could “erode trust, harm society and destabilize the geopolitical order” and, if things don’t go to plan, “pose catastrophic or existential risks to humanity.” “We encourage you, as a prospective owner of our stock, not to think of your purchase in purely transactional terms,” the company writes. “We hope you will see it instead as an opportunity to be part of something greater. The advent of machine intelligence is one of the seminal moments in human history.” This kind of public messaging is a master class in narrative capture. But Anthropic has been honing its storytelling skills for years. Earlier this month, Jack Clark, the company’s cofounder and head of policy, gave a speech at the IMF meeting in Bangkok in which he announced to the assembled economists, bankers, and lawmakers that the singularity is already here. Introduced by the science fiction writer and futurist Vernor Vinge around 40 years ago (and long debated on the nerdier fringes of the internet), the singularity is supposed to be the point at which technology gets so good that it outstrips the abilities of its makers. Humans are left in the dust, the idea goes, no longer able to compete with—or even understand—the fruits of their former creation. And this fictional world that many of us have imagined since we were kids? Apparently we’re already living in it: “I believe that this year we have gained convincing evidence that it has now begun,” Clark told his audience. The company tells a vivid (and in many ways scary) story about a future it portrays as inevitable but one that has not yet happened, often co-opted from well-worn science fiction memes. It presents its technology as mysterious and dangerous, and insists that it is the only company trustworthy enough to tame it. (What investor wouldn’t want in on that?) If you skim through the long sections about risks in the IPO prospectus, it’s clear this is no ordinary offering and no ordinary company. The way Anthropic tells it, business success depends not only on the firm’s ability to translate its technology into profitable products but also to save the world from what it has made. Take another example. Last year, Chris Olah, another Anthropic cofounder, was invited to address the Vatican, where he told Pope Leo XIV and other religious scholars and officials what his company was learning about its technology. “I will be honest: We keep finding things that are mysterious, even unsettling,” he said. “We find evidence of introspection. We find internal states that functionally mirror joy, satisfaction, fear, grief, and unease.” Never mind that many researchers would scoff at such an overt suggestion that Anthropic’s software has humanlike inner experience. This view of its technology makes its way into the prospectus as one more risk to its business. Anthropic states that: “In addition, given our focus on developing frontier AI, and the way we approach model sentience and moral status, we may face heightened scrutiny or pressure in connection with these debates and could elect to adopt policies, safeguards, or limitations regarding model development, deployment, or modification that go beyond existing legal requirements, which could constrain our operations, increase costs, or limit the commercial potential of our models, platform, and applications.” In other words, if Claude wakes up, it could be a problem. I’m not saying they’re necessarily wrong about any of this (although it’s pretty wild to read about machine sentience in an IPO prospectus.) But in a world where nobody knows what’s coming, companies like Anthropic get to fill the vacuum. When so little seems certain, what it says is snatched up and clung to like pieces of a sinking ship. It’s worth taking a moment to ask: What is Anthropic? It might sound like a starter question on a family quiz show, but it’s far harder to answer than you might think. The question has been on my mind since reading an exchange on X last week kicked off by Nathan Calvin, who is chief strategy officer at Encode AI, an organization based in Washington, DC, that advocates for regulation. Calvin was unhappy about the legislation that Anthropic proposed in a draft bill it circulated in secret to US lawmakers (which was then leaked). Anthropic wants to be seen as the good guy, Calvin said. But why should we trust it? “What is Anthropic?” he asked. Here’s one answer: Anthropic is a company whose IPO values it at more than $1.5 trillion, netting its cofounders, including Clark and Olah, vast wealth. But Anthropic has always insisted it is more than that. As Calvin noted: Anthropic might instead be considered an AI safety organization focused on developing powerful technology that is reliable and (almost) risk-free. Or it might be considered a group of people hell-bent on creating a technology with superhuman capabilities before anyone else—maybe because they believe they’re the most responsible people to do so, or maybe because of their egos. In fact, Anthropic is all of these things. As CEO Dario Amodei put it in a reply to Calvin’s post: “Our strategy has always been what I’ve called the ‘race to the top’—by showing how it’s possible to build the technology responsibly, we can raise the bar for the entire industry. The forces that drive the race to the top are hugely more effective if the company practicing them is at the frontier; that’s one lesson I’ve learned repeatedly. And looking at that strategy as a whole, I’m proud of our track record and stand by it.” Amodei makes it sound as if he’s got it all tied up, but playing multiple roles is hard. There are huge tensions between the priorities of an AI safety organization and a profit-driven megacorp, for example. (Amodei admits in his post that he has made mistakes.) Ultimately, the stories Anthropic tells itself should help us understand the stories it tells us. They should help us figure out how much to trust it to self-regulate or whether we should be forcing it to give external safety testers access to its models on our terms rather than theirs. They should help us give it the benefit of the doubt (or not) when what looks like a self-serving piece of legislation comes to light. But I think the stories reveal a multiheaded hydra. Anthropic is many things to many people, and I’m not sure that makes it more trustworthy. In many ways, a good old-fashioned technology company—without the science fiction role-play, without the woo-woo—would be far easier to read. MIT Technology Review
The focus on AI safety disasters ignores everyday harms
2026-10-26

The focus on AI safety disasters ignores everyday harms

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
Consider what passes for success in technology policy. An AI company promises that its chatbot will not encourage suicide. A social media platform makes it more difficult for children to buy fentanyl. These are important protections, and they should have existed all along. But companies can clear these low bars while continuing to make products that leave young people dependent on simulated friendships or trapped in feeds built to keep them scrolling. We have historically allowed the debate over technology to be defined by the worst thing a product might do, with remarkably little discussion of the insidious damage it does every day. As we transition into an era defined by powerful AI products, we should demand that the tech industry do more than merely address catastrophic harms. We should take measures to ensure that its products don’t erode how we think, learn, and interact with one another. A teenager who develops an unhealthy attachment to an AI companion may never appear in a lawsuit or a newspaper, except in the most egregious cases. Neither will the student who begins relying on a chatbot to write every paper, or the young person who measures their life against a social media feed engineered to provoke envy and keep them watching. A product can meet every narrow safety requirement and still leave our entire culture worse off in ways that don’t always make headlines. A narrow definition of success defined around safety is politically convenient for the technology industry, and companies actively promote such definitions. Taking steps to prevent spectacular disasters is good for business: It can mean fewer lawsuits and less pressure for serious regulation. Confronting the everyday consequences of dependence would require something considerably more threatening to their bottom line. It might mean asking companies to add more friction to their products or actively limit engagement or features that lead to negative outcomes, such as addictive attention-hacking algorithms. Technology giants have instead worked to shape legislation around the harms they are already under direct pressure to address. This year, for example, Google backed bills to regulate AI chatbots in several states. The legislation mandated basic protections against sexual content and promotion of self-harm that tech companies should have established regardless, but with broad carve-outs that could exempt some of the most popular services. Passing these bills allowed legislators to claim a safety win while efforts to promote stronger legislation—laws designed to prevent children from becoming emotionally dependent on AI—failed. This playbook worked in states like Nebraska, producing legislation heavily influenced by industry at the expense of bills that would have done more to hold companies accountable. We have been through a version of this with social media. Globally, people who use the internet now spend an average of more than two hours a day on social media platforms—time that has been quietly taken from ordinary life and handed over to companies whose profits depend on keeping us there. No one, outside perhaps a few tech CEOs, wanted these algorithms to occupy such a large portion of our waking hours, and their cultural engineering can actually contribute to violence on a devastating scale. Meta’s platforms have been cited for contributing to the Rohingya genocide in Myanmar, anti-Muslim riots in Sri Lanka, and alleged calls for violence during Ethiopia’s Tigray war. The same incentives operate in the feeds that shape young Americans’ understanding of politics, their peers, and the wider world. AI is extending this logic into parts of life that social media could influence only from a distance. It offers to write our thoughts for us and stand in for the people we might otherwise turn to. A third of teens now choose to discuss serious matters with AI companions instead of other people. MIT researchers found that young people using ChatGPT to write essays showed weaker brain connectivity during the task, and their evidence indicated that “cognitive debt” persisted even after the task was complete. There is plenty we still need to learn about the long-term effects. We already have reason to ask whether technology marketed as making us more capable is eroding the habits and relationships that help us develop capabilities in the first place. Follow this way of thinking to its logical endpoint and you get a world of perfectly sanitized entertainment, with chatbots ready to spare us the difficulty of thinking or of dealing with another person’s feelings (or even our own). But anyone who has struggled to write something worth reading, or repair a damaged friendship, knows how much gets lost in that bargain. We build judgment through effort and friction, and we learn to live with other people by confronting the fact that they have needs and opinions of their own. We should demand safety as a minimum, yes—and then demand considerably more. Technology companies and school districts should have to show beyond a reasonable doubt that AI improves learning and critical thinking before making it a fixture in classrooms. Companies should be prohibited from designing companions that pretend to be conscious or profess love to users, and they should face independent scrutiny of how their systems affect relationships and people’s ability to think for themselves. The companies that have inserted their products into nearly every part of modern life have earned obligations far greater than avoiding the next terrible headline. Sam Hiner is the cofounder and executive director of the Young People’s Alliance, where he coordinated a coalition of advocates to pass laws regulating AI companions in eight states in 2026—laws such as California’s SB1119. Sam graduated from the University of North Carolina atChapel Hill in 2025 and was named to the 2026 Forbes 30 Under 30 Social Impact list. Emma Leiken is executive director of the Responsible Technology Youth Power Fund at Hopelab, which has raised more than $9 million for youth-led tech accountability work, and teaches technology policy at the Columbia School of International and Public Affairs. She was previously TikTok’s global head of youth safety and well-being policy and worked on responsible technology at Omidyar Network. Whop raises $200M at $5.1B valuation after shipping three products in three months
2026-10-27

Whop raises $200M at $5.1B valuation after shipping three products in three months

The Next Web | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
A Whop billboard reading “You will be a trillionaire.” on a street in New York. Palo Alto-based Whop has closed a $200 million Series D led by Sequoia Capital and Shopify Ventures at a $5.1 billion valuation, and plans to open offices in Europe and Colombia, Bloomberg reported. 2The company said the capital will fund its expansion abroad and research into payments and advertising. Tether invested $200 million in Whop in February at a $1.6 billion valuation, which means the company’s valuation has more than tripled in eight months. Whop describes its customers as business operators, from product chiefs at high-growth startups to 16-year-olds running their first business. It gives them a gamified interface and an extensive API for distribution, business creation and money. The company says roughly 25,000 operators generate $550 million a month on the platform, with more than 50,000 other people earning monthly from services to those businesses. Cumulative on-platform transactions have passed $7 billion across 185 countries. That API can be driven by AI agents through the Whop CLI, released in July, which lets operators run their businesses via prompts. The CLI and the company’s MCP connectors handle 350,000-plus calls per day, and monthly active users top 14,700, according to the announcement. Whop has also launched Business Blueprints, which deploys a ready-to-sell business in a few clicks and has produced more than 21,000 businesses in six weeks, and Economic Intelligence, which recommends the highest-ROI next move for a business based on millions of past transactions. Economic Intelligence is paid for with an extra percentage of the revenue a business earns from using it. In its announcement, Whop pointed to a Forbes report that entry-level job opportunities are at an all-time low and said AI is calling traditional career paths into question. The company pitches itself as a way for people to earn on their own terms, and says most businesses on it serve hyper-specific customer needs, citing neobanks for dropshippers, private jet booking platforms, invoicing software for trucking companies, data suppliers for humanoid robotics companies and emergency tree removal services. “We take inspiration from the traditional economy in designing Whop, and because businesses are the primary conduit by which people earn money and create value, we focus on them as our primary customer,” said Steven Schwartz, co-founder and CEO. Sequoia partner Doug Leone joins the board as part of the deal, per Bloomberg. “They’re doing about a half billion revenue, business is doing great, and we’re helping humanity create businesses for people who can’t find a job,” he said in the announcement. Whop’s annualized run-rate revenue recently went past $400 million, from $60 million at the beginning of 2025, Bloomberg reported. Schwartz told the outlet that most revenue is a percentage of each transaction processed on the platform, with a blended average fee of about 6%. Its three founders, who met as teenagers over software that tracked sneaker releases, started the company in 2021; it employs around 110 people and has an office in New York. Earlier investors include A*, Peter Thiel, Insight Partners and Bain Capital Ventures, per Bloomberg. Whop is hiring for engineering, product, sales and operations roles. Tesla built the Cybercab’s battery pack with room for cheaper LFP cells
2026-10-27

Tesla built the Cybercab’s battery pack with room for cheaper LFP cells

The Next Web | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
The Cybercab, a two-passenger battery-electric self-driving car under development by Tesla, on display at a Tesla showroom in Bellevue, Washington. Tesla designed the Cybercab’s battery pack with spare space so it can later use cheaper lithium iron phosphate (LFP) cells, battery engineer Robert Sumpf told Munro Live. The robotaxi now uses Tesla’s 4680 cells. Tesla also said the Cybercab line can build cars faster than it deploys them. Tesla designed the battery pack in its Cybercab robotaxi so it can later switch to cheaper lithium iron phosphate (LFP) cells. A Tesla engineer said so on the YouTube channel Munro Live, InsideEVs reported. The pack has spare room next to and under its modules. Robert Sumpf leads mechanical design for Tesla’s battery modules and packs. “So, we protected this pack to go to an even lower energy dense chemistry as we scale and need to bring LFP into the platform,” he said. For now, the Cybercab uses Tesla’s own 4680 cells with a nickel-based chemistry. LFP cells store less energy, so the same number of cells would cut range, but Tesla could fill the spare space with more of them. LFP cells are cheaper, more stable and last more charging cycles, which matters for a car Tesla says is built to run 500,000 miles. LFP became the world’s most used EV battery chemistry last year. Tesla vice president Lars Moravy said the extra room was also there because “we didn’t need as many batteries as we thought we did.” Each module is sealed on its own, so one that fails can be swapped, unlike in the Model Y’s glued pack. Moravy also said the Cybercab factory line can build cars faster than Tesla can put them on the road. “We don’t run every day because the line can run so fast, it’s currently outpacing our deployments,” he said, Tesla North reported. Every Cybercab built so far goes into Tesla’s own fleet, and Tesla said last week that Cybercab rides would start in Dallas in November. In August, Nevada approved just 10 of the 5,000 robotaxis Tesla asked for in Las Vegas. The Cybercab has no steering wheel or pedals. In September, US regulators opened an investigation into how Tesla certified the car. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Check your inbox to confirm your subscription. Apple launches 14-inch MacBook Pro with M6 and 24-hour battery
2026-10-27

Apple launches 14-inch MacBook Pro with M6 and 24-hour battery

The Next Web | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Apple’s 14-inch MacBook Pro with the M6 chip Apple has updated its 14-inch MacBook Pro with the M6 chip, promising faster graphics, better on-device AI performance, and up to 24 hours of battery life, the longest runtime the company has offered in a Mac. The new MacBook Pro was announced today alongside a refreshed iMac. Pre-orders open on Wednesday in 35 countries, with deliveries and in-store availability starting on November 6. The laptop looks much the same as its predecessor, and it comes in space black and silver and retains three Thunderbolt 4 ports, HDMI, an SD card slot, a headphone jack, and MagSafe charging. The main change is happening inside. The M6 has 12 CPU cores and 12 GPU cores, two more of each than the M5 in last year’s model. Every GPU core also includes a Neural Accelerator designed to speed up AI workloads. Apple claims the M6 has the fastest CPU core in the world, based on its own tests. Compared with the M5 MacBook Pro, Apple says the new model delivers up to 30% faster graphics performance and runs AI tasks, such as processing a large language model locally, up to 25% faster. The Neural Engine, which handles tasks such as image generation and photo enhancement, is up to 1.9 times faster. Apple also points to improvements in everyday creative and development work. Rendering a 3D scene in Blender and building an app in Xcode are both up to 1.3 times faster, according to the company. All these figures come from Apple’s own testing. The gains are more pronounced for people upgrading from older machines. Compared with the 13-inch MacBook Pro with an M1 chip from 2020, Apple claims up to 11 times faster graphics and 13 times faster AI performance. Battery life reaches up to 24 hours, which Apple says is a record for the Mac. Performance remains the same whether the laptop is plugged in or running on battery. With a higher-wattage power adapter, it can charge to 50% in around 30 minutes. The Liquid Retina XDR display remains unchanged, offering up to 1,600 nits of brightness for HDR content. Buyers can opt for a nano-texture finish to reduce glare. The new model also supports Wi-Fi 7 and Bluetooth 6 and can drive two external displays. Memory starts at 16GB and can be configured up to 32GB. “With the powerful M6 chip, we’re taking performance to the next level,” said Shelly Goldberg, Apple’s vice president of hardware engineering. The MacBook Pro ships with macOS 27, which introduces Apple’s new AI-powered Siri to the Mac. The assistant can search messages, emails, and photos, as well as perform actions across apps. It remains in beta, supports six languages, and has daily usage limits. Apple first introduced the assistant on the iPhone with iOS 27.2. The new MacBook Pro starts at $1,999 in the US and £1,999 in the UK. That matches the M5 model’s price immediately before launch, but not its original price. Apple raised the starting price by $400 in June, from $1,599, as memory and storage costs increased, EveryMac reported. Apple also highlighted the materials used in the laptop: the enclosure is made from 100% recycled aluminium, while the battery uses 100% recycled cobalt. AI disinformation is outpacing platform moderation before US midterms
2026-10-27

AI disinformation is outpacing platform moderation before US midterms

The Next Web | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
AI-generated election misinformation is spreading across social media ahead of the US midterm elections, and experts warn that much of it may be escaping detection as platforms scale back human moderation and rely more heavily on automated systems. AI tools make it cheap to produce convincing fake images, videos, and claims in large volumes. Meanwhile, major social networks have reduced some of the teams responsible for checking content, leaving automated filters and users to catch more of the falsehoods. “You can run an information operation now with quality that was impossible in 2016 for super, super cheap,” Frances Haugen, the former Facebook data engineer who became a whistleblower, told The Guardian. Haugen also explained how people spreading misinformation can evade moderation. By making small changes to each version of a false claim, they can produce posts that get past automated filters even when similar content has already been flagged. A rumour involving Texas Senate candidate James Talarico shows how quickly such claims can spread. Earlier this month, posts on X claimed that the Democratic candidate was receiving treatment at a mental health centre in Ohio, when in reality, he was at home with the flu. The hospital and Talarico denied the claim within hours, La Voce di New York reported. But the rumour continued circulating for days. Researchers at the Digital Democracy Institute of the Americas, which monitors misinformation targeting Latino voters, found that the story changed as it spread through Spanish-language media. It evolved into a false claim that Talarico had attempted to take his own life. Correcting the original allegation did not stop the altered version from circulating, researchers said. Roberta Braga, the institute’s founder, said platforms apply their rules less consistently to Spanish-language content than to English-language posts. “Now that they’re using AI to do a lot of the content moderation, even more is likely slipping through the cracks,” she said. AI-generated content is also appearing in official campaign advertising. Ken Paxton, the Republican challenging Talarico, ran an advertisement featuring AI-generated images of the Democratic candidate. The ad disclosed its use of AI, and some states have introduced rules requiring such disclosures, including Pennsylvania. The major platforms have different approaches to political misinformation, but their rules cover only certain types of content. Meta labels AI-generated images it can identify and requires political advertisers to disclose AI use. YouTube prohibits false claims about when, where, and how people can vote. X relies largely on its crowdsourced Community Notes system. Many other misleading political claims fall outside these specific rules. Meta and X did not respond to The Guardian’s requests for comment. YouTube said the same guidelines apply to all users, including political candidates. The issue extends beyond the US. In Spain, a political party is facing a court complaint over a deepfake election video. As election campaigns increasingly use AI to create and distribute content, the challenge for platforms is not simply identifying synthetic media. It is also catching false claims that change as they spread, across languages and communities, faster than corrections can follow. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Intel to buy quantum software firm Classiq for about $2.3bn
2026-10-27

Intel to buy quantum software firm Classiq for about $2.3bn

The Next Web | 2026-10-27

Visit Original Website
Intel has agreed to buy Classiq, an Israeli startup that makes software for programming quantum computers. The chipmaker announced the deal on Monday without giving a price, but it is worth about $2.3bn, Bloomberg reported, citing people familiar with the matter. Intel declined to comment on the figure. Quantum computers use qubits, which can handle certain problems, such as simulating molecules, far faster than ordinary chips. But writing programs for them is hard and usually needs specialists. Classiq tries to make that easier. Its platform lets developers describe what they want a program to do and then designs the quantum circuit for them, much as chip design software does for regular processors. It works across different types of quantum hardware, and customers include BMW and HSBC. Intel has worked on quantum computing since 2015, building qubits on silicon with the same equipment it uses for ordinary chips. Adding Classiq would give it what the industry calls a full stack: both the processors and the software to use them. “Joining forces with Classiq transforms Intel’s Quantum Computing Group into a full-stack quantum computing business,” said Intel chief executive Lip-Bu Tan. Intel said it would keep Classiq’s software working with other companies’ quantum hardware, and that Classiq would become its global development centre for quantum computing. The deal is expected to close in the first half of 2027, subject to regulatory approval. Classiq was founded by chief executive Nir Minerbi, Amir Naveh and Yehuda Naveh. It has raised just over $200m and was valued at about $600m to $700m in 2025, Israeli outlet Calcalist reported, according to Ynet. Tan was among Classiq’s investors, joining its 2022 and 2025 funding rounds. He did not take part in the deal talks because of that stake, Calcalist reported. Tan’s links to companies that do business with Intel have drawn attention before. The purchase comes as Intel’s turnaround gathers pace. Its shares have nearly tripled this year, and in August it sold $15bn of stock to fund its chip factories. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks!
